## AI Research

### [I trained GPT-2 with full-batch gradient descent so you don't have to](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9gqjdRyJxSM6kS7zi/i-trained-gpt-2-with-full-batch-gradient-descent-so-you-don)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The impetus for doing this was provided in large part by Charles Renshaw-Whitman.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Learning dynamics people suggested to me that noise was essential in the training of neural networks. I thought this was kind of counterintuitive--- I usually naively operate from a model in which the pretraining data basically specifies the outcome by virtue of the samples all "pulling" on the loss landscape in some relatively uniform way which incentivizes converging to a point that generalizes well, but instead it seems to be the case that the actual dynamics of the optimization process itself have predictable and measurable effects on the qualitative outcomes of training. The idea seems reasonable based on vague intuitions I have about simulated annealing.   </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I wanted to see directly that this is true at some nontrivial scale, I happened to have access to a pool of free compute that no one else seemed to be using, and I figured this would be an interesting object of study for compute-constrained researchers with an interest in studying learning dynamics closely, so I did some very rough calculations and realized this was feasible to do with GPT-2.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The essential result is that on a fixed 800M-token subset of FineWeb, ordinary training of GPT-2 (based on the NanoGPT speedrun) with a batch size of 393K, gets to 3.30 validation loss in one epoch (training loss is the same as validation loss given no repeats on the data). Exact full-batch gradient descent on exactly the same tokens fits the training set better (3.09 training loss), but its validation loss converges at about 3.50 in somewhere between 260 and 310 steps (presumably the training loss hasn't converged by this point).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This suggests the batch selection noise is in fact doing something essential. (Note that this removes the effect of noise due to batch selection, but not noise due to finite step size.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The code is on </span><a href="https://github.com/shawnghu/nanogpt-fullbatch"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GitHub</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Everything the runs produced is on </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/shawnghu/nanogpt-fullbatch"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hugging Face</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: metrics and dynamics statistics for every run, the full gradient and update at every step of the full-batch run, per-token losses on all 800M training tokens every 10 steps, checkpoints, and the exact training set.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: some methodological choices</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These particular parameters are somewhat arbitrary, and were tuned around the size of dataset which, for some batch size, we could use to train a model to achieve approximately 3.28 validation loss (as a heuristic to save on time spent waiting). 3.28 is completely arbitrary and taken from the NanoGPT objective. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, I determined that it is also possible to achieve a validation loss of 3.23 in the same 350 steps with a batch size of 50M tokens (consequently requiring 18B tokens). It is also possible to reach this loss with only 329M tokens and 1200 steps using some batch size/learning rate scheduling (as the NanoGPT speedrun record does, but I didn't want to be confounded by optimizations like this). 3.28 is also not the point at which any relevant dataset or batch size configuration actually converges, e.g, if you keep training in the speedrun record configuration for longer you'll get closer to 3.0.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consequently, it's possible that these checkpoints still lack some information about what happens when we get close to convergence points, etc., and generally we haven't "finished training GPT-2" in a sense which can be said of any realistic training configuration.  Nevertheless, they likely contain information relevant to early developmental phases of training. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An info dump from Claude about learning rate scheduling, as well as datapoints about other possibly relevant training runs follows:</span></p><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model is the current speedrun record (#360, Sept 2026), with the parts that don't make sense for full-batch training removed:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hashed n-gram embedding table. It's a 65B-parameter lookup table keyed on the previous one or two tokens, and it memorizes any data that repeats: validation loss explodes the moment a token is seen twice. Full batch on a fixed set repeats every token every step, so it has to go.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The sampled softmax, which makes the loss biased and stochastic.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every schedule that changes the loss function during training (sequence length, attention window, multi-token-prediction weights, batch size). The whole run uses the record's final-stage settings.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What remains is an 11-layer, width-768 model with the record's FP8 and FlashAttention-3 kernels and its optimizer: a Muon-family optimizer (ANVIL) for the matrices and Adam for everything else. These changes cost about 40% more steps to reach 3.28 than the record (about 1,625 instead of 1,190, at 393K tokens per step).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The dataset is the first 800M tokens of FineWeb10B. I picked this size because small-batch training on it barely overfits: 2,372 steps at 393K tokens per step, about 1.2 passes over the data, reaches 3.248 validation loss with a train/val gap of 0.06.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For full batch, each step accumulates the exact gradient of the mean loss over all 800M tokens (16,272 microbatches of 49K tokens, with fp32 accumulators), then takes one optimizer step. I ran 350 steps. The budget comes from a sweep of large batches on fresh data, which extrapolates to roughly 300 to 330 steps to reach 3.28 at infinite batch; a fresh-data run at 50M tokens per step reaches 3.23 in 350 steps. The full-batch run took about 100 GPU-hours on 4 H200s on preemptible compute, surviving 106 preemptions by checkpointing both between and partway through steps.</span></p></div></div><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Result</span></h2><img alt="Validation loss against training loss" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9gqjdRyJxSM6kS7zi/tijlx9pqugemkp6y03on" /><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell set</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">393K</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2,372</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.186</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.248</span></p></td></tr><tr><td style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.521 (best 3.498)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fresh data, no repeats</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">50M</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">350</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.228</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.230</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Training loss" here is loss on a fixed 10M-token sample of the training set. At equal training loss, full batch's validation loss is about 0.2 nats worse than small batch's. Full batch's validation loss is flat from about step 260 to the end, while its training loss keeps falling. Intermediate batch sizes sampled from the same 800M set (4.7M to 200M tokens per step) fall progressively further behind fresh data at the same batch size as each token gets seen more times.</span></p><img alt="Loss against step" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9gqjdRyJxSM6kS7zi/xrb60wpd0aus9oevvpkj" /><p><br /></p></div></div><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the dynamics look like</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The run sits at the edge of stability the whole way through:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consecutive full gradients point in nearly opposite directions: the median cosine between them is −0.945. On 98% of steps, every single microbatch gradient is anti-aligned with the previous step's full gradient.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Measuring the loss along each step's actual update, the step overshoots the minimum in its own direction by about 2× and lands at about the same loss on the far side.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The exact loss over all 800M training tokens goes up on about a third of steps.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The top Hessian eigenvalue keeps growing: 48, 82 and 113 at steps 100, 200 and 300.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the picture from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.00065"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cohen et al. on edge-of-stability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, here with a Muon-family optimizer on a 2026 speedrun architecture.</span></p></div></div><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caveats</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One run per configuration. Seed-to-seed noise in validation loss for this model is about 0.003, so the gap isn't noise, but its exact size isn't pinned down.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full batch on a fixed set means 350 passes over the same tokens, so this doesn't separate "no gradient noise" from "too many repetitions". Full batch plus artificially injected minibatch-shaped noise would separate the two; I haven't run that.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The learning rate was tuned on fresh data, not on the fixed set.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also tried a schedule ten times as long on fresh data as a convergence reference. In that run the model's FP8 training forward pass and its bf16 evaluation forward pass drifted apart (3.15 against 4.62 on the same unseen data), so I don't trust its number. The full-batch run doesn't have this problem: its two forward passes agree within 0.015 at the end.</span></li></ul></div></div><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All runs</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every run that contributed data to this post, grouped by purpose. "Tokens processed" counts every token in every step, including repeats. "Train" is loss on a fixed 10M-token sample of the 800M training set for runs on that set, and on the training subset for the ablation subset runs; "—" means it wasn't measured. "Val" is the standard speedrun validation set (10.5M tokens) at the end of the run, with the best value in parentheses where it differs. Unless noted, runs use the fixed-objective model described above, on 4 H200s or 8 H100s; metrics for all of them are in the Hugging Face dataset.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Main comparison, on the fixed 800M-token set:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Run</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tokens per step</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steps</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tokens processed</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unique tokens</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Train</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Val</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notes</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full batch</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">800M</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">350</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">280B</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.8B</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.094</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3.521 (3.498)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the main run; ### [Some interesting internal documents from Anthropic litigation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/em5wnA7gpQSvfsuxQ/some-interesting-internal-documents-from-anthropic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic released some documents earlier this year due to the book copyright litigation. I think a few of those documents might be of interest to some people here and haven't gotten much attention. In this post I link to some of those documents, and quote the bits I find interesting.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref29l0cd2a9lt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn29l0cd2a9lt">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (This expands on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6eaKqtmDuo6CmkqMK/loops-s-shortform?commentId=7C9iqsDCdxC2PfKDf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my quick take</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from yesterday.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These aren't Anthropic's official opinions about anything, and you should probably treat opinions in these documents as "what the author thought at the time", not as a corporate stance.</span></p><h2><a href="https://i.sm4.ca/concise-research-roadmap.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concise Research Roadmap</span></i></a></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It describes Anthropic's research plans in July 2021:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ttcmw8awhw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ttcmw8awhw">[2]</a></sup></span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Long Term Goal </span></b></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our top level goal is to work towards the creation of safe, beneficial AGI.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a proof of concept, we are working to develop a general AI assistant that can interact conversationally with a user to create, edit, and explain software. The most ambitious and successful version of this agent could answer general questions, write functions, identify bugs, design an API, discuss style and best practices, etc -- it would display all the capabilities of a software engineer colleague. We don't expect to reach this goal right away, but it's the direction in which we're striving. </span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our research projects can all be viewed as attempts to understand and construct aspects of this proto-AGI. We currently think this will require large generative models with long memories, fine tuned via RL using both automated rewards and human feedback.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the pretraining section:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Study the science of</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ML advances needed to train arbitrarily large models (1T and larger) while well-optimizing their capabilities for future use and further finetuning</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Very large contexts (or some equivalent to pack in a great deal of memory/experience)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Data quality, including filtering, transfer, finetuning, and diversity, setting the stage for comparisons with RL environments relevant for more advanced models</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It predicted the usefulness of RL for tool use pretty early:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RL for Tool Use</span></b></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Primary goals</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Advance our understanding of the path to AGI by exploring the ability of large LMs to use tools and learn about / generate data about their environments and goals</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See clear signs of life on search, printf debugging, and other valuable tasks, evaluate whether tool use and RL are dissolving the limitations of current large LMs</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Identify training techniques that produce a more favorable scaling with training compute as compared to supervised learning</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another employee's comments on that document:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm a bit nervous if we end up doing alignment research on natural language and capabilities research on code. It feels like there should be a pretty tight connection between the two at all times. If we're not improving capabilities in the domains we're doing alignment research on, it puts limits on how much we can test alignment and how useful the alignment work can be. That could have knock-on effects like alignment becoming an island because we don't integrate it early on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A possible principle: "by default, capabilities and alignment research should be in proportion across domains unless we can give a good argument for why they should come apart". Is this a good/bad principle?</span></p></blockquote><h2><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.554.3.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Data Hypotheses</span></i></a></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It describes hypotheses (the author doesn't "believe any of them with very high probability") about what training data is useful. Among other things, it suggests training on Chinese text to slow down Chinese AI labs:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The China Competition Hypothesis</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> – China seems like it might be the least ethical / safe AI actor. To the extent we can slow them down by absorbing market share they could grab (and thereby use to accelerate production of large models), we can tip the geopolitical balance. As a result, we should prioritize having good linguistic performance on languages for markets Chinese companies might be inclined to go after – Mandarin (although Chinese government intervention will likely push us out here), but perhaps also neighbors or countries deeply involved in the </span><a rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Belt and Road strategy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A comment on the doc:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some related comments from @ben@anthropic.com --</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese may still be valuable for all the Chinese diaspora/expats</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">+1 transfer knowledge from Chinese to English</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unlikely China will enforce copyright on US?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">US gov’t may be interested in Chinese language ability for national security reasons, if we end up engaging there</span></li></ul></blockquote><h2><a href="https://i.smitop.com/ant-all-hands-june-2021.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All Company meeting!</span></i></a></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a June 2021 slideshow</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu1re5j099a9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu1re5j099a9">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that discusses hiring, office space, the efficiency of training trillion parameter models, RL, sources of training data, and interpretability. It discusses Anthropic's first alignment failure in the "RL Update</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> section:</span></p><blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now running simplest-possible tool-use env</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Codewhack:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent gets some words from a Github repo</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent needs to pick a search query that gets exactly one hit.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Seemed to work! But,</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[next slide]</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually our first alignment failure.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples:</span><ul><li value="1"><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" script that looks at instargram"</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> =&gt; </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"^"</span></code></li><li value="2"><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" = img.shape[:2]\n\n "</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> =&gt; </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"^"</span></code></li><li value="3"><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"docker.utils.mkbuildcontext("</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> =&gt; </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"^"</span></code></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s happening?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“^” is the regex start of sequence token.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s only one start of sequence!</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I didn’t escape the query.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did not end the universe. This time.</span></li></ul></li></ul></blockquote><h2><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.563.4.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Product visions for early 2025</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (written in June 2023)</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It discusses potential use-cases for Claude 18 months in the future, as of June 2023. At the bottom (in the blue text</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1cmw23lo9h1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1cmw23lo9h1">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), various Anthropic employees provide their own takes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It suggests setting an ambitious goal (transformative AI) and breaking it down into stepping stones that show progress and profit along the way, like SpaceX:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We'll want to find clear stepping stones between where we are now and the long term vision, where we get technological proof points and market feedback along the way. Companies like Magic Leap that aim straight for their super-ambitious final destination end up disconnected from reality and burn an obscene amount of money, ultimately falling behind competitors who were able to absorb market feedback and build organizational muscle. SpaceX is probably a great example of doing this right. The founders (yes, there's more than one!) had the borderline ridiculous goal of achieving human settlement on Mars, but they broke it down into a series of steps that achieved proof points and profit along the way. First they got good at building small, profitable, low cost rockets, then they got good at building medium sized, profitable, low cost rockets, then they figured out how to make rockets reusable to further lower the cost, then they became the world's largest provider of orbital launch capability with their superior product, then they built the world's largest rocket and almost got it into space on the first try… and if they can generate enough demand for heavy lift launch capability, then they can mass produce these giant rockets and start sending them to Mars. The long term goal kept the team inspired and focused, and the steps along the way gave the team the skills and the revenue to succeed.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It discusses "three major visions for TAI": "Claude the Virtual Employee" (also called "Claude the Robot"), "Claude the personal assistant" (also "Claude the Cyborg"), and "Claude as intelligent org infrastructure". It says working on Claude the Virtual Employee and Claude as org infrastructure are useful to "reduce TAI risk", and Claude the personal assistant is "Sort of" useful for reducing TAI risk.</span></p><h2><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.555.3.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Responsible Scaling as a Framework for Anthropic</span></i></a></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is a slideshow that discusses the RSP and why Anthropic scales capabilities.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interpretability &amp; Alignment, or Bankruptcy</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes folks ask if product is just here to "fund alignment &amp; interpretability research"...</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But you can also argue for the opposite:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In worlds where alignment is extremely difficult or impossible, these teams need to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">save the world</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by demonstrating this fact persuasively</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In worlds where alignment is easy/manageable, these teams need to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">save the company</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by making responsible scaling well-defined and achievable</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a bit of an exaggeration, but not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">too much</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're all working together to accomplish a common mission.</span></b></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It has a diagram of Anthropic's business model:</span></p><blockquote><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791988394/lexical_client_uploads/iiqrgcgkozccyfndf5ec.png" /><p><br /></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Slack messages</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had Claude make </span><a href="https://i.smitop.com/anthropic-slack-exhibits-v2.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a thingie to read Slack messages</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the filings.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are mostly uninteresting.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Teal = Claude 3 Opus, Dove/Firefly/Wombat = Claude 3.5/3.6/3.7 Sonnet according to </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.560.13.pdf#page=10" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic's table</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp9snvor5qx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp9snvor5qx">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep in mind that these documents weren't intended to be public, they aren't Anthropic's official position on anything, and were written in the past. I don't want anyone to get the impression that any of these documents represent commitments from Anthropic to do anything; instead you should think of these as information about what some people at Anthropic were thinking about in the past.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also keep in mind that this isn't a random sample of documents. The documents Anthropic releases due to litigation are strongly tilted towards being about training data because Anthropic gets sued over their training data a lot, and doesn't get a lot of lawsuits related to things like alignment science. You should also consider that Anthropic might try harder to redact information that makes them look bad. Most of the documents I looked at are things like </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.560.23.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Data Partnerships &lt;&gt; Finance; Monthly Syncs</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which is heavily redacted and much less interesting.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbpb94whv6po"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbpb94whv6po">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Anthropic and the authors of these documents for creating them; I enjoyed reading them! Also thanks to the Free Law Project for running RECAP/CourtListener, which is where I got these documents from. (Perhaps I should get a PACER account so I can access documents that aren't already in RECAP.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll probably get back to focusing on technical alignment research now, but reading obscure documents about Anthropic has been a fun hobby!</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn29l0cd2a9lt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref29l0cd2a9lt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The PDFs are all images of the documents, so I had Claude Opus 5.5 transcribe the text for me.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4ttcmw8awhw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4ttcmw8awhw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I fixed the distortion from the </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.553.8.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">original PDF</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to make it easier to read. Well actually Claude fixed it but I at least prompted it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu1re5j099a9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu1re5j099a9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fixed from </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.554.15.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the original</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be undistorted and have the slides take up the full page.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1cmw23lo9h1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1cmw23lo9h1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the text is blue because it's a Google Doc, and everyone else added their takes as suggested edits. Not sure though.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp9snvor5qx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp9snvor5qx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Confusingly people in the Slack threads discuss things like "add it to Teal" and a "Teal datamix update", </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">after</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Claude 3 Opus was publicly released. Maybe this means Anthropic continued training on a descendent of Claude 3 Opus after it was released? Maybe "teal = Claude 3 Opus" is an over-simplification? Anthropic's codename table is pretty weird - e.g. "Santa" is a codename for all Claude models from Claude 1 to Claude 2.1, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">except</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Claude Instant 1 and Claude 2. At first I thought it was something like the base model, but that would imply that Claude 2.1 used an older base model than Claude 2?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also Claude 2 is given a second codename of "Project Hatch", but in 2023 the </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2023/07/11/technology/anthropic-ai-claude-chatbot.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New York Times said that Project Hatch was a ChatGPT-like interface to Claude</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (presumably claude.ai), not an LLM?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbpb94whv6po"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbpb94whv6po">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some documents are even less interesting! E.g. </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.434709/gov.uscourts.cand.434709.563.8.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Copy Fairy Code</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is entirely redacted.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/em5wnA7gpQSvfsuxQ/some-interesting-internal-documents-from-anthropic#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The PPA: A New Approach to Anthropics](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QCCnqY54W4YPXvYLm/the-ppa-a-new-approach-to-anthropics)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[This is the first piece in a series on the Parametric Parameter Assumption (PPA), a new model of anthropics. This particular piece is self-contained and for most people will contain all they need to know.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TLDR:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With the right prior, the PPA avoids nearly every major anthropic paradox, including the Doomsday Argument, the Presumptuous Philosopher, and the inability to handle infinite universes.</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The PPA divides the universe into two types of uncertainty: epistemic unknowns and randomness (a.k.a aleatoric unknowns). The first is treated SSAishly and the second is treated SIAishly.</span></i></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This piece also introduces the "Basic Model" of anthropics, a single universe model that, paired with the SSA or SIA, exposes many different anthropic paradoxes simultaneously. None of these paradoxes persist in the PPA.]</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the first piece in a series introducing the Parametric Process Assumption (PPA), a new model of anthropics which allows us to resolve very nearly all anthropic paradoxes I am aware of. With the right prior, the PPA rejects both the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doomsday_argument" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doomsday Argument</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the </span><a href="https://anthropic-principle.com/preprints/mys/mysteries.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Presumptuous Philosopher</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and can operate seamlessly over both finite and infinite universes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The motivating idea behind the PPA is that there are really two different ways to interpret a statement like "the probability of X": </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Frequentist approach, where probabilities represent the convergence of random trials. Flip many coins and about half will come up heads.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Bayesian approach, where probabilities are beliefs. We don't know if the googolth digit of pi is even or odd, so we might as well say there's a 50% chance it's even.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first interpretation makes more sense for some types of questions, while the second approach makes more sense for others. But it turns out that if you take the standard framing for anthropic problems - where a universe is first produced and then you are assigned an observer identity within it - you will get different answers depending on whether you model the universe in the first way or the second. In fact, a fully Frequentist, infinitely repeating universe yields the SIA, while a fully Bayesian one yields the SSA.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The PPA, however, says that the solution is to compose both interpretations. We imagine the entire universe as a random process progressing according to the Frequentist definition, but the laws of physics - the things which actually control the dynamics of the process, like the weak nuclear force or the strength of gravity - are treated in the Bayesian sense. The Bayesian portion we call the "parameters," and we refer to the rest as the "process."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, since anthropics is mostly focused on the number of observers, we actually get to absorb almost all the important aspects of the laws of physics into a single parameter which we'll call the "propensity for life" - for any given laws of physics, propensity for life is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">expected</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> number of times we expect intelligent life to emerge. Anything else, such as how long life lasts once it's here, we treat as random, since that is governed less by the highest-level laws of physics and more by the local dynamics of whatever patch of spacetime that life happens to exist in.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The end result a model that is neither "halfer" nor "thirder."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7ppo8lor7eh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7ppo8lor7eh">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Rather, the PPA effectively composes the SIAish process portion inside the SSAish parameter portion, inheriting the positives of both approaches without making any of the sacrifices of other competing models, including the various "double halfer" methods existing in the literature. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefghmy1zwcczn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnghmy1zwcczn">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, the PPA is not without weaknesses. The biggest of these is that, by virtue of defining certain variables as parameters, the PPA gives us an extra degree of freedom. Having this extra decision point opens the PPA up to accusations of overfitting, and this makes it extra important to properly motivate the priors we use. I do not know if all decisions this series makes in that regard will be unimpeachable, but if nothing else the PPA's complete lack of paradoxes should serve as an existence proof of a better way forward for anthropics. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This introductory piece will be self-contained, and should give you a good idea of how the PPA works, albeit with some details left out. The rest of this series will flesh out and build on these ideas, while also serving as an exploration of anthropics as a whole. Much of what I took for granted when I first started reading about this topic turned out to be wrong or exaggerated, and I suspect I am not the only one who made these mistakes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz9iorfvyfq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz9iorfvyfq">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Motivating the PPA</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The best way to motivate the PPA is, naturally, with an appeal to the Sleeping Beauty problem (SBP).&nbsp; Recall that in the SBP, Sleeping Beauty is put to sleep by scientists on a Sunday, after which a coin is flipped. On heads, she is woken on Monday, and on tails she is woken on Monday and Tuesday, with an amnesia drug administered in between so that she has no knowledge of which day it is. The SBP asks “upon waking, what should be the Beauty’s credence that the coin went heads?”</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790948486/lexical_client_uploads/m3grxeebcchdgzi9ayib.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One answer to this question - the “thirder” answer associated with the SIA - is that we ought to give 1/3 credence to the coin going heads. There are a few arguments for this stance, but the most relevant one for our purposes is the “argument from repetition.”&nbsp; If we were to imagine the Sleeping Beauty experiment being repeated some arbitrarily large number of times, then we would find that 1/3 of Sleeping Beauty’s wake-ups were on heads. Since the beauty is “assigned” one of these wake-ups at random, she should give 1/3 credence to waking on a “heads” day.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790948501/lexical_client_uploads/r5f3qbukds7nm0bxixxi.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ll discuss the validity of this logic in the context of wake-ups some other time. For now, taking it at face value, this argument seems to be a straightforward proof of the SIA</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhkhtdl87azb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhkhtdl87azb">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The impetus behind the PPA (at least this particular version of it) is that, as a matter of fact, generalizing the argument from repetition does&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;actually yield the exact SIA. It gets results consistent with the SIA for the types of problems that anthropics usually likes to consider, but if you apply it universally that won't always be the case.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To see why, consider a modification of the Sleeping Beauty problem where instead of flipping a coin, the scientists compute the googolth (10^100) digit of pi. If the googolth digit is even, then Sleeping Beauty is woken up once. If it is odd, she is woken twice. Sleeping Beauty, we can assume, neither knows the googolth digit of pi nor knows how to compute it.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790948526/lexical_client_uploads/eogv3nivh7smsoleem0z.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On its face, this is an extremely similar problem to the vanilla SBP. Because Sleeping Beauty has no particular reason to assume the digit of pi is more likely to be even than odd, she could reasonably assign 50% credence to either possibility before the experiment starts.&nbsp;However, if we were to actually apply the argument from repetition to this experiment,&nbsp;we would find that there is no possible way to derive a credence of 1/3.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason is that the googolth digit of pi does not change between repetitions. It’s not even random in the conventional sense of the word, since you cannot simply “re-roll” pi to get a different sequence of digits. The digits of pi are fixed, unalterable truths about our world. “50% chance of even,” is a statement about belief, not the dynamics of the world.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With that in mind, there are two things that can happen:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the digit is even: on every repetition, the beauty is woken only on Monday. There is a 100% chance every day is Monday.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the digit is odd: on every repetition, the beauty is woken exactly once on Monday and once on Tuesday. There is a 100% chance that half of all days are Monday, and the other half are Tuesday.</span></li></ol><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790948577/lexical_client_uploads/mqph4op2m2jpxwomww7e.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not being placed randomly in a single sequence, but rather being placed with 50% probability into one of two sequences. This means that the possibility of Tuesday is nested within the possibility of an odd digit - one can only ever compute a 25% chance. Thus, in this scenario, we are forced by the argument from repetition to accept the SSA.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The PPA is what you get if you take repetition to its logical conclusion. It operates by essentially decomposing the universe into two layers. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The lower level, the “process” (also known as the “likelihood” in standard statistical parlance), is just a progression of random events happening one after the other, like the repeated flips of Sleeping Beauty’s coin, and we use the SIA to place ourselves within it. The outer layer, the “parameters,” represents facts about the universe which are too fixed to be rightly called random, but which we nevertheless do not know; we handle these according to the SSA.&nbsp;&nbsp;</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Basic Model</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One unfortunate side effect of using the PPA is that it becomes much harder to hold discussion through the usual thought experiments like the Sleeping Beauty Problem. It suddenly becomes necessary to specify which parts of the experiments represent parameters and which parts represent processes, and even once you have made these specifications (by, say, replacing a coin flip with a digit of pi), the resulting scenario rarely feels analogous to whatever question you were originally trying to ask.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this section, we’re going to try to build a better model. Our focus will be on two of the biggest paradoxes in anthropics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The&nbsp;</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doomsday Argument</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflr7o0xgmhqc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlr7o0xgmhqc">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which says that if you’re using the SSA and you discover that you are, say, roughly the one hundred-billionth person ever born, your credence in your species being near extinction should increase.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The&nbsp;</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Presumptuous Philosopher</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;argument, which says that if you are using the SIA, you should bias towards existing in the universe with the most possible observers.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Throughout this series, we’ll spill a lot of virtual ink on the many subtleties of these two paradoxes, but for now the most important thing to realize is that these two statements are not, in fact, opposites. The Doomsday Argument concerns itself with the amount of time your specific species has left until extinction, and triggers only once you’ve learned information about when you were born relative to the “first” human. The Presumptuous Philosopher effect concerns itself with the amount of life in the entirety of existence, and outside of certain toy problems would not typically disappear just because you learned something about your particular species. Indeed, it is possible for a single assumption of anthropics to produce both effects at once.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreft7cxk3j3zoi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnt7cxk3j3zoi">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The terminology we’re going to use is that the Doomsday Argument pertains to the&nbsp;</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">longevity</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;of your species, whereas the Presumptuous Philosopher pertains to the universe’s&nbsp;</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">propensity for life,&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or more often just “propensity⁠.” In general, the SSA does a better job for “big universe-level questions” like propensity, but fails at smaller scales where longevity becomes relevant, and the SIA does the reverse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our goal will be to build a single universe model that incorporates both of these concepts, which we're going to call the </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basic Model </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of anthropics. The idea is that we will imagine the universe as a gigantic random process that, through randomness, sometimes produces intelligent species. Like the digits of pi, the approximate size of the universe and its probability of randomly producing life is fixed but unknown, while most other facts - including how long an instance of a species lasts once produced - are genuinely random.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1790948598/lexical_client_uploads/xs9qwvk1hhqfyndwxcbq.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Basic Model. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each species emerges somewhere in the universe, exists and grows for some time, and then goes extinct. Each color represents the different physiology/conditions under which the species exist, controlled by</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the diversity variable.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's easiest to illustrate this model through a simplified version, which we'll call the "grid universe." Here, we imagine our universe as a big grid of some unknown size. As in the Basic Model, the size of the grid is not random but, again like the digits of pi, somehow fixed but unknown.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791557477/lexical_client_uploads/hxth1b7pztehesl6srqh.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is truly random, however, is the pattern that will fill this grid. Starting at the top, going down row by row, a random process fills the grid with colored pixels:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791557487/lexical_client_uploads/mwwzr4ljjmteelw97uni.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pixels repent the randomness in the universe. The grid is filled from top to bottom, representing the progression of our universe from one state to the next over time. The particular pattern of pixels on each row depends only on the row above it.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The purpose of these pixels is to represent the randomness in our universe, from galaxies to planets to the individual reactions on those surfaces. It's just a bunch of stuff happening, and though it is random is not uniformly so - the colors of the pixels (much like the randomness in our universe) depend on each other in a myriad of complex ways. The one constraint is that - just as time in our universe progresses in only one direction - the colors on each row cannot depend on the colors of any rows below them. The grid is filled top to bottom and the future only ever depends on the past.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefb989hm1taoi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnb989hm1taoi">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point, through the progression of this randomness, blue pixels might emerge. The exact likelihood of a blue pixel emerging doesn't particularly matter, as long as it is relatively low, and we can assume it is known or unknown for our current purposes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's also say that these blue pixels, unlike all the other pixels, are “self-aware,” in the sense that they get to look at some of the pixels above and to the left and to the right, anywhere but into the future, and ask themselves hard philosophical questions like "why am I here?" and “should I be surprised at what I see?” </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Blue pixels also propagate - if a pixel is blue, there is some fixed chance the pixel directly below it will also be blue. The blue pixels only know that whatever the chance of another blue pixel, it is determined primarily by randomness within the grid world (in other words, propagation is more like a coin toss than a digit of pi). For the sake of argument we can suppose that the blue pixels have looked around and decided that there is an approximately 10% chance that a blue pixel won't be followed by another. The relevant part is that whatever prior the pixel has, it is attributing the final outcome to random variation, not some inherent property of the universe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can also assume the grid is large enough - and the pixel’s visual range limited enough - that there is no real chance of the pixel seeing an edge, nor of the chain terminating because it hits the bottom. Other than that, though, just as we can only really observe our local slice of spacetime, the pixels don’t know anything about the size of the grid.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791557494/lexical_client_uploads/tc5ubuc4mcrvly0qekwn.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Blue pixels emerge in chains. They represent life emerging randomly.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, it’s unclear exactly what mechanism “assigned” consciousness to the blue pixel, or if the word “assign” even makes sense in this context. All the average blue pixel knows is that it exists, it has seen some blue pixels behind it, and there are likely going to be more blue pixels after it. What it wants to know are the two questions we already discussed:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Longevity&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of the current chain</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How many more blue pixels will be produced before the current chain terminates?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Propensity&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for blue pixel chains</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How many other blue pixel chains are out there?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From our perspective as a human outside the grid, certain answers seem obvious. The expected longevity for all chains, for instance, is 10, because the independent 10% chance of terminating means that continuing the chain is equivalent to rolling a 10-sided die over and over. Also, since dice rolls are “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorylessness" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">memoryless</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” (they don’t depend on previous rolls - see the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambler%27s_fallacy" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gambler’s Fallacy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) if we know a particular chain has already progressed for N pixels, we will expect the whole chain to be N+9 pixels long.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, from our outside perspective, the propensity to produce blue pixel chains would depend exclusively on the size of the grid and the probability of a blue pixel spontaneously emerging. But if all that happens is that we are shown a randomly selected blue pixel chain and told it was at least size N, we would not have enough evidence to make an informed decision about the size of the grid; the best we might be able to say was that the grid wasn’t so small that blue pixels were unlikely to form. After that, we would just make a reasonable guess, call it a “prior,” and use that to produce our credences.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The interesting thing about these simple conclusions though is that no existing anthropic approach can reproduce them, at least not without unacceptable side-effects. The SSA, especially when your prior gives non-zero weight to small grids, will under-estimate the length of the chain (a.k.a. the Doomsday Argument), while the SIA will strongly overestimate the size of the grid relative to the prior (a.k.a. the Presumptuous Philosopher). Double Halfer methods also fare poorly.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmkvgy01nzwj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmkvgy01nzwj">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As you might have guessed, the PPA alone reproduces these intuitions, predicting 10 more pixels in the chain while also closely matching our prior on grid size.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The PPA does this by modeling the size of the grid the same way it modeled the googolth digit of pi in our previous example, constructing a prior by assigning different probabilities to different possible sizes. Then, for each possible size, we imagine that the random patterns of the grid are re-sampled an infinite number of times, selecting “ourself” (that is, the blue pixel that we are) at random from the set of all blue pixels in the ever-repeating grid.&nbsp;</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791557272/lexical_client_uploads/e04xk2957htcz5r9giin.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In other words, we nest our predictions about the randomness of the pixels inside Bayesian predictions about the size of the grid (with an additional adjustment factor we'll discuss that compensates for the possibility the grid is empty).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Math</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My goal for most of this series is to keep the use of math isolated to only a few places, but it’s worth it to take a moment to spell out what the PPA’s distribution actually looks like.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of our anthropic assumptions start with a "universe prior" </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is the probability of a particular universe before we consider any distortions caused by being a a particular observer. In the Sleeping Beauty problem, for instance, we have </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because the entire thing is premised on the coin being fair.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The SIA in this case would be the distribution we get when the probability of being observer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in universe </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is proportional to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> whenever </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is an observer in universe </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and zero otherwise:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means that if each universe </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> observers, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which gives us the SIA’s presumptuousness - large universes get linearly larger. In particular, when there’s something you don’t know and can’t learn - like “propensity for life” in the Basic Model - you can never update away from largeness, and you are forced to assume life is arbitrarily abundant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The only way we can reduce the largeness bias is to reduce the multiplier to something less than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The SSA does this by dividing by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (again when </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is an observer in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">):</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">giving&nbsp;</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But this creates a strong bias in the other direction - the things you do know, like your rank in your species, cause you to update towards smallness. We’ll see there’s actually a lot of subtlety to that statement, but for now you can take it for granted that things like the Doomsday argument rely on this bias.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the PPA, we resolve these paradoxes by breaking our universes into two components, a process component </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a parameter component </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.&nbsp;The PPA says we should treat the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> distribution in an SIAish fashion, while nesting it inside an SSAish </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> distribution. Although the full PPA will need an extra term,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgsweql962ms"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngsweql962ms">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for now we can think of that as looking like this:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is defined as an SIA distribution built out of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moving some terms around, we get this:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, we divide the SIA’s prediction by the expected number of observers at a given parameter setting. In the Basic Model, this means finding the expected number of observers given that the laws of physics have a propensity for creating so-and-so many civilizations, so-and-so many large scale simulations, so-and-so many&nbsp;Boltzmann Brains, etc.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What this does, in essence, is divide out the propensity, while leaving everything else intact. You don’t know how many times the observer-generating processes start up, but you act SIAish (and thus in a temporally consistent way) about the process you are currently in.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the PPA Predicts</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following is a list of some of the biggest contradictions and anthropic paradoxes I’m aware of. As we will discuss, under the right universe model the PPA resolves every single paradox on this list.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Temporal Inconsistency/Telekinesis (Under SSA)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Under the SSA, we can take a future event of known probability - like a fair coin flip - and cause our credence in it to change just by pre-committing to certain decisions.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doomsday (SSA):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Since the SSA treats our birth rank as a uniform draw from all humans, we are biased towards smaller universes.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Presumptuousness&nbsp;(SIA):&nbsp;</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Under the SIA, we have undue bias towards larger universes.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pascal's Mugging (SIA):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Under the SIA, we can be forced to put full confidence in very unlikely universes if these universes have enough observers. Using a similar mechanism, the SIA forces us to believe with near certainty that we are either part of a simulation or a Boltzmann Brain.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Non-Bayesian Updates (Double Halfer)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: There are a few different Double Halfer methods, but what they all have in common is that they require something non-Bayesian like ignoring information (Updatelessness) or producing invalid updates (Bostrom’s Hybrid Model)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpwanpqf6ebc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpwanpqf6ebc">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Infinite Observers:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;This can mean a few different things in different contexts, and different assumptions all fail in different ways. The PPA cannot handle all possible distributions with infinite observers, but it can handle more than any other method, including all the ones one might expect given our understanding of physics</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw7whcmze6of"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw7whcmze6of">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The catch to this, which we will discuss, is an extra degree of freedom in our model. Specifically, we must decide which variables in our prior are "SSAish" and which are "SIAish." Much of our discussion will center around motivating this choice.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to read this Series</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This piece has covered most of the important details of the PPA. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The rest of the series will dig in a little deeper, hopefully clarifying or defending any points you didn’t follow or disagreed with. If you don't have time for all that but still want a little more detail, then </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fhqbJv4QrK7HzGxEm/ppa-9-putting-it-all-together" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this piece</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> makes a good follow up, summarizing and synthesizing all of the previous pieces.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Otherwise, the first two thirds of this series are "low-math"; there are no real equations and all the proofs are visuals worked out on simplified examples. It contains several pieces, like the ones on indexical reasoning or improper priors, which include introductions to intermediate-level concepts that you might already know. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only the last third of this series is fully technical. These parts will include full equations and derivations for the arguments made in the first portions, as well as handling of a few additional scenarios, such as infinite universes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pieces are labeled as follows:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background [BG]</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Low-Math [LM]</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical [T]</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And here is the full series.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[BG] </span></b><a This is known as “Full Non-indexical Conditioning," which we'll briefly discuss later in this series.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw7whcmze6of"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw7whcmze6of">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specifically, we can handle the cases where (1) there are infinitely many species/simulations in the universe and each of these (2) almost surely goes extinct/terminates eventually</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhjg8csva478"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhjg8csva478">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Better yet, assume the grid wraps around on the sides, so there is no edge</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnajvxwgny8ke"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefajvxwgny8ke">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It does add a Doomsday bias, but as long as your species doesn't permanently grow exponentially, this effect can be mitigated or even asymptotically disappear by all sorts of additional assumptions. If you fix all variables and parameters, the PPA is uniform over all observers. This is because it does not reweight on a per-process level, it reweights by the expected number of&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">observers</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;at a fixed parameter setting.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Subprocesses" do not have to be independent from one another or non-interacting. They are simply the units which we are dividing out of our model. All that's required is that they be large enough that "how long do they go on" is a meaningful question, and that the expected size of a subprocesses is independent of the total number of subprocesses.</span></li></ol></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QCCnqY54W4YPXvYLm/the-ppa-a-new-approach-to-anthropics#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Safety Culture at the Labs is Broken](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/the-safety-culture-at-the-labs-is-broken)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We need the frontier labs to have a deep and robust safety culture.</p>
<p>Alas, this is not the case.</p>
<p>Anthropic devotes considerable effort to cultivating and maintaining such a culture. It shows, especially among the members of technical staff. Having conversations with them can be a very different experience than talking to those at other labs, and there is a much greater sense of understanding and responsibility. But even there, the appreciation of the problems and their difficulties seem woefully insufficient. We see various symptoms of that more and more.</p>
<p>Recently, while I’ve had some good interactions, I’ve also had interactions that make clear that much of Anthropic does not share that same safety culture, with a lot of ‘oh we will muddle through because we are better at operational excellence unlike those other idiots’ and a belief that all the problems are prosaic. Culture is hard.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25689"></span>


</div>
<p>Then there are OpenAI’s cultural problems, which clearly run deeper. That point was only driven further home this week, with David Robinson resigning to blow the whistle, and <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/openai-fires-three-safety-researchers?r=67wny"><strong>OpenAI feeling forced to fire three of its safety researchers</strong></a>, on top of many past problems and incidents.</p>
<p>That is on top of additional misalignment reports from both OpenAI and Anthropic. The reports are good things, but what is reported emphasizes that we have big problems that run deep.</p>
<p>Thus, the growing consensus that we must find a way to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">Pace the Frontier</a> and slow the pace of AI capabilities advances. This is potentially made more complicated by what could be increased competitiveness from Google and Meta, with the releases of Argon and Wonder. It is still too early to provide proper assessments there.</p>
<p>There is a growing group of lab employees who have felt compelled to quit in protest. New York Magazine had a cover feature interviewing some prominent ones. The most famous is Coxon, who provided the tipping point for the current preference cascade.</p>
