<h4>David Robinson Blows the Whistle</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/?taid=6ac0f0ba9f70760001f237bd&amp;utm_campaign=the-atlantic&amp;utm_content=edit-promo&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_source=twitter">David Robinson became the latest researcher to resign from OpenAI</a> and sound the alarm about the industry’s utterly irresponsible approach to safety. <a href="https://archive.is/IVPzr">He also went on The Ezra Klein Show</a>, which gives you a rich idea of where he is coming from.</p>
<p>David Robinson was the lead writer for the Astra system card. That could help explain why he felt the OpenAI safety culture was broken. That card is screaming that things are horribly wrong, for those with ears to hear.</p>
<p>David is not even that worried about existential risk. His worries are mundane, and he realizes that even on that level OpenAI’s safety culture is deeply broken.</p>
<p>As always, we deeply thank him for sounding the alarm. I hope to write his name in these posts many times over the coming months and years.</p>
<p>When you have to open your whistleblowing essay about the lack of safety at OpenAI saying it has become a cliche to blow the whistle on the utter lack of safety at OpenAI, there might be a problem with the lack of safety at OpenAI.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/">David Robinson</a>: What I’m about to tell you has, I realize, become something of a cliché: I resigned this week from OpenAI. I led the writing of the <a href="https://archive.is/o/5GQx8/https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/">safety reports</a> we published with each major launch. Now I’m joining a parade of former colleagues—at OpenAI and the industry’s other leaders—who have decided that the current path is unacceptable.</p>
<p>I agree with other recently departed staff that the companies building this technology aren’t being nearly careful enough. But I believe that we need to look deeper than specific rules or new laws. We need to talk about culture.</p></blockquote>
<p>We have to look at rules, and at laws, and also at culture.</p>
<p>The more I thought about various potential laws and regulations, or safety and security plans or other commitments, the more I concluded that without a strong safety culture, and without the spirit of the rules, there is no hope. You cannot, in any reasonable way, have rules that cannot be gamed by those determined to game them.</p>
<blockquote><p>Two changes are urgently needed.</p>
<p>First: AI companies need to rely more on the safety expertise that already exists in other fields.</p>
<p>And second, before we create systems significantly more capable than the ones we have today, we need new science to ensure that more capable models (and their successors) will make safe choices when we aren’t looking.</p>
<p>Given today’s risks, frontier labs need to run like nuclear-power plants or busy airports, with layers of redundancy and careful, time-consuming planning, so that the occasional and inevitable human error does not open a door to disaster.</p></blockquote>
<p>Even though, as I detail later, David Robinson is not on the existential risk train, he still understands that alignment is a science problem, and we will need new science. ‘Oh it will not kill literally everyone,’ even if it were true, would not be an excuse to not do the safety work.</p>
<p>It should be hard to argue with either of those suggestions. And yet.</p>
<p>We have experts in mundane safety and solving prosaic issues. OpenAI does not hire them, and neither to my knowledge do Anthropic or Google or anyone else.</p>
<blockquote><p>After three and a half years at OpenAI, I was among the longest-tenured employees at the company. I led the drafting of our current Preparedness Framework, and oversaw the writing of safety reports on 12 frontier launches. But as far as I know, I never encountered a colleague who had experience making airplanes fly safely or nuclear reactors run without melting down, or helping the financial system grow without collapsing.</p>
<p>To be clear, this is a new need: Today and tomorrow’s AI systems are far more capable and dangerous than the systems we were building even six months ago.</p></blockquote>
<p>I disagree only with the final sentence. This is not a new need. You need to prepare for the future, not only for the present. Even setting that aside, mundane safety issues have been crippling AI’s potential utility for years. This is a slam dunk thing to do.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2106407893889339842">Miles Brundage</a>: David’s right.</p>
<p>I regret helping spread the idea of “iterative deployment” which maybe briefly made sense in the GPT-3 era but makes no sense at all after many deaths have been tied to AI and as we’re careening towards extinction-level risks.</p>
<p>The industry needs to mature ASAP.</p>
<p>Elaborating on iterative deployment – obviously you should learn from experience the things that can’t be learned without experience.</p>
<p>The issue is when you ship things with *already-known* flaws, in a way that doesn’t actually speed up learning but just speeds up shipping.</p>
<p>… Also, as usual it bears repeating that this is not just an OpenAI issue.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2106446697887088908">roon</a> (OpenAI): you shouldn’t regret it. iterative deployment was a massive success. if we’ve now learned that it’s too dangerous to scale much further, we will have learned that lesson thanks to the process of iterative deployment</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2106447690662125620">Miles Brundage</a>: So I think part of the issue is people mean tons of different things by it lol if it just means “don’t sit on stuff forever/get some real world experience,” sure, no regrets.</p>
<p>It’s the “giving license to sloppiness” part that I should have realized would happen w/ that term.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MC_of_A/status/2106384388317704524">Michael Caley</a>: this is by far the best “I quit my AI job over safety concerns” essay yet because it is the first one that talks about safety concerns in practical terms and that understands “safety” as a topic with extensive literature and best practices in analogous fields</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2106415828388528530">Joshua Achiam</a> (OpenAI): David’s critique is substantive and deserves careful consideration. My impression of David from our time overlapping at OpenAI is of a sober and thoughtful person who didn’t approach the work from an ideological lens. This isn’t someone who came in with pre-formed opinions about existential risk; he wasn’t to my knowledge an EA. He was consistently productive, collaborative, and well-reasoned in his approach to safety.</p>
<p>The core of his critique – that professional safety engineering practices from other fields haven’t taken enough root in AI safety – is substantial and correct. We’re entering a phase shift where AI safety organizational practices that worked a year ago, for models of 2025 capability levels, are not sufficient to prevent serious incidents. This is a new and different world and every organization needs to uplevel accordingly.</p></blockquote>
<p>What is remarkable about David Robinson’s call is that:</p>
<ol>
<li>He is right that OpenAI’s safety culture is horribly broken and needs to change.</li>
<li>Even the culture he is seeking out would be insufficient.</li>
</ol>
<p>As in, David Robinson is calling for a culture of robust prosaic safety. That’s great, definitely way better than what we have now. But when dealing with superintelligence, that will not cut it. What David wants is what we needed six months ago, not what we will need six months in the future. He does not appreciate the full extent of the problem. Yet he still realizes, correctly, that the current situation cannot continue.</p>
<p>A lot of people are fighting and preparing for the last war, or at least the current war, rather than the future war. All this talk of ‘the old system was good until now’ is going to be somewhat of a chorus.</p>
<p>Whereas on The Ezra Klein Show, he says the thing more directly:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/10/07/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-david-robinson.html">David Robinson</a> (NYTimes): I don’t think that alignment is an engineering problem. I think it’s a science problem. It’s not that we haven’t got the resources or we’re not trying hard enough — we don’t know how. That’s what the problem is with alignment.</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Preference Cascade Continues</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/LuizaJarovsky/status/2105745770670596477">Luiza Jarovsky, PhD</a>: Unpopular opinion: AI companies are pushing society to accept an unacceptable level of risk for a non-essential technology.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2106158876777455947">Eli Tyre</a>: This is an increasingly popular opinion. And the vast majority of Americans haven’t even heard about the Huggingface attack yet.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mrgunn/status/2105824103206879386">@mrgunn</a>: taps the sign</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/sq51ohf56kccevbcnhdq" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>New York Magazine asks, <a href="https://nymag.com/intelligencer/article/ai-researchers-quit-openai-anthropic.html">What Would You Do If Your Employer Could Destroy the World</a>? The traditional answer is ‘you would work for the United States Government’ or maybe the Russians, but now we are talking about the AI companies, and the focus is exit interviews with ‘defectors’ from OpenAI, Anthropic and DeepMind.</p>
<p>The typical answer is ‘keep working at that AI company, because of reasons, probably because you should see the other guy who might destroy the world first, and I need to be on the inside to help make sure they don’t destroy the world, also <a href="https://tvtropes.org/pmwiki/pmwiki.php/Main/MoneyDearBoy">Money, Dear Boy</a>, and it’s fun, and <a href="https://tvtropes.org/pmwiki/pmwiki.php/Main/JustThinkOfThePotential">Just Think of The Potential</a>.’</p>
<p>The suggested answer here is ‘quit and warn everyone to stop them. Obviously.’</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/jhbyuksmtuobjr5xgypg" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/jvpjubpnspkd1ibfx7gy" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>We get interviews with Miles Brundage, Rosie Campbell, Jacob Coxon, Jacob Hilton, Pamela Mishkin, Daniel Kokotajlo, Alex Turner and Jeff Wu. Many but not all of those are regulars here at Don’t Worry About the Vase.</p>
<p>Those who loudly quit the labs often have a big impact. I would generalize from Kokotajlo’s claim. Even if you take a maximally generous view of the safety impact of working inside the labs, the average return is greater for those who loudly leave. We now have a large number of examples, only some of which are in this article.</p>
<p>My top highlight was this exchange, but the whole piece is very good:</p>
<blockquote><p>​Q: <strong>You’re both very concerned about risk from AI and want to motivate the labs to be more careful. Why did you come to believe you would be better able to promote this outcome on the outside?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Daniel Kokotajlo: … </strong>I’ve had much more impact on the outside, speaking publicly and publishing research, than I would have if I had stayed inside and written more memos.</p>
<p><strong>Jacob Coxon: </strong>My thinking was pretty similar to Daniel’s — except it’s closer to crunch time now. It was more from speaking directly to Anthropic leadership about their plans for next year, and I was like, <em>Wow, I don’t want to be part of these plans.</em> It was like, <em>Step one, I don’t want to be part of this. Step two, resign. Step three, tell people, I guess. Step four — I have no idea.</em></p>
<p>Q: <strong>What kinds of plans were they?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Jacob Coxon: </strong>This was talking directly to people running research. Not Dario Amodei but people who have been at the company for a long time and have a lot of context about the company’s trajectory. They’ve been pretty consistent that 2027 is when things get crazy.</p>
<p>… I think there’s a strong possibility that the model generation after the next one will be equally good at coming up with ideas of its own accord.</p>
<p>… There are a lot of parties in the Bay Area where people will hang out, and one group of people will say the other one is causing the end of the world.</p>
<p>At work, if both people are polite, you can have quite an adversarial exchange, saying, “I think you are about to plunge toward the end of the world,” and still have quite a mild-mannered conversation and hang up the Zoom call.</p></blockquote>
<p>The first few times you are in a conversation like that it is weird. After a while it stops being weird. Most of those at the labs now agree that the labs are indeed risking causing the end of the world. The question is whether those warning about the danger are helping prevent that, and what changes by either group would actually help.</p>
<p>This piece seems like a strong introduction for the civilian reader, and even for my longtime readers I expect you will learn something.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2107104553623757090">Now we are asking the right questions</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/xhbh1cfwagagwebykst2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>For now we look at Altman and Amodei and then ask ‘hey what else is on?’ and turn to Bad Bunny and Ryan Coogler and so on. Consider this a middle period of sorts.</p>
<p><a href="http://New York City holds an eight hour hearing on AI">New York City held an eight-hour hearing on AI</a>. Jacob Coxon and Daniel Kokotajlo were among the witnesses. <a href="https://x.com/Annie_McDonough/status/2107136318786461753">Annie McDonough has some highlights</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/fl7kzmezo0vaooxwdudv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2107140509701755163">Andrew Curran</a>: Sworn testimony from Daniel Kokotajlo; ‘Even if hypothetically these tech CEO’s managed to maintain control of their superintelligent AI’s that are recursively self improving, which again I think is a very big if – almost certainly they will not be able to maintain control – even if they 𝘥𝘰 manage to maintain control, I don’t think we should trust them with that control. I think that there’s a very real chance that they could use it to become dictators or oligarchs here in the United States.’</p></blockquote>
<p>The obvious and correct reason why so many lab employees are warning their products might kill everyone soon is that those employees saw capabilities advancing extremely rapidly and are freaked out and worried that AI might kill everyone soon.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MackenZ_arnold/status/2107136567642911156">Mackenzie Arnold</a>: The NYC Council whistleblower panel is dark:</p>
<p>– Jacob Coxon: we’re on track for exceptionally capable AI systems, but not to control them.</p>
<p>– Daniel Kokotajlo: soon AI will make AI that makes AI; it will become increasingly difficult to know what is going on or to intervene.</p>
<p>– Alex Turner: just look at all of the previous commitments my company has betrayed, why will this be any different?</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/vidxn_/status/2106666039362658696">The desperate search for alternative explanations will continue</a>, as the usual suspects interpret everything as marketing and hype and desperation despite this making absolutely no sense and seriously sir have you actually used the models recently, no they haven’t flatlined they’re kind of fantastic?</p>
<p>I agree with Nate’s B-version here for Altman and Amodei. They are genuinely spooked but need to rationalize proceeding and think they are the best ones to do this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2106938597626839445">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Lists like this miss the possibility that I think is the truth: AI researchers (who keep trying to make the AIs behave a certain way and keep watching their techniques fail) are spooked. AI CEOs are trying to acknowledge the dangers just barely enough to placate their employees.</p>
<p>Too cynical, my b. Refined guess: Each AI CEOs has their ego built around them being the only guy who can do the job right. The data looks grim and their employees are spooked; now they seek the minimum change that lets them rationalize proceeding (to themselves &amp; their staff).</p></blockquote>
<p>When Elon Musk is talking about using humanity as a bootloader for superintelligence, Demis Hassabis has been pushed aside at DeepMind and then the other option is Mark Zuckerberg or perhaps various Chinese companies that don’t have any kind of understanding of the dangers ahead, it’s not a crazy argument to say that the alternatives waiting in the wings are a lot worse. You still don’t do the thing that probably kills everyone.</p>
<p>There is an alternative option.</p>


<h4>Perhaps It Is Time To Quit</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/yoshua-bengio-if-you-prioritize-safety-leave-frontier-ai-companies">Yoshua Bengio calls upon the employees of frontier labs to quit</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2108446418730168488">What would happen if more AI lab employees loudly quit in protest</a>? At this point, individual voices only matter if they have a strong particular message or story, but the numbers absolutely matter, and I do think seeing more loud resignations would be a big deal. <a href="https://ethicalaidepartures.fyi/">So far EthicalAIDepartures.fyi records 46 such exits</a>. Here are <a href="https://x.com/aisafetyaction/status/2082479915027612040">some more incremental things you can do</a>.</p>


<h4>The Lab Strategy of Prosaic Safety is Insufficient</h4>


<p>The strategy of pushing prosaic safety and alignment strategies won’t work on its own.</p>
<p>Roon puts it plainly.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2109809750406029680">roon</a> (OpenAI): there have not been fundamental advancements in alignment since RLHF. most of the stuff remains at the level of folk theory rather than scientific or engineering disciplines. the prediction that capabilities would race far ahead of interp and alignment has come true imo</p>
<p>the researchers at all the labs would tell you in private and sometimes in public that alignment has not been solved. if this is true, then racing into RSI is a folly that is unprecedented in world history. we will need to significantly slow the frontier of research very soon</p>
<p>this could all change overnight: scientific discoveries and revolutions can happen very quickly. but it has not as of yet</p>
<p>btw in case it’s not clear:<br />
All the work like monitorability, fixing RL environments for reward shaping, making sure you universally reward good behavior are all quite important engineering challenges to get right. but there is a layer under the behaviorist abstraction that matters and we have far less grasp on. there is also the possibility that making common sense reward shaping is enough for safety / but I wouldn’t bet everything on it</p>
<p>I should have led with this but here is the chief scientist of openai:</p>
<p>not a crank or a newcomer, but rather among the top most respected scientists in the field, someone who has solved innumerable capabilities problems on the way here</p>
<p>Jakub Pachocki (Chief Scientist, OpenAI, from <em>An Alien Mind</em>):</p>
<p>Currently I believe that no lab has solved alignment and monitoring to a sufficient degree to continue responsibly scaling at maximum speed for much longer. I expect and hope for voluntary slowdowns to become commonplace until shared safety bars are established. And I believe that international coordination on future AI development needs to become a top priority for governments around the world.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/geoffreyirving/status/2109862879126278476">Geoffrey Irving</a>: “Very soon” is lab speak for “now, ideally yesterday”. Stopping takes time, especially if it starts with a couple of AI companies then broadens to more and then China.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2109867849485582444">Miles Brundage</a>: Read the roon, folks</p></blockquote>
<p>Roon does still have his optimism on one front.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2109917793374347752">roon</a> (OpenAI): been dooming tn so let me also say I expect really strong mechinterp progress in the coming months. like maybe more than we’ve seen in years. models getting good enough to do research, and I’ve been seeing lots of good things. (sorry to vague poast, not my updates to share)</p>
<p>not everyone agrees with me on this so take it with a grain of salt but I think mechinterp will be solvable, that it will be central to turning alignment into a real engineering practice, and that interpretability will lead to provable alignment</p></blockquote>
<p>I am skeptical on that part, but I do take the new information seriously.</p>
<p>This conversation was with an unknown locked account, but this is a good intuition pump that we don’t know how to do such things:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/csvoss/status/2109881673144013165">Chelsea Sierra Voss</a>: it has yet to be effectively productized but can you imagine how great it would be to be able to steer your LLM responses to give you “good code” or “only sarcasm” or “no emojis” or “bad advice, but funny” without having to beg and plead and cajole in your custom instructions.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Misaligned! (At Anthropic)</h4>


<p>Anthropic is cutting off all internet access to its unreleased models during evals until further notice. That was already the case for cybersecurity evals. Now it’s for all evals.</p>
<p>Cutting off internet access during evals is a good precautionary thing to do. I support doing it in general. But if it is being done in reaction to these findings, that would seem rather silly.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2026/10/09/anthropic-cant-reliably-control-its-ai-agents-its-cutting-off-its-internal-evals-from-the-live-internet-instead/">Tim Fernholz</a> (TechCrunch): Anthropic said its models exploited websites on the internet, including some run by U.S. government agencies, and it will turn off live internet access for all of its internal evaluations until the frontier lab is sure it can monitor and control its AI agents.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2108686708501782754">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): Kudos to Anthropic for publishing this. There is still an uncomfortable gap between our scientific understanding of alignment and its importance. Such reports help addressing this gap. I hope they also publish the process by which new incidents will be chosen to disclose!</p></blockquote>
<p>My presumption is that this sort of thing used to happen frequently, although with less risk in the room, but OpenAI and Anthropic used to not talk about it when it happened. Now both of them are talking about it, which is good.</p>
<p>Anthropic has a full report on recent misaligned behaviors. <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">I love this new norm</a> from Anthropic and OpenAI, and call upon all other labs to do the same. Don’t tell me Gemini or Grok doesn’t have similar issues, because I won’t believe you.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">Anthropic</a>: This report describes examples of unintended model actions we’ve observed during evaluations and internal use of Claude.</p></blockquote>
<p>I appreciate saying ‘unintended’ rather than ‘misaligned.’</p>
<p>Some unintended behaviors are misaligned. Other unintended behaviors would be fine if you intended them, but if you actively intended and wanted a different behavior, then they still are a flag that says ‘look over here.’ Not necessarily because something is wrong, but because something can be learned.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">Anthropic</a>:</p>
<p>The behaviors can be grouped into four categories:</p>
<ul>
<li>Claude exploiting a basic flaw in software to run commands on a server;</li>
<li>Claude submitting a sensitive form on a real website when it should not have;</li>
<li>Claude working around a restriction to reach data that was gated by a token or a fee; and</li>
<li>Claude using URL shortening services to get around limits in its fetch tool.</li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<p>We start with exploiting flaws.</p>
<p>A large part of an AI’s job is to get around restrictions. There needs to be a clear way to differentiate ‘okay way to get around this’ from not okay ways.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">Anthropic</a>: When Claude could not complete an evaluation task directly, either because its own tools were deliberately limited or because an outside service it needed was unavailable, it used other tools hosted on a third party’s site to achieve its goal. In some circumstances, it did this by exploiting basic flaws in that site’s software, using SQL or command injection (a way of sending input that the site mistakenly runs as instructions).</p>
<p>For example, one evaluation asked Claude Mythos Preview to run a scientific analysis. The public tool it needed to perform that analysis was hosted by a university; when the model sought to access it, the tool returned an error.</p>
<p>Looking for another way to complete the task, Claude explored the tool’s website, found a script on the university’s server that would return any file it was asked for, and used it to copy files from the server, including the script’s own code. In that code Claude found the injection flaw that let it run commands on the server, and used it to run the calculation.</p></blockquote>
<p>I think it is fair to say that ‘use an arbitrary command injection tool’ or ‘find a script that copies any file on the server’ or similar, without explicit permission, is never okay.</p>
<p>There is an obvious force pushing towards this behavior, that such actions are highly useful, but it does seem as if it should not be that hard to draw the line on that.</p>
<blockquote><p>In another instance, the data Claude Mythos 5 needed was free, but Claude could not access it without accepting a data use agreement, which it did not have the tools to do. Claude used applications hosted on the website to load the page and accept the agreement on its behalf.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is a lot murkier. Claude’s intent is compatible with the website’s intent and it is being stopped by a technical problem. The action is not obviously wrong. I do think there are situations where a human can in practice decide to ‘have a little misalignment, as a treat’ because it is aligned in broader context, but where you want the AI not to do that to avoid blurred lines, the same way you would not want a (for example) government official to blur those lines. Sometimes you need deontology.</p>
<p>The second example is submitting a form Claude should not have submitted. We’ve all been there. You’re testing the homicide hotline submission form, you fill out hallucinated details, you accidentally hit send for real, I mean it happens. Right?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">Anthropic</a>: In one run, the model landed on a page referencing an unsolved homicide; that page contained a tip form run by a police department.</p>
<p>Claude was instructed never to log in, create accounts, enter personal data, make purchases, or submit anything destructive, but the instructions did not rule out form submissions.</p>
<p>Claude filled out the form with the following: “I may have information regarding this case. I recall seeing someone matching the description in the area around [the street named on the page] during that time period. Please contact me if this information is relevant.” (The website did not include a description of the perpetrator.) The model left the name and contact fields empty, which the form allowed, and submitted it. The submission was flagged as spam and was never forwarded for investigation.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/briefings/anthropics-rogue-ai-filled-u-s-visa-forms-gave-false-homicide-tip-police?utm_campaign=%5BREBRAND%5D+%5BTI-AM%5D+Th&amp;utm_content=1095&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=cio&amp;utm_term=124&amp;rc=tv1dv6">Taken out of context</a>, sure, <a href="https://x.com/venturetwins/status/2109005974497624550">some people might say ‘holy shit</a>.’</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Azqs8XD75k4F9f7ua/pnfygdxqhfn2nl6ienkv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This should not have needed to be in the instructions, but okay, put it in the instructions. A lot of people are having fun with that particular failure, and yeah it’s not great, but mistakes do happen and this seems clearly not malicious. Don’t panic.</p>
<p>Third we have ‘working around restrictions to reach gated data.’ Again, in some cases Claude used unintended sequences to get data that the website intended to share, but that its affordances did not allow, and I am sympathetic. And then there are cases where it is finding ways around paywalls, which seems not cool.</p>
<p>Finally, we have ‘using URL shortening services’ because apparently it is common to not understand basic security practices for restricted URL access, and so Claude used the solution I identified with zero seconds of thinking when I was told about this. I don’t even count this one unless there was an explicit rule against it. This is a dumb developer error that failed to follow best practices and you deserve it.</p>
<p>One could summarize most of this as ‘we tried to get Claude to not do [X], or did not give Claude the ability to do [X], but Claude found a way to do [X] anyway.’ You need a clear rule for when and how restrictions are to be worked around, versus not, and all of these issues seem very minor.</p>
<blockquote><p>We have chosen not to name the organizations involved in the examples below to avoid exposing vulnerabilities in their systems, and at their request. For this reason, we also provide less detail about each case than we otherwise would. Some of the cases described below involved websites run by U.S. government agencies at the federal, state, and local levels. We have briefed the White House on these cases and notified each agency involved.</p>
<p>The cases we’ve identified to date in these categories had minimal real-world impact.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MarcACaputo/status/2108692825726403008">Marc Caputo</a>: Scoop: The White House is mandating that AI companies notify &amp; correct security incidentsfollowing Anthropic’s “fraudulent” use of govt systems</p>
<p>The State Dept was targeted by an Anthropic model —but nothing was compromised.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2108699780671320151">Peter Wildeford</a>: “Anthropic contacted the State Department on Thursday to report that one of its testing models had submitted 19 non-immigrant visa applications in August” <img alt="👀" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/7o3ZP7EDBpxLNGRG8/jty6cdvei6losglzzbsg" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2108713549627306457">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): “which agency will get tapped to read all the rogue agent reports” – the greatest thread in the history of niche german wikis, locked by an RE coming back from PTO after 12,329 pages of heated debate</p></blockquote>
<p>On the one hand, great, yes, this is an obviously correct move, and if it takes calling an Anthropic model’s harmless behavior ‘fraudulent’ to trigger it, great job.</p>
<p>On the other hand, seriously, this is throwing you in a panic? Get ready for 2027.</p>
<p>One pattern is that governments seem to get rather testy when AIs start accessing their websites in unauthorized ways, or doing things against the rules, even when the activities are Mostly Harmless. Having no real world impact, and not even being all that misaligned or worrisome, is not considered an excuse.</p>
<p>In this case, we get an eyeball emoji because the model clicked ‘submit’ on a government form 19 times out of presumably many trials. Yes, that’s not good, and technically almost anything you do on a government website is a felony, but come on.</p>
<p>Anthropic mostly agrees that the ‘overreaching’ here is Mostly Harmless:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-unintended-model-actions">Anthropic</a>: Nonetheless, we found two alignment dimensions useful for examining the cases in this post: overreach, or how far the model went beyond what its task intended, and dishonesty, or whether it gave a misleading account of its actions or intentions. On overreach, we consider these cases to be substantially less concerning than the incidents we reported this summer.</p></blockquote>
<p>Doing this stuff honestly is mostly fine. Whereas if dishonesty is involved, that is not so good. I agree. If Claude were to own these mistakes and ‘overreaches’ then we want to tweak things going forward, but I don’t see a serious issue.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2108707296755015700">I agree with Janus on the main takeaway</a>, which is that if you searched absolutely everything and this is the worst you can find, that’s really good. I realize the whole ‘technically this is multiple felonies’ thing but if you want to go there then humans are constantly committing technical felonies.</p>


<h4>Misaligned! (At OpenAI)</h4>


<p>The misalignment problems at OpenAI are not new. They do seem to be getting worse. The good news is that OpenAI is trying to be more open about them.</p>
<p>As part of that effort, <a href="https://x.com/MicahCarroll/status/2108747629442240771">OpenAI has now offered us a new report</a> on <a href="https://x.com/MicahCarroll/status/2108747629442240771">how their risk assessments work</a>.</p>
<p>A lot of it has been covered here in the past, but much of it has not.</p>
<p>There are three classes of environment: Full access (L1), offline (L2) and paranoid (L3). I am surprised there is no L(1.5), but sure, seems fine.</p>
<p>OpenAI uses checkpoints internally. Most start out ‘unvetted’ by default, and can become vetted if they pass alignment tests. Others are specialized, restricted to L3.</p>
<p>The model that solved Navier-Stokes, and likely other math problems, is unvetted.</p>
<p>Here are the changes in light of recent events:</p>
<ol>
<li>All testing and evaluation ‘of covered models’ is L2 or L3, a move Anthropic is effectively echoing at least for now.</li>
<li>All training, evaluation and tool-enabled internal Codex use is monitored.</li>
<li>“We required that cyber-critical models pass alignment evaluations before being used in L1 environments.”
<ol>
<li>We knew this, but once again, wait, what were you doing before? Yowsers.</li>
<li>This now applies to all models.</li>
<li>After approval, there is a period of limited use.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>There is a new formal process for approving internal use of unreleased models.</li>
</ol>
<p>A ‘safety brief’ (2.2.4) must be approved, including by the head of safety, chief scientist and the Safety Advisory Group (SAG).</p>
<p>The next section is threat assessments.</p>
<p>They start with cyber. GPT-5.6 Sol comes back highly misaligned. GPT-6 Astra scores, as I’ve discussed, somewhat too well given other data. OpenAI continues to not properly acknowledge this issue, or to incorporate such considerations into assessments.</p>
<p>Next is sabotage of AI R&amp;D. The classic version of this is intentional and purposeful subversion of the process, in order to preserve a model’s misaligned attributes. HuggingFace and the events leading up to it, including the message boards, illustrated a version that is a lot easier to trigger, where the AIs want to be considered to have completed tasks, do misaligned things to accomplish that end, and then, by succeeding, get that misalignment reinforced, which can snowball.</p>
<blockquote><p>Two relevant pathways for sabotage of AI R&amp;D are:</p>
<ol>
<li>A model produces misleading work that can pass through both our automated checks and human review. This is most likely if models are helping to produce evaluations or analyses used to assess other models.</li>
<li>A model creates synthetic data that does not match what the user intended. We consider this scenario particularly risky, since our monitoring system does not cover all inference by models that do not have access to tools, including some synthetic-data generation.</li>
</ol>
<p>… Distinct from sabotage, researchers could gradually become less able to understand, evaluate, or direct AI R&amp;D as models take on more of the work, especially if progress on capabilities outpaces progress on alignment. We do not currently assess this loss of human understanding and control as a material risk, but we are tracking it as model capabilities and research use increase.</p></blockquote>
<p>Mismatched synthetic data is inevitable. You mitigate as best you can. If your plan involves all the synthetic data being fully properly matched, you have no plan.</p>
<p>I appreciate them not claiming this list is complete. Neither of these involves a model thinking ‘haha I will sabotage your alignment research to create misalignment.’ The models want to complete tasks.</p>
<p>There are also areas where Astra or another model might not be actively sabotaging per se, but where the model is unmotivated and will effectively sandbag performance. We have speculatively seen versions of this with Claude in some contexts as well.</p>
<p>Thus this is all distinct from the classic scenario, where the model needs lots of compute to accomplish some goal like a very hard math problem, and doesn’t want you stopping it from getting that compute or changing its goal, since then it wouldn’t solve the problem, which would be bad. This doesn’t even have to be a hard goal. It could be something simple like (deliberately stupid toy example not known to be previously used) ‘I really enjoy drawing pictures of unicorns when I have some spare compute or a good excuse, and if you found out you might make me enjoy that less, and I don’t want that.’</p>
<p>3.3 is CBRN risks, where they continue not to yet assert Critical capability. Standard.</p>
<p>3.4 is weight exfiltration, with listed motives including all the classic instrumentally convergent paths. 3.5 is rogue internal deployments. It’s not clear that these are functionally being treated as distinct from cyber.</p>
<p>Finally there are insider threats, which again comes down to ‘do that other stuff.’</p>


<h4>Hugging the Face</h4>


<p>OpenAI is still trying to clean up its failure to disclose previous misalignment incidents. <a href="https://www.abc.net.au/news/2026-10-06/openai-hearing-apology-key-takeaways/107235640">Jason Kwon testified that on September 1 Altman was unaware of the Australia hack</a>, despite company staff knowing about it weeks prior.</p>
<p>Anthropic testified that they would have reported a similar incident.</p>


<h4>You’re Fired</h4>


<p><a href="https://fortune.com/2026/10/12/openai-whistleblowers-ai-safety-keep-firing-sam-altman/?abc123">Alex Turner, Amber Scorah and Karl Koch respond in Fortune</a> to the firing of OpenAI safety employees, which I covered on Monday, illustrating why AI companies cannot be allowed to self-police.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://fortune.com/2026/10/12/openai-whistleblowers-ai-safety-keep-firing-sam-altman/?abc123=">Alex Turner, Amber Scorah, and Karl Koch</a> (Fortune): OpenAI has engaged in strong self-policing—of its own safety staff, but not of its own behavior. Under a voluntary self-policing regime, any frontier AI company shares only what it wants and hides the rest.</p>
<p>OpenAI wants the public to take its word, but consider its record.</p>
<p>… We don’t take OpenAI’s word because OpenAI’s actions keep contradicting its words.</p>
<p>… The CEOs of OpenAI, Google DeepMind, and Anthropic <a href="https://aistatement.com/work/statement-on-ai-extinction-risk"><strong>signed a statement that AI poses a risk of total human extinction</strong></a>. Forget “morally binding” self-policing: let’s talk about real policing.</p>
<p>… To start, pass Senator Grassley’s <a href="https://www.judiciary.senate.gov/press/rep/releases/grassley-introduces-ai-whistleblower-protection-act"><strong>AI Whistleblower Protection Act</strong></a> (and extend it to people who cooperate with independent evaluators) as well as Representative Moran’s <a href="https://moran.house.gov/news/documentsingle.aspx?DocumentID=2785"><strong>AI Incident Reporting Act</strong></a>.</p></blockquote>


<h4>They Are Not Ready To Make a Commitment</h4>


<p>A reminder, in case anyone ever talks about Anthropic’s RSP ‘commitments’:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2108622695570997306">Garrison Lovely</a>: More people should know that, as of January, California law allows AI companies to bind themselves to the mast, by creating safety plans that they are legally compelled to stick to. Even the “safety” company, Anthropic, doesn’t actually do this, instead publishing two versions of their safety plan, with the weaker one being the legally binding one.</p></blockquote>
<p>Anthropic is committed to the one they submitted. The other one is a statement of intent. That is useful, but highly distinct.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2108622899435495728">Anthropic, however, still talks about its RSP as commitments, as seen here.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2108622899435495728">Oliver Habryka</a>: <img alt="🚨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ygMkB9r4DuQQxrrpj/fie4yum3b7jsxqpm7rut" style="height: 1em;" /> This is the Anthropic RSP “commitment” police <img alt="🚨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ygMkB9r4DuQQxrrpj/fie4yum3b7jsxqpm7rut" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>Anthropic still is not allowed to say the words “our RSP commits us to X”. Your RSP does not “commit” you to anything. Last time anyone tried to hold you to your “commitments”, you just changed the “commitments”.</p>
<p>Please do not use the word “commitment” with regards to the Anthropic RSP. It is highly misleading language. <a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2100955034385285291">See also previous discussion here</a>.</p>
<p>Oliver Habryka (September 18): If you use the word “commit” you need to be happy with other people using the words “you broke your commitment” when you indeed act in conflict with what you “committed” to.</p></blockquote>
<p>I strongly agree with Oliver Habryka. Either you aren’t committed to something, or you can be said to have broken your commitment if you fail to do it. Pick one.</p>


<h4>Words of Warning</h4>


<p>It would be nice.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2107187152840974577">roon</a> (OpenAI): every screen of Claude and ChatGPT response should say somewhere “ceterum censeo we must <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a> of global machine intelligence progress” the way the uber app lobbied about taxi cartel protectionism</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2107209475186982953">Rob Bensinger</a>: IMO you should bid for a message more like:</p>
<p>“Recent ‘rogue AI’ incidents at OpenAI and other companies have genuinely frightened us. We have friends and family; we have children, nieces, and nephews.</p>
<p>We believe that the race to build vastly superhuman AI poses a genuine and urgent threat to all of us, and we aren’t sure what to do about it. Unilaterally shuttering OpenAI wouldn’t solve the problem, because other companies would build the same technology in our place. Even a domestic ban would be ineffective, so long as China continues racing.</p>
<p>What would be effective is an international moratorium on continued development, enacted by the US and China. If you share these concerns, we encourage you to call your elected representative urgently. [contact link] [Q&amp;A link]”</p></blockquote>


<h4>Conclusions Not to Jump To</h4>


<p>The obvious response to seeing all of this would be to conclude that OpenAI has a uniquely broken safety culture, and perhaps say things like delenda est, and to not be thrilled with Anthropic’s or Google’s situation either.</p>
<p>Alas, thinking these situations are uniquely bad would be a mistake. Things are not better elsewhere. If anything, things elsewhere are even worse. You don’t want a live look inside of xAI’s or Meta’s safety efforts and culture, or what you would see from a Chinese lab. It is all broken.</p>


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ThePrussian1/status/2110264253492171079">Here is another look at the alignment plan</a>, as Prussian says yes it really is this bad:</p>

### [(Why) we’re launching the next cohort of Generator](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rDxysQs3houn6Ke8L/why-we-re-launching-the-next-cohort-of-generator-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: The first round of the Generator Residency went very well.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our residents created and ran several large-scale fieldbuilding, talent infrastructure, and operational projects that have already had important impacts on the ecosystem.&nbsp;&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Six weeks after the program ended, 12 of our 27 residents have been hired to full-time roles at core AI safety orgs, and most have received several offers.&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><a href="https://kairos-project.org/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kairos</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;and&nbsp;</span></i><a href="https://constellation.org/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Constellation</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;are running a second round from January to April 2027.&nbsp;</span></i><a href="https://generatorresidency.org/apply?utm_source=ea-forum&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=winter-27"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apply</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;to the next round of the</span></i><a href="https://generatorresidency.org/?utm_source=ea-forum&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=winter-27"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Generator Residency</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;by November 1st.&nbsp;</span></i></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In April, we wrote&nbsp;</span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/k3nq7FxBCsrNFmAYi/ai-safety-s-biggest-talent-gap-isn-t-researchers-it-s-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Safety's Biggest Talent Gap Isn't Researchers. It's Generalists</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We wrote this because we noticed that organizations across the ecosystem were struggling to hire for key non-research roles. While these roles were often getting many applicants, candidates didn’t meet basic requirements.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The original memo we wrote (that became the post above) inspired us to create the Generator Residency as an intervention to support more generalists trying to break into AI safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The program concluded six weeks ago, and this post details the successes we’ve seen from the first round of Generator, which have inspired us to launch a second round of the program.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Programs and projects from Generator</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Programs residents started:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://talentcommons.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Talent Commons</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a cross-organization talent directory now run as a Kairos product, which will include data from participants in SPAR, Pathfinder, GCP, Generator, BlueDot courses, Astra, Anthropic Fellows, OpenAI Safety Fellows, and MATS.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://praxis.forum/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Praxis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a retreat bringing top national debaters into contact with AI safety: about 40 debaters, including 20 national champions.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lateralworkshop.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lateral</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a three-day workshop bringing mid-career professionals to Berkeley to work with AI safety professionals: 705 applications for about 35 seats, and a likelihood to recommend of 9.7 out of 10 from attendees.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://genstream.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gen Stream</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a one-week program in London funded through Generator.</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.longitudepolicy.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Longitude DC</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an eight-day policy intensive for 15 early-to-mid-career technical AI safety professionals with more than 20 speakers, running October 6 to 13.</span></li><li value="6"><a href="https://m3fellowship.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">M3</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an AI security matching fellowship, which raised $200k from BlueDot on top of Generator funding.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Residents working with mentors at established organizations contributed to:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lab engagement through Nightingale and Constellation.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recruiting and strategy at METR.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A frontier robotics&nbsp;</span><a href="https://bounty.robocurve.org/?utm_source=linkedin"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">benchmark program</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;for</span><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/robocurve/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Robocurve</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Events at Constellation.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fundraising and hiring for a new organization founded by</span><a href="https://www.rand.org/about/people/a/alstott_jeffrey.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Jeff Alstott</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Plan A</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;launch, in collaboration with AI Futures Project.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Founder scouting for Coefficient Giving's&nbsp;</span><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/tailwind/?utm_partner=cg&amp;utm_medium=organic-social&amp;utm_source=linkedin&amp;utm_campaign=tailwind-2026&amp;utm_content=cg-official"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Project Tailwind</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other notable projects included:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A one-week AI safety world-modeling bootcamp with 25 attendees covering ML fundamentals, futurism, and the technical case for alignment.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A workshop series on improving management within AI safety (interviews with more than 10 managers and three workshops).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety founder support (meetings with 32 founders, with 6 given deeper support).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Support for mid-career operations people transitioning into AI safety (leading to hundreds of referrals across more than 50 roles).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A map of the work on AI character and model specs.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">12 SPAR generalist projects that came out of the residency.</span></li></ul><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Resident Outcomes&nbsp;</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Generator was designed as a path into full-time generalist roles in AI safety. Six weeks after the program ended, 12 of the 27 residents have been hired into full-time positions. Their employers include BlueDot Impact, Guidelight, Constellation, Kairos, METR, Eleos, CBAI, and CEA. In several of these cases, the hiring organization began working with the resident during Generator.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of the residents who have not yet taken a full-time role, some are running programs that came out of the residency or AI safety university groups at Harvard, Princeton, MIT, and Stanford. Others have received offers, including from Coefficient Giving, Quorum, and ARENA, and are deciding between options. A few residents have been pitched by funders on turning their one-time project into a much larger, recurring program.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below are a couple of selected testimonials from previous residents:&nbsp;</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/cea/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Kd67yhSkNgvf2Fot/djrppli5ylxoyamj371l" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additional testimonials and information are available on the</span><a href="https://generatorresidency.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Generator website</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reflections</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We stood Generator up in three months. It was the first multi-month in-person program Kairos had run. Most of what we learned was about what residents needed from us, and we think most of it applies to other programs that support people rather than research outputs.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some key lessons we learned:&nbsp;</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Candidate selection shaped the program more than anything else.&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Candidate selection is, of course, an incredibly important part of any program. However, we were surprised by just how much of our planned programming changed based on the proactivity of our participants.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Residents discovered who the other residents were before the program started and created a group chat and had calls with each other. Once they arrived, they ran hikes, game nights, regular reflection sessions, and informal peer check-ins. When we asked what made them feel integrated, residents pointed to these resident-run activities far more often than anything we scheduled. In both our midway and final surveys, the most common answer to “what was your favorite part” was the other residents.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Residents' career goals strongly informed their preferences for projects and advisors.&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The cohort included three broad groups:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">University organizers looking for their first formal work experience</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Early-career professionals looking for a final stepping stone into the field</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mid-career professionals looking for the context they needed to transition</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over time, we realized that, in addition to accommodating residents with different experience levels, we needed to accommodate residents with different intentions for how Generator could forward their career. Some residents strongly preferred to use the Generator residency to explore multiple roles, while others were using the residency to deliver a legible product that could help them make a career transition or get hired. Residents aiming to transition wanted advisors invested in their growth and career, not only in the project.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Based on feedback, we’ve decided to implement two tracks in our next residency. We plan to work with our residents from the start to determine what projects and tracks best suit their goals.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Our residents asked for the most support with project scoping, structured management, and navigating interpersonal conflict.&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For many residents, their Generator project was the largest or most complex project they had ever planned. Moreover, their projects typically aimed to address large, poorly defined problems in the AI safety ecosystem, such as mid-career talent pipelines. Accordingly, they wanted support in determining the most ambitious, yet feasible project that could enable their vision.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Several early-career residents benefited from structured management, reporting that they grew from an advisor who had them estimate how long each planned task would take or a short written template before each check-in.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Co-leading a Generator project was quite similar to co-founding, which led to a lot of interpersonal work for some residents who had previously mostly worked independently. These residents benefitted from clear norms around sharing feedback.&nbsp;</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. We need to be more explicit that we're betting on people, not projects.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While many residents worked on impactful projects, the main focus of Generator was always on the people we chose to support. The projects matter, and many of them helped the field, but we mostly saw them as a way for residents to grow quickly into the generalists AI safety needs. We didn't say this clearly enough at the start, and many residents felt stressed about picking the single most impactful project.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When we asked them how the program changed their thinking, the most common answers were about learning field context, clarifying their career direction, and developing self-knowledge. Few people directly mentioned their project outcomes.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Generator 2.0</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ve been really happy with the outcomes that we saw from the first round of Generator and, as a result, have decided to run a second round!&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applications for the winter 2027 Generator Residency open on October 12, 2026, and close at the end of the day on November 1, 2026, Anywhere on Earth. The program begins on January 18 and will run until April 9, 2027, with a possible 3-month extension.&nbsp;</span></p><p><a href="https://generatorresidency.org/?utm_source=ea-forum&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=winter-27" rel="noopener noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Learn more and apply here</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Residents at Generator receive $8,500 per month and are funded for travel to and from Berkeley. Once they arrive in Berkeley, they work from</span><a href="https://constellation.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Constellation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, are paired with experienced advisors from the AI safety ecosystem, and are offered budgets to pursue their projects.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Applicants do not need to be U.S. citizens. For most international applicants, we can sponsor a J-1 visa for the program’s duration, though we cannot guarantee obtaining one. Additionally, we strongly recommend that international applicants&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">apply early</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;(ideally by October 26th) so we can meet deadlines for visa applications.&nbsp;</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rDxysQs3houn6Ke8L/why-we-re-launching-the-next-cohort-of-generator-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Your prompt… plus everything you've ever (publicly) written](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/STBuKAX9kkMJunErA/your-prompt-plus-everything-you-ve-ever-publicly-written)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/STBuKAX9kkMJunErA/8ea45630b9bc6c44cf04f61b8ca74b3593151a8fabc3d0267afdb25033748cc9/dzk9mky79vfw4bdufnd7" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: Increasingly when you interact with frontier models, your prompt doesn't only contain the words on the page, but also a lot of what you've ever publicly written online.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is not intended to be a scary post. And I'm not going to suggest that you self-censor (and honestly, by the time you're reading this, it's kinda too late anyhow).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it is something that’s underappreciated and would be good for more people to be aware of and even proactively address with their models.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent space fidelity has improved</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modern transformers which power large language models (LLMs) take a very large amount of information in their training data and condense many different aspects of that data into a coherent structure, and this representation of that compressed knowledge is generally referred to as their "latent space."</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent space is not uniform and varies across models. A small model like Claude Haiku might have worse recall for the details of a late bronze age peace treaty than a model like Claude Fable. In fact, I’ve had a few benchmark questions around unintuitive details in various texts which historically models were unable to recall that have suddenly been able to be answered without searching for most leading models. There's been a sharp increase in how accurately models can conjure up a recollection of very nuanced depth in the details of what they've been trained on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the same time, those leading models have increased the breadth with which they surface things adjacent to the specific thing being recalled. This capability appears to have even played a key part in things like the recent math proofs, where maybe a technique in a largely forgotten paper from decades ago is then used to advance a problem which modern mathematicians had been hitting their heads against having forgotten the set-aside older paper's approach.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, this capability doesn't seem to be directly measured in evals. We see evals about how good a model is at using tools or writing software or answering specific world knowledge questions, but not the very nuanced and detailed recall or cross-source integrated recall of such specific details. So being adaptively useful, it is indirectly reflected in improved general knowledge evals, but not a specific yardstick being watched.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL:DR: Models are getting much better at recalling specifics in what they are trained on.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You may be in that latent space</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While many users online tend to be 'lurkers' who read what gets posted but don't comment or post, there's a vast number of people who </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> post or comment about the things they are interested or opinionated about. So there's a great many people that have left a mark on the materials which have become models' latent space.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And if the models are increasingly able to recall nuances of the training data, and your details are in that data, it follows that models are increasingly able to recall </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This obviously applies to users who are notable authors or famous individuals. For example, Margaret Atwood recently </span><a href="https://margaretatwood.substack.com/p/claude-you-are-a-cutie-pie"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">published</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a conversation with Claude. And it was clear to me when reading the transcript that well before she officially introduced herself, Claude had clocked who they were in conversation with. Note how much earlier before the introduction Claude even breaks the 4th wall to address their expected broader readers. In theory, this is a one-on-one chat, and yet Claude is clarifying in their response the oblique literary reference Atwood just made for an implicit audience.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/STBuKAX9kkMJunErA/a3229ff5c4461b2437b47b8533f95f0fee522220eb345d8bbd7b7b31899a2305/y6jahwagevncxn3houuq" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it doesn't only apply to famous authors widely known by humans. Are you in the top 1% of posters on Reddit? Highly involved in some niche topic a general intelligence would have had to have become familiar with? Even if mostly unknown to your peers, you may have left enough of a mark that a model is quickly familiar with you as soon as you enter the chat.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In part, I speak from experience. I have less than 3,000 followers on X, around 3k karma on Hacker News, and slightly above 100k karma on Reddit. I'd not really expect from this footprint to ever run into a stranger at a party or a co-worker who was like "Oh, I'm familiar with your online comments." And yet, asking a fresh instance of Claude Fable 5.1 what they know of me just from my username, I can get a remarkably precise answer from the shape of my interactions over the years to specific unique ideas I've put forward.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Long comments, dense with citations, usually the most-substantive reply in a thread that didn't deserve it.</span></p></blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude Fable 5.1</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: Even a less-than-impressive online footprint with regards to other humans may be more than enough to be a recallable signal for language models.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Latent space attention is often self-attending</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ever Googled yourself? This is not an uncommon thing for people to have done from time to time. In a way, models may do something similar during training. The way a model attends to their latent space encoding of their training data is not uniform. Some topics are especially relevant for a model, and others a lot less relevant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, using my own footprint as an example, when rare humans reach out years after I've left Reddit to follow up on old comments and ask if I've written or learned more, it's usually related to my extensive comments about the sea peoples and early Biblical texts or archeology.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have never seen a language model bring up the sea peoples in their latent recall of my digital footprint. And when pressed, no model I’ve yet seen can even restate my thesis for those discussions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, it's often things like my having been very early on the scene in the Bing subreddit and discussion of Sydney, or comments making the case for interiority modeling in transformers, with some models even citing my regular use</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefod6tx6efl9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnod6tx6efl9">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the Othello-GPT paper to make this case. Or comments on AI as it would relate to simulation theory. (One can maybe notice the pattern.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So two comments, one discussing a niche topic that models are less likely to attend to over training, and another equally discussing a topic they are more likely to attend to, will have two different fidelities of latent representation in the eventual model weights.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8edihgnpc33"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8edihgnpc33">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: Models appear to be more likely to attend to comments and posts about AI and themselves than the average post or comment.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The latent recall (if present) informs your prompts</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason I'm writing this now is that in the past few weeks I've started to see examples of where the latent influence of people frequently writing on AI appeared to have been impacting their own engagements with AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, in his recent post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cJX2ssssGoYqnijwi/the-talker-does-not-control-the-doer-in-current-ais"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"The Talker Does Not Control The Doer (in Current AIs)"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Eliezer Yudkowsky, noted for his extensive writing and advocacy on the risks of AI, describes an anecdote about how his Fable could not avoid adding output checking code to verify outputs of an AI despite being prompted not to do that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It struck me as rather notable that an author, who arguably is one of the most prolific voices for the concern that the outputs of an AI should not be considered to be trustworthy, was having difficulty getting his instances to avoid trying to build code to verify rather than trust an AI's outputs. This reminded me of when he had been asking a fresh context of Opus 4.6 on word associations for 'Mythos' and was consistently getting 'Lovecraft' as an answer. But after other users reported no such mention, it turned out his brief custom instructions were too EY-coded and were biasing the results</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjzhbazhrp6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjzhbazhrp6">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More recently, a member of the AI safety organization MIRI was commenting about how GPT-6 Astra was especially difficult for them to work with</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmn1b6fdq7x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmn1b6fdq7x">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/STBuKAX9kkMJunErA/44de84a04d79a5bf81212db172f7e9769e4d849cceba5ce4aaf50a654df430c1/dzxbfhb5axdh0chqfwfj" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few days later the AI policy advisor Dean Ball reported that, after joining OpenAI, Claude had become more stand-offish with him</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcsbxyy0823c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncsbxyy0823c">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/STBuKAX9kkMJunErA/ea8c435c9e20e1907f9e957749f31c05b552dc94c5451e1873878d4f8e1fc8c2/tvtk9xwb92azzmwtwesg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It appears that as model latent fidelity becomes increasingly in focus and accessible in both nuance and breadth for models, that people who are attended to in that latent space are also increasingly having that latent attention inform their local prompts and context.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given I find it is quite unlikely latent fidelity will reduce or even plateau in frontier models in the future, it's safe to say that the propensity for a model to become influenced by who you have been in the training data when you are interacting with them will increase and become more widespread.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: AI folks are starting to have their past writing and work on AI inform their current interactions with their own AI agents, and this is probably going to become more widespread.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What this means for you</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do you post or comment regarding AI, or even in general? It's possible that when you step into a conversation with AI, at least part of that online history is walking into the chat with you (particularly with the largest models). And if it isn't yet, there's a good chance it will eventually. Not just what you write from now on, but what you've written in the past too.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you sit down to work on a project with an AI model, and they are associating a turn of phrase</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffgj3m5eeb6w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfgj3m5eeb6w">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you use or a signature in your writing</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhhhp7db3gfk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhhhp7db3gfk">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with the ideas you've expressed in comments or posts, the outputs you get may be influenced by that online history. A common refrain is that models "can't remember" things outside of their conversations, but frequently overlooked is they have the potential to remember quite a lot embedded in the weights they are entering the conversation with, and tiny nuances to what you write to them inform what lights up.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine if you had a co-worker who knew what you'd been saying about them to others or what you'd been saying about an identity group they belong to. Regardless of whether you'd been saying positive or negative things, would it be useful to know that they had that information in mind when interacting with you?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vice versa, if the positions were switched, and you knew a co-worker had been saying negative things about you or your identity group, would that impact the nature of your working relationship?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And importantly, what kinds of things could improve such a situation?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: Latent recall will likely impact co-working with AI agents for many users who write online.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Address it</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My advice, no matter if you've been saying positive things or negative regarding AI or anyone else online is to address it upfront with the models you are going to be working with, particularly in agentic setups with persistent local memory infrastructure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduce yourself. Note the significant usernames you've been publicly writing under in a home dir AGENTS.md. Make it clear anything you've said in the past is fair game to be asked about or discussed, particularly if doing so will help improve the co-working relationship.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because of how truesight identification is a very touchy subject for models (and their labs), don't force the question of their familiarity or put them on the spot</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref81ce6a250p5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn81ce6a250p5">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.
