### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Integrating Netflix’s Foundation Model into Personalization applications](https://netflixtechblog.com/integrating-netflixs-foundation-model-into-personalization-applications-cf176b5860eb?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>Authors: <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/divya-gadde-3ba01551/">Divya Gadde</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/markhsiao/">Ko-Jen Hsiao</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/dhaval-patel-1a23aa8/">Dhaval Patel</a> and <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/moumitab/">Moumita Bhattacharya</a></p><p>The motivation for building the Netflix Foundation Model for personalization was laid out in this <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/foundation-model-for-personalized-recommendation-1a0bd8e02d39">previous blog</a> post. The Netflix homepage is powered by several specialized models and it requires significant time and resources to maintain and continue innovating on these individual models. Therefore, we decided to centralize member preference learning and have one powerful model learned from comprehensive user interaction histories and content data at a large scale and have this model distribute its learnings to other models.</p><p>The previous two blog posts(<a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/foundation-model-for-personalized-recommendation-1a0bd8e02d39">1</a>, <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/fm-intent-predicting-user-session-intent-with-hierarchical-multi-task-learning-94c75e18f4b8">2</a>) focus on the development of the Foundation Model and here we talk about how we integrate the Foundation Model into various applications. We found that there is extensive literature focused on the training and inference of large-scale transformer-based models. However, there remains a significant gap in practical guidance and research on how to effectively and efficiently integrate large models into existing production systems. At Netflix, we experimented with 3 distinct integration approaches — embeddings, subgraph and fine-tuning. The motivation for trying different approaches stems from varying application needs. Specifically, different teams have different latency requirements, tech stack constraints and different appetites for leveraging the full power of a large foundation model.</p><p>In this blog, we go over the 3 approaches we experimented with and which are now used in production for different use cases. Each integration approach has its own set of motivations, tradeoffs, complexities and learnings. The following sections go into the details of each of these integration approaches.</p><h3>Embeddings from Foundation model</h3><p>Transformer style foundation models naturally lend themselves to generating comprehensive user profile and item representations. Our approach for generating embeddings includes getting the hidden state of the last user event as the profile embedding and weights from the item tower as item embeddings.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/960/1*9xBaz1zkvALNwB3iT3xK8g.png" /></figure><p>Netflix has an elaborate set up for refreshing the embeddings every day. First, the Foundation Model is pre-trained from scratch every month. Then, the monthly pre-trained model is fine-tuned everyday based on the latest data. The daily fine-tuning also expands the entity id space to include the newly launching titles. After the daily fine-tuned model is ready, we run batch inference to refresh the profile and item embeddings and publish them to the Embedding Store.</p><p>An important part of this setup is the embedding stabilization technique (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.07574">paper</a>). When the pretraining job retrains the model from scratch, the embedding spaces between different runs are completely different due to the random initialization in the model. In addition, the embeddings start to drift when the daily job fine-tunes on the latest data, despite the daily fine-tuning job warm-starting from the previous day’s model. Therefore, it is important to map the embeddings generated everyday into the same embedding space, so that downstream models can consume pre-computed embeddings as features.</p><p>This embedding approach relies heavily on the Embedding Store, which is a feature store specializing in embeddings built by the platform team at Netflix. The embedding store makes producing and consuming embeddings easy by taking care of versioning and timestamping the embeddings. The Embedding Store also provides various interfaces to access the embeddings offline and online.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/419/1*i7hLvlBy0Pn1XA_x6xIduw.png" /><figcaption>Embedding Store integration</figcaption></figure><p>This approach is often the initial and most straightforward way for applications to interact with the Foundation Model. Embeddings can serve as powerful features for other models or for candidate generation, helping to retrieve appealing titles for a user or facilitate title-to-title recommendations. Integrating embeddings as features into existing pipelines is generally well-supported and offers a low barrier to entry. Compared to other integration approaches, using embeddings from the Embedding Store is expected to have a relatively smaller impact on training and inference costs.</p><p>However, this approach also has some cons. The time gap between embedding computation and downstream model inference can lead to staleness, impacting the freshness of recommendations. This may prevent applications from fully unlocking the benefit of the Foundation Model, especially for use cases requiring real-time adaptability.</p><p>Overall, the default starting point for leveraging the Foundation Model is through its profile and item embeddings via the Embedding Store, provided that the embeddings’ freshness and stability meet application-specific needs. While embeddings may not leverage the full power of the Foundation Model, they offer a good initial step.</p><p>Our biggest learning from working on this approach is that embeddings are a low cost and high leverage way of using the Foundation Model. A resilient embedding generation framework and embedding store are essential for updating the embeddings daily and serving them to application models. We found this infrastructure to be so valuable that we stepped up our investment here to build a near-realtime embedding generation framework. This new framework also enables us to update the embeddings based on user actions during the session, making the embeddings and our downstream models more adaptive. Although the near-real time framework cannot be used for very large models, it represents an important direction for addressing embedding staleness and adaptiveness of recommendations.</p><h3>Foundation Model as a Subgraph</h3><p>This approach involves using the Foundation Model as an input to the downstream model i.e. the Foundation Model decoder stack is a subgraph of the application model’s full model graph and hence the name “subgraph integration”. The Foundation Model subgraph processes raw user interaction sequences and outputs representations that are then fed into the downstream model graph.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/571/1*jmoqPX4Zzp5kNbhzwtfkjQ.png" /><figcaption>Foundation Model Subgraph integration</figcaption></figure><p>Applications can fine-tune the Foundation Model subgraph as part of their own training process, potentially leading to better performance than just using static embeddings. In this approach, there is no time gap or staleness between the Foundation Model inference and the application model’s inference, ensuring that the most up-to-date learnings are used. This approach also allows applications to leverage specific layers from the Foundation Model that may not be exposed through the Embedding Store, uncovering more application-specific value.</p><p>However, the subgraph approach comes with some complexities and trade-offs. To use the Foundation Model as a subgraph, application models need to generate all the features necessary for the subgraph as part of their feature generation process. This can add time, compute and complexity to their feature gen jobs. Merging the Foundation Model as a subgraph also increases the application model size and inference time. To alleviate some of these complexities, the Foundation Model team provides some reusable code and jobs that make the feature generation more compute efficient. To optimize the inference performance, we split the subgraph and ensure it only runs once per profile per request and is shared by all items in the request.</p><p>Overall, the subgraph approach allows for a deeper integration and enables the applications to harness the full power of the Foundation Model. But it can also make the application’s training code more complex, requiring a trade-off between metric improvement, compute cost, and time. Therefore, we would reserve this approach for high impact use cases where the increase in metric wins compensate for the cost and complexity.</p><h3>Fine-tuning Foundation Model and use directly</h3><p>This approach is similar to fine-tuning LLMs with domain specific data. In the previous blog post, we described how the Foundation Model is trained on a next-token prediction objective, with the tokens being different user interactions. Different interactions are of different importance to various surfaces on the Netflix website. For example, for the ‘Trending now’ row, recent interactions on trending titles might be more important than old interactions. Hence, the Foundation Model can be fine-tuned on more product specific data and then the fine-tuned model can be used to directly power the product. During fine-tuning, application teams can choose to do full parameter fine-tuning or freeze some layers. They can also choose to add a different output head with a different objective. We built a fine-tuning framework to make it easy for application teams to develop their own custom fine-tuned versions of the Foundation Model.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/419/1*5ZC7Tp-8haeW9m8sq8BO7Q.png" /><figcaption>Fine-tuned Foundation Model Integration</figcaption></figure><p>This approach offers the ability to adapt the Foundation Model to application’s specific data and objectives, making it optimized for their use case. A side benefit of this integration approach is that it provides a de facto baseline for new models/applications. Instead of coming up with a new model stack and spending months on feature engineering, new applications can directly utilize fine-tuned foundation models. However, this approach can lead to more models and pipelines to maintain across the organization.The latency and Service Level Agreements (SLAs) of the fine-tuned model must be carefully optimized for the specific application use case.</p><h3>Conclusion</h3><p>We are continuing to innovate on these integration approaches over time. For example, the Machine Learning Platform team is working on near-real time embedding inference capabilities to address the staleness issues with the embedding approach. We are also working on a smaller distilled version of the Foundation Model to reduce the inference latency of the subgraph approach. We also provide a fine-tuning framework to make it easy to fine-tune and leverage the Foundation Model. We also continue to refine and standardize the APIs used in these approaches to make it easy for application teams to use them. Overall, these three integration patterns provide a comprehensive framework for how Netflix applications can interact with and extract value from the Foundation Model.</p><h3>Acknowledgements</h3><p>We thank our stunning colleagues in the Foundation Model team for developing and continuing to innovate on the Foundation Model. We thank our colleagues in the AIMS applications teams for driving the integrations and their valuable feedback and discussions. We also thank our Machine Learning Platform team for building and supporting the infra components to get these approaches up and running in production.</p><p>Contributors to this work (name in alphabetical order): <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/davis-shepherd-919838132/">Davis Shepherd</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/inbar-naor-6b973a50/">Inbar Naor</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jinwangw/">Jin Wang</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jonathanindig/">Jonathan Indig</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kzielnicki/">Kevin Zielnicki </a><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/lequn-luke-wang-9226b2129/">Luke Wang</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/mario-garcia-armas/">Mario Garcia-Armas</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/maryam-e-2077a127/">Maryam Esmaeli</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/tuzhucheng/">Michael Tu</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/nicolas-hortiguera/">Nicholas Hortiguera</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/qiuling-xu-a445b815a/">Qiuling Xu</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/robert-gruener-6762396b/">Robbie Greuner </a><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rogermenezes/">Roger Menezes</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sejoon-oh/">Sejoon Oh</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/trungnguyen324/">Trung Nguyen</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/vito-ostuni-0b576027/">Vito Ostuni</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yesufeng/">Yesu Feng</a>.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=cf176b5860eb" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/integrating-netflixs-foundation-model-into-personalization-applications-cf176b5860eb">Integrating Netflix’s Foundation Model into Personalization applications</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [DataJunction as Netflix’s answer to the missing piece of the modern data stack](https://netflixtechblog.com/datajunction-as-netflixs-answer-to-the-missing-piece-of-the-modern-data-stack-92af926b40a5?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>by <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/agorajek/">Olek Gorajek</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sredai/">Samuel Redai</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/shyiann/">Yian Shang</a></p><h3>Intro</h3><p>Our Netflix journey from data to dashboards, particularly within the space of the experimentation platform, always presented a unique set of challenges. This blog post aims to illuminate these hurdles and explain how an innovative open-source metric platform became instrumental in overcoming some of them. We’ll showcase the critical role that DataJunction played in streamlining our data workflows and enhancing our ability to derive meaningful insights from our vast datasets.</p><p>Metrics are the foundation of data-driven decision-making, but at many organizations, they are scattered, inconsistently defined, and difficult to discover or trust. At Netflix, this challenge first became apparent in our experimentation platform, where reliable metrics are essential for interpreting test results. But the complexity of the original tooling made it very difficult to understand and introspect these metrics both in terms of their definitions as well as their values.</p><h4>Initial Problem</h4><p>Scattered metric definitions, redundant datasets, and pipelines are often a natural effect of a fast-moving company with a large and powerful analytical workforce. This is a common problem in big data-driven firms, and it is not easy to solve across multiple departments, which are often governed by somewhat different rules and regulations. An easy-to-use, well-governed, and centralized semantic layer can help with these issues. Our initial problem was most evident in our Experimentation Platform team, where the previous system required:</p><ul><li>extensive business knowledge</li><li>familiarity with experimentation conventions</li><li>and complex engineering practices</li></ul><p>This led to metric authoring processes that would take weeks, which was a prohibitive requirement for Data Scientists and Analytics Engineers who wanted to onboard new metrics to the Experimentation Platform.</p><h4>Enter DataJunction</h4><p>To address this problem and to set ourselves on the path to a successful semantic layer solution in the future, we looked at several candidates. Among other solutions we evaluated Mando (an internal framework), Minerva (an open source semantic layer from AirBnB), and DataJunction (another open source solution, “DJ” for short). All of them had some strength, but DJ offered one thing that the others did not at the time: a solid SQL parsing and SQL generation engine. At the time of the POC, DJ was also the only open-source solution that stored all its definitions in a connected graph, similar to how relational databases store metadata on their views and queries. Easy “SQL wrangling” with rich metadata was the main feature that convinced us to try it for our immediate use case.</p><p>DataJunction originated from a similar internal Facebook project created (around 2015) by the data science and infrastructure (DSI) team, mainly as an answer to the need for metrics portability. During several iterations of the Deltoid project — which was the main experimentation platform at Facebook at the time — users were required to rebuild and repopulate their metrics when they wanted to move to the next version of Deltoid. But after implementing DJ and abstracting all metric definitions the team offered seamless migrations by separating the concerns between the metrics and the stats computation platform. That project was subsequently renamed to MDF (Metrics and Dimensions Factory) and promoted as a generic semantic layer used widely by various departments. In 2021, one of the Facebook engineers (<a href="https://github.com/betodealmeida">Beto Dealmeida</a>), started the <a href="https://github.com/DataJunction/dj">DJ Github project</a> and built out some basic functionality. Then together with some folks at Netflix expanded the scope further, and soon after that the project was introduced at Netflix. Following that introduction, three Netflix engineers (authors of this blog) together with a couple of external contributors, most notably <a href="https://github.com/CircArgs">Nick Ouellet</a>, have been building out the remaining features of the platform.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*dcANBgrIzPINy2sZeZNXMQ.png" /></figure><h3>Architecture Decisions</h3><p>A critical design constraint for building DJ at Netflix was resource scarcity. We refused to compromise on product quality, choosing instead to limit the feature set. Although DJ was clearly capable of addressing many simultaneous problems across diverse use cases, our lack of adequate resources and official support meant we developed it as a side project for over two years. Our hope was that demonstrating its value would eventually secure management attention.</p><h4>API First, UI and Other Interfaces Later</h4><p>A metrics platform doesn’t need a flashy front-end to deliver value — its core is the semantic layer, which stores a consistent set of dimensions and metrics and serves this information through a rich API service. You can see below how we structured the main components of DataJunction in the Netflix implementation. Our DJ services (in the middle) interact with the underlying data (on the left) and supply our access layer with the business definitions in the form of SQL w/ metadata or directly with data depending on the client request.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/512/1*yp7ewmMUnuEqKquBkCADTg.png" /></figure><h4>Consistent Graph First, Other Features Later</h4><p>A complete semantic layer system can seem like an octopus, with its tentacles reaching out into many functional areas and can expand from a basic semantic metadata stored for some datasets to full dashboard functionality. Let’s call out some of them:</p><ul><li>semantic metadata authoring and management and monitoring</li><li>metric and dimension query / definition generation</li><li>metric and dimension data generation (requires a query engine)</li><li>data visualization and data tracking</li><li>performance optimization (aka caching or materialization)</li><li>integration layer with other tools and</li><li>any / all the above functionality served as: UI, API, client libraries, MCP servers and more.</li></ul><p>Of course it is extremely hard to provide the best in class functionality across all these layers, so with our small “unofficial” team we decided to focus on the most important piece: solid semantic layer engine (and API) with the ability to parse SQL as the input to the graph and generate SQL as the serving layer. That is the core of the system and everything else can be added later with less effort and w/o the need to rebuild the core.</p><h4>Cube as The Perfect Consumption Protocol</h4><p>Analytical multi-dimensional cubes have been a fundamental concept for decades, with their representation integrated across various data processing and analysis platforms. While definitions and features may vary between tools, they all serve the common purpose of encapsulating, or abstracting, a subset of semantic objects.</p><p>In DataJunction, we adopted cubes as first-class features for two main reasons: their familiarity to users and their suitability as a serving interface. They efficiently deliver both the semantic definitions (of metrics and dimensions) and fast data access. See an example of data “flow” graph (dotted lines) and dimensional relationships graph (solid lines) below.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*w9KMcJqSumBk_bimonH-8Q.png" /></figure><p>We simplified the creation of basic cubes, allowing them to easily aggregate arbitrary collections of shared metrics and dimensions. Furthermore, accessing, managing, and visualizing these cubes is straightforward for any client integrating with DJ. Custom integrations, such as for Netflix’s internal fast Druid data analysis tool (Data Explorer → see below), became trivial to build, and we also made it easy to materialize these cubes directly in our Druid clusters.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*FtAGCA-qrTHJ-W25E6hdpw.png" /></figure><p>One of DJ’s most powerful client integrations is the ability to link any DJ-originating metric definition back to the DJ ecosystem. This creates a valuable feedback loop: troubleshooting and introspecting business dashboards becomes simple if they display DJ-defined metrics that include links back to the definition source. This link then makes inspecting and running ad-hoc analysis, by modifying existing metrics, a trivial process. This integration is not only possible with our inhouse tools, but also with other well known open-source products, like Superset.</p><h3>Experimentation at Netflix</h3><p>Metrics are the backbone of experimentation. They translate data into signals that tell us whether a change is meaningful, safe, and worth scaling. Without trustworthy metrics and dimensions, experiments risk becoming inconclusive or misleading, so a robust metrics layer becomes crucial for data-driven decisions.</p><p>At Netflix, the original metrics layer integrated into the experimentation platform was overly complicated. Creating new metrics not only required tribal knowledge of experimentation data artifacts, but also familiarity with complex engineering workflows. This unnecessary friction meant that onboarding a single new metric could take weeks, slowing adoption of new metrics and hindering experimentation.</p><p>This is where DataJunction comes in: it provides a central hub for defining metrics and dimensions that can easily be shared and explored via the semantic graph. It also provides flexible, user-friendly interfaces (including a UI, API, and various clients), making it easy for users to author and manage their metrics and other semantic models.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*YrHR2wUvy6ZOn2VHya2oNQ.png" /></figure><h3>Conclusions</h3><p>With experimentation domain well supported by DataJunction, we would like to take on next steps to unifying of the general analytics at Netflix and to make it easier for Netflix users to both:</p><ul><li>find and use metric definitions across verticals (defined in DJ)</li><li>easily access, modify and experiment with new metrics across Netflix data stack</li></ul><p>That will require building out strong integrations between DJ and other tools, but that has been underway and it is one of the most enjoyable parts of this project, where most of the feature exists and we are simply enabling existing systems to exchange information in a structured and reliable way. With a system like DataJunction our internal LLMs can deliver accountable information about the business metrics as well as explanation of how they are defined and used across Netflix.</p><p>Related resources:</p><ul><li>2023 : DataJunction at Netflix (<a href="https://github.com/DataJunction/dj/blob/main/talks/2023-03-02/DJ%40Netflix%20-%20A%20DJ%20Meetup.pdf">slide deck</a>)</li><li>2025 : DataJunction talk at Data Engineering Open Forum 2025 (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t9XEJnEyz3I">video</a>)</li><li>2025 : DataJunction: Unifying Experimentation and Analytics (<a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/part-1-a-survey-of-analytics-engineering-work-at-netflix-d761cfd551ee">blog</a>)</li></ul><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=92af926b40a5" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/datajunction-as-netflixs-answer-to-the-missing-piece-of-the-modern-data-stack-92af926b40a5">DataJunction as Netflix’s answer to the missing piece of the modern data stack</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [Measuring the Impact of Personalized Recommendations](https://netflixtechblog.com/measuring-the-impact-of-personalized-recommendations-4c26be3a4d96?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p><em>By Kevin Zielnicki, Guy Aridor, Aurélien Bibaut, Allen Tran, Winston Chou, and Nathan Kallus</em></p><h3>Introduction</h3><p>Personalization is everywhere on the internet. It shapes the products we discover, the stories we read, the music we hear, and the movies and TV shows we watch. At Netflix, personalization has been central to the member experience for decades — from the Netflix Prize to our modern recommender system that helps members navigate a large and constantly changing catalog.</p><p>But personalization is not only an algorithmic problem, it is also a measurement problem. How do we know how much value personalization creates? More precisely, how do we separate the appeal of a title from the value created by recommending that title to the right member at the right time?</p><p>In a <a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0167718726000561">new article</a> in the <em>International Journal of Industrial Organization</em> — which we are also presenting at this year’s <a href="https://ec26.sigecom.org/"><em>ACM Conference on Economics &amp; Computation</em></a><em> (EC ‘26) </em>— we develop a model of member behavior, title choice, and personalized recommendations. The model lets us estimate how recommendations affect both whether members choose to watch and, if so, which titles they choose. In this post, we summarize our approach and main findings.</p><h3>The Measurement Challenge</h3><p>A natural first attempt at valuing recommendations is to ask: what share of viewing comes from titles that were recommended? This is an intuitive metric, but it fails to answer the causal question at hand.</p><p>The problem is that recommendations are not randomly assigned. A title appears on a member’s homepage precisely because the recommender system predicts that the member might want to watch it. If a member watches that title, was it because the recommendation <em>caused</em> the member to watch it? Or would she have watched it anyway because of her underlying preference for that kind of title? This is the core measurement challenge: recommendations are targeted to demand, so viewing a recommended title combines the effect of personalization with the member’s underlying preferences.</p><p>One way to solve this would be to run a large experiment that replaces Netflix’s personalized experience with an unpersonalized one. That would create clean variation — but it would also be a poor member experience. Personalization is a central part of how members locate valuable titles in a large catalog, so turning it off broadly is not an acceptable measurement strategy.</p><p>Instead, we take a hybrid approach. We build a behavioral model that separates members’ underlying preferences from the incremental effect of recommendations. The model uses the natural variation in the recommendation system, for example due to algorithmic exploration, to estimate how recommendations affect choice. Then, to validate the model predictions about title substitutability, we use small-scale A/B tests that “nudge” the salience of a handful of titles exogenously, generating ground-truth substitution patterns for a subset of titles. This lets us combine the scale and richness of modeling with the credibility of experimental evidence.</p><h3>A Model of Member Choice</h3><p>To measure the value of personalization, we need a model of what members would watch under different recommendation systems. Our model treats Netflix viewing as a day-by-day choice process. On each day, a member chooses among the titles available in the catalog, or she chooses not to watch anything. This gives us a concrete target to estimate: the probability that a member watches each title on a given day, as a function of their preferences, their viewing history, and the recommendations they see.</p><p>A simple version of this model would be too restrictive. It might assume, for example, that removing one title from the catalog shifts viewing toward all other titles in roughly proportional ways. But that isn’t how member choice works. If a member is considering a romantic comedy, the closest substitutes are likely other romantic comedies, not every title on Netflix equally. For similar reasons, two members may react very differently to the same recommendation.</p><p>To capture this complexity, we use a <em>low-rank choice model</em>. The model learns a representation of each title and a corresponding representation of each member’s current preferences. These representations are not limited to manually specified features like genre, cast, or language. Instead, they are learned from viewing behavior itself, allowing the model to capture nuanced similarities between titles that may be difficult to describe with coarse features, such as genre or release date, alone.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*lWLZiuDQ7WWaPsdEHZoNnw.png" /><figcaption>Our Model Attributes Choices to Dynamic Member-Title Affinity and the Effect of Recommendations</figcaption></figure><p>Member preferences are dynamic. What someone wants to watch today depends on what they have watched recently. A member who just finished a crime thriller may be in a different preference state than the same member after watching a stand-up special or a kids’ movie. Rather than estimate a fixed preference vector for each member, the model summarizes each member’s past viewing history into a current preference state. This lets us capture heterogeneous and time-varying tastes while also scaling to millions of members.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*oB9yt-UxQag3NO7vBhugJQ.png" /><figcaption>Our Model Architecture Allows Us to Scale to Millions of Members Efficiently</figcaption></figure><p>The key component for our purposes is that the model explicitly parameterizes the effect of recommendations. When a title is recommended, the model gives it an additional recommendation effect, or “bonus,” that increases its probability of being chosen. This bonus is a reduced-form way to capture two related mechanisms. First, recommendations can create awareness by helping members discover titles they may not have known about. Second, recommendations can provide information by signaling that a title is likely to be a good match.</p><p>Once estimated, the model becomes an engine for predicting counterfactuals. We can ask what would happen if a title were not recommended, if a different set of titles were recommended, or if the current recommender system were replaced with simpler alternatives. Because the model separately represents member preferences, title appeal, and recommendation effects, it lets us estimate not only how recommendations affect total viewing, but also how they change which titles are watched.</p><p>The model is only useful, however, if it captures real substitution patterns. The next step is therefore validation: comparing the model’s predictions to randomized experiments that directly perturb, on a small scale, the visibility that each title receives.</p><h3>Validating the Model with Randomized Experiments</h3><p>The model relies on an important assumption. After conditioning on the member, title, and state information available to the recommender system, we treat the remaining variation in whether a title is recommended as if it were randomly assigned. In our setting, this assumption is reasonable: we observe all of the information available to the recommender system, so we should be able to model its behavior accurately. But it is still an assumption. A useful model should not only fit historical viewing patterns; it should also predict what happens when recommendations change for external reasons.</p><p>Thus, to validate the model, we used randomized experiments that introduce variation in title visibility exogenously. These experiments mirror the kinds of variation that occur routinely when recommendation algorithms are updated: some titles receive more impressions, while others receive fewer. The key difference is that, in the validation experiment, this variation was prespecified so that we could estimate clean experimental benchmarks.</p><p>Specifically, we randomly assigned members to treatment arms that slightly nudged recommendations towards and away from different categories of titles. This let us measure diversion ratios: when a set of titles becomes less visible, how much viewing shifts to other titles, and how much shifts out of Netflix viewing altogether?</p><p>We then asked whether the model could reproduce those experimentally measured substitution patterns. We estimated the model on the control group, simulated the same recommendation changes, and compared the model-implied diversion ratios to the experimental ones. The agreement was strong: the model’s predicted diversion ratios had a correlation of 0.86 with the experimental estimates and an R-squared of 0.73.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*uTILkxkmtnndgCJev_zkQA.png" /><figcaption>Our Model Predicts Out-of-Sample Experimental Diversion Ratios Well</figcaption></figure><p>This validation gives us confidence that the model captures counterfactual substitution patterns accurately. Subsequently, we ask what would happen under more dramatic changes, such as different recommender systems.</p><h3>Counterfactuals: Aggregate Consumption and Diversity</h3><p>With a validated model in hand, we can move from measurement to counterfactuals. Instead of asking only what happened under the current Netflix experience, we can ask what would have happened if members had seen recommendations generated by a different system.</p><p>We compare the current recommender system to three baselines:</p><ol><li>The first is <em>random recommendations</em>: every member receives titles selected uniformly at random from the catalog. This is not intended as a realistic product experience, but it helps isolate the value of having recommendations that are relevant.</li><li>The second is <em>popularity-based recommendations</em>: every member receives broadly popular titles. This is a natural non-personalized benchmark.</li><li>The third is a basic <em>matrix factorization</em> model, a classic personalized recommendation approach and a useful baseline for older generations of recommender systems.</li></ol><p>In each counterfactual, we replace the recommended titles while keeping the rest of the choice environment fixed. This lets us estimate how much viewing would change if the recommendation policy changed, holding constant the catalog, members, and model of member choice.</p><p>The results show that the current recommender system generates substantially more engagement than each benchmark. Relative to the current system, random recommendations reduce engagement by 16%, popularity-based recommendations reduce engagement by 12%, and matrix factorization reduces engagement by 4%. These are large differences at Netflix scale. Even the comparison to matrix factorization — a personalized baseline — suggests that continued improvements in personalization translate into meaningful gains in how effectively members find titles they want to watch.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*HHRJPxHxvEKnIkyhEW1JnQ.png" /><figcaption>Personalized Recommendations Increase Engagement and its Diversity</figcaption></figure><p>Engagement is only part of the story. A recommender system could increase viewing while concentrating attention on a narrow set of already-popular titles. That would be a worse experience for members whose tastes are not well represented by those titles, and it would make poorer use of a broad catalog.</p><p>We therefore also examine consumption diversity: how broadly viewing is distributed across titles. The current system performs well on this dimension too. Popularity-based and matrix-factorization recommendations both substantially increase concentration, meaning that fewer titles receive meaningful viewership. Random recommendations produce slightly more diversity, as expected, but at a large cost to engagement.</p><p>Taken together, these counterfactuals show that the value of personalization is not just that members watch more. It is also that members are matched to a broader set of titles. The current recommender system increases engagement while preserving a wide distribution of viewing, reflecting the diversity of member tastes across the Netflix catalog.</p><h3>Decomposing the Impact on Titles</h3><p>The aggregate counterfactuals show that personalization increases engagement while maintaining broad consumption across the catalog. We can also use the model to ask a more granular question: why does a recommended title get watched?</p><p>There are three conceptually distinct channels:</p><ol><li>The first is <em>exposure</em>: simply showing a title to a member can increase the probability that the member watches it. This captures the basic value of surfacing a title that may otherwise have been hard to find.</li><li>The second is <em>selection</em>: recommended titles are shown to members who already have a relatively high baseline probability of watching them. This channel doesn’t lead to incremental engagement, but it can smooth the discovery experience and help users feel that the recommender system understands their preferences.</li><li>The third is <em>targeting</em>: the recommender system may identify members for whom the recommendation itself is especially effective, meaning members who are much more likely to watch the title when it is recommended than when it is not.</li></ol><p>The distinction matters because not all recommendation-driven viewing should be interpreted the same way. If recommendations worked only through exposure, then any reasonably visible surface could generate much of the same value. If they worked mostly through selection, then recommendations would mainly be finding members who were already likely to watch a title. Targeting is different: it captures whether the system is placing titles in front of members for whom the recommendation changes the decision.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*WW5lIlFaHbW35BcjS1zaPQ.png" /><figcaption>Decomposing the Impact of Recommendations into Selection, Exposure, and Targeting</figcaption></figure><p>Our model lets us estimate these channels title by title. For each title, we can compare the predicted probability that a targeted member watches it when recommended to the probability that an average member watches it when not recommended. We then decompose that difference into selection, exposure, and targeting.</p><p>We first show that exposure is meaningful: showing a title to an average member roughly triples its consumption probability relative to the baseline. Exposure is not the main driver of differences in consumption, as mechanical exposure explains only about 7% of the gap under the current recommender system, while selection and targeting account for the vast majority of the difference and the targeting effect is nearly seven times larger than the exposure effect.</p><p>This pattern is especially informative when compared with simpler recommendation systems. Random recommendations are dominated by exposure because they do not personalize: differences in consumption mostly reflect differences in what titles are shown. Matrix factorization changes this by making recommendations personal, reducing the role of exposure and leaving exposure, selection, and targeting to contribute nearly evenly to consumption differences. The current recommender system goes further: its gains come less from mechanical exposure, but more from targeting the right titles to the right viewers.</p><p>Targeting is also not uniform across the catalog. It is strongest for mid-popularity titles: titles with enough potential audience to be broadly relevant, but not so much mass appeal that everyone already knows about them. Very popular titles need less help from targeting, while very niche titles may have too small an audience to target effectively. The largest gains come from the middle of the catalog, where personalization helps connect specific titles with specific member tastes.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*m_HbeqimgsdkEvyOGZE97A.png" /><figcaption>Personalized Recommendations Give the Strongest Boost to Medium-Popularity Titles</figcaption></figure><h3>Conclusion</h3><p>Personalized recommendations are valuable because they help members navigate a large catalog and find titles they want to watch. They are also valuable to Netflix because they make the service more useful, more engaging, and better able to serve a wide range of tastes.</p><p>But measuring that value is not straightforward. Recommendations are targeted by design, so we cannot simply compare recommended and non-recommended viewing. At the same time, turning off personalization at scale would create a worse member experience.</p><p>Our approach combines a large-scale model of member choice with true experimental validation. The model lets us separate underlying title preferences from the incremental effect of recommendations, and the experiments verify that the model captures real substitution patterns.</p><p>Using this framework, we find that personalization affects both how much members watch and which titles they watch. The value of recommendations comes not just from exposure, but from matching the right titles to the right members.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=4c26be3a4d96" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/measuring-the-impact-of-personalized-recommendations-4c26be3a4d96">Measuring the Impact of Personalized Recommendations</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [TimeSeries Tiered Storage Journey: Kafka/Flink Streams to Native Cassandra Cold Reads](https://netflixtechblog.com/timeseries-tiered-storage-journey-kafka-flink-streams-to-native-cassandra-cold-reads-e59d597c9d60?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>By <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/oleksii-tkachuk-98b47375/">Oleksii Tkachuk</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rajiv-shringi/">Rajiv Shringi</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/clohfink/">Chris Lohfink</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/abe-ratnofsky/">Abe Ratnofsky</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kartik894/">Kartik Sathyanarayanan</a></p><h3>Introduction</h3><p>In an earlier <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/introducing-netflix-timeseries-data-abstraction-layer-31552f6326f8">post</a>, we introduced the TimeSeries Abstraction, our high-throughput, low-latency platform designed to store and query temporal event data at scale. It has since become the operational backbone for Netflix’s largest datasets, which now span multiple petabytes.</p><p>However, managing such large datasets in <em>hot</em> storage, <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/dynamically-splitting-wide-partitions-in-cassandra-for-time-series-workloads-0eded064f456">tuned for low-millisecond latency access</a>, is not cheap. At Netflix, cost-efficiency is a continuous discipline rather than a temporary initiative. This post shares two solutions that significantly reduce storage costs by continuously moving older, less frequently accessed data into cheaper object storage:</p><ul><li><strong>Solution 1</strong>: Uses <a href="https://kafka.apache.org/">Apache Kafka</a> and <a href="https://flink.apache.org/">Flink</a> to stream data directly to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/serv-s3/">S3</a></li><li><strong>Solution 2</strong>: Leverages <a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/_/index.html">Cassandra</a> backups in S3 and <a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/_/blog/Apache-Cassandra-4.1-Features-Pluggable-Memtable-Implementations.html">pluggable Memtables</a> to read data directly from S3</li></ul><p>We’ll start with the Kafka/Flink-based design, then describe how we evolved to a native Cassandra-based cold tier.</p><h3>Challenges</h3><p>As Netflix’s TimeSeries datasets have expanded, we’ve encountered several compounding challenges:</p><ul><li><strong>Growing retention demands</strong>: Users often push for increasing data retention to serve a broader range of use cases, which in turn increases the storage costs.</li><li><strong>Escalating costs</strong>: Maintaining massive, dense clusters for infrequently accessed historical data is prohibitively expensive.</li><li><strong>Sparse historical access:</strong> Most queries target recent events, but older data must remain readily available for occasional audits and investigations.</li><li><strong>Operational strain</strong>: High storage density on hot nodes complicates scaling, deployment, and cluster stability.</li><li><strong>Latency requirements</strong>: Even historical data sometimes requires sub-second query performance for internal tooling and analysis.</li></ul><p>This creates a clear opportunity: keep the most recent data on fast storage and migrate older, rarely read data to cheaper storage <em>while preserving a consistent API</em>. Done well, this can meet user and operational needs while greatly reducing our infrastructure spend.</p><h3>TimeSeries Abstraction</h3><p>Before we dive into the cold storage solutions, here is a quick recap of how TimeSeries partitions its dataset into multiple tables, each covering a window of time we call <strong>Time Slices</strong>.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*ZL0rDP0Un-DxepYPmFo4aQ.png" /><figcaption>Time Slices and Immutable Windows</figcaption></figure><p>This enables several key optimizations, most notably allowing a table to become immutable once it crosses the write boundary, which lets us safely apply further optimizations. For more details, refer to our previous <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/introducing-netflix-timeseries-data-abstraction-layer-31552f6326f8">post</a>.</p><p>This slice information is critical and is maintained in a dedicated metadata table:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/857/1*awpc-KnTpnKdPMEWPql_wQ.png" /><figcaption>Time Slice Metadata Storage</figcaption></figure><p>Creating new Time Slices and maintaining their metadata is a primary responsibility of the TimeSeries control plane. The TimeSeries data plane uses this information to correctly route the read and write traffic to the provisioned tables.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*gMEWgeg9XIxbyNf642RUTQ.png" /><figcaption>TimeSeries Data and Control Plane</figcaption></figure><p>With that background, let’s look at the different solutions we built for data tiering.</p><h3>Solution 1: Streaming with Apache Kafka and Flink</h3><p>Our first solution followed a familiar streaming pattern using Apache Kafka and Flink, where we streamed data to S3 in realtime while simultaneously persisting it to Cassandra.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*GzdLV6xrVy1ZYm-EL06K6A.png" /><figcaption>Streaming with Apache Kafka and Flink</figcaption></figure><h3>Getting Data Into S3 Efficiently</h3><p>To optimize data streaming to S3, the system hash-partitions Time Series IDs into buckets. This partitions the stream and lets dedicated Flink tasks handle each bucket, ultimately batching and reducing the write throughput to S3. Flink workers batch events into <a href="https://parquet.apache.org/">Parquet</a> files grouped by event time within the hour. These files are great for high-throughput streaming writes, but not for low-latency reads.</p><p>Enter the Compaction job.</p><h3>Compaction Job to Optimize Reads</h3><p>The compaction job runs daily and merges small Parquet files into a single, optimized daily Parquet file per namespace.</p><p>The job further optimizes the Parquet files in the following ways:</p><ul><li><strong>Small Row Groups</strong>: Creates small (4–8 MB) row groups to minimize lookup cost. We can then use the <a href="https://github.com/apache/parquet-java/blob/master/parquet-hadoop/src/main/java/org/apache/parquet/hadoop/ParquetFileReader.java">Parquet Java library</a> to perform range reads of row groups from S3. This is intentionally well below <a href="https://parquet.apache.org/docs/file-format/configurations/">Parquet’s recommended row group floor</a> — smaller groups were better because our reads are dominated by point/range lookups rather than full scans.</li><li><strong>Event Sorting</strong>: Sorts events within a row group by time, eliminating brute-force scans when querying for a time range.</li><li><strong>Bloom Filters</strong>: Caches Bloom filters and file metadata in <a href="https://netflix.github.io/EVCache/">EVCache</a>, allowing the TimeSeries query planner to efficiently skip unnecessary reads from S3.</li></ul><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*4A292YQxQuHs765hHcbINw.png" /><figcaption>Compacted and Sorted Parquet File Layout</figcaption></figure><p>Once the compaction job completes, all compacted files per partition are recorded in the TimeSeries control plane, and the hot storage slices can now be truncated!</p><p>On some of our largest datasets, this job typically takes about 12 hours, prioritizing lower compute costs over execution speed.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*QztJu3-j5JkqrggKfC46Gw.png" /><figcaption>Compaction Burndown Per Day</figcaption></figure><h3>Streaming Cold Tier Read</h3><p>Once compacted files are optimized in cold storage, they are ready to be read. The TimeSeries data plane fans out a given query across both the hot and cold tiers and merges their result. The users still use the same query interface:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*CbAZhy_MBT7ezI2FnLanPA.png" /><figcaption>TimeSeries Tiered Storage Read Path</figcaption></figure><p>The Cold Tier Data Plane uses compaction metadata for aggressive planning and pruning, which dramatically reduces the S3 query fan-out. As a result, retrieving a full day of data requires at most three sequential S3 reads, even in the uncommon case where an ID spans two row groups. This yields a p50 latency of ~150 ms and a p99 latency of ~500 ms per read.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*LhVBIsC7rmxXOO8kUbwUgQ.png" /><figcaption>Cold Tier Read Latency in Seconds</figcaption></figure><p>At the time of writing, the streaming cold tier implementation was processing up to 1,250 reads per second from cold storage globally:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*dLs8v1QhAUtW0Q6-TzylVQ.png" /><figcaption>Cold Tier Read Requests Per Second</figcaption></figure><h3>Why not stop here?</h3><p>The streaming cold tier has delivered real savings, shrinking both Cassandra’s <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ebs/">EBS</a> footprint and the Cassandra backup footprint in S3. But the streaming infrastructure itself is expensive and adds moving pieces that widen the team’s on-call scope:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*F8yLub9ro7xakoOu31x6zw.png" /><figcaption>Additional Infrastructure and Operational Cost</figcaption></figure><p>We wanted to improve this further and asked ourselves:</p><ul><li>Can we avoid streaming data into the object store and the associated data transfer costs?</li><li>Can we reduce the compute and operational overhead of new components?</li></ul><p>That led to a more radical question: <em>Can we read directly from existing Cassandra backups in S3?</em></p><h3>Solution 2: Cassandra Cold Storage for Immutable Data</h3><p>At Netflix, we back up Cassandra data into S3 at regular intervals for disaster recovery. The idea for tiering is simple:</p><p><em>Read directly from these S3 backups instead of paying a separate data transfer cost to S3.</em></p><p>Once TimeSeries marks a hot Time Slice as ready for cold storage, we can customize Cassandra to read directly from its backups, allowing us to delete the on-disk data copy. This would also incur minimal changes to the TimeSeries read path!</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/964/1*a7eZ2CCwL3frcEQgVofMaA.png" /><figcaption>New TimeSeries Read Path</figcaption></figure><p>To make this viable in production, we had to answer a series of “How…?” questions.</p><h3>How Can We Read the Backups Efficiently?</h3><p>We introduced a new compaction strategy in Cassandra, which we call <strong>ReadOnlyCompactionStrategy</strong> (ROCS). Here is how it works:</p><p>Once a time slice crosses the user-configured <em>cold-tier boundary</em>, TimeSeries stops accepting writes for that time slice making it immutable. This immutability unlocks significant optimization opportunities:</p><ul><li><a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/doc/4.0/cassandra/architecture/storage_engine.html#sstables"><strong>SSTable</strong></a><strong> overlap:</strong> Standard strategies (LCS, TWCS) often produce SSTables that overlap in the token space, and so a single partition key may exist across multiple files.</li><li><strong>Read fan‑out:</strong> Because a read must touch every SSTable covering the key’s token range, this overlap turns a single point-read into multiple lookups, increasing latency across all tiers.</li></ul><p>ROCS re-compacts all SSTables once, aligning them precisely with token ranges:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1000/1*4BN39NYMLHB0ceptr1cYBw.png" /><figcaption>ROCS SSTable Boundaries</figcaption></figure><p>ROCS applies more broadly to all TimeSeries use cases, driving tail latency down on all storage tiers by reducing the SSTable fan-out during read operations:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/925/1*17eFU5KKPlsi0b2Yub3Now.png" /><figcaption>Tail Latency Improvement in Reads after ROCS</figcaption></figure><p>Once the immutable Time Slices have been optimized, it is time to trigger backups.</p><h3>How Would We Leverage Backups?</h3><p>Cassandra backs up every node in the cluster, organizing each backup on S3 as a declarative manifest, along with the SSTables backing the keyspace’s tables:</p><pre> {bucket}/<br />  └── {prefix}/                             ← cluster identity (env_backup/hash_app)<br />      └── {token}/                          ← node identity (ring token)<br />          ├── META_V2/                      ← manifests, one per snapshot<br />          │   ├── {snapshotInstantMs}.json<br />          │   ├── {snapshotInstantMs}.json<br />          │   └── ...<br />          └── SST_V2/                       ← actual SSTable files<br />              └── {backupTimestamp}/        ← snapshot timestamp<br />                  └── {keyspace}/<br />                      └── {table-with-uuid}/ <br />                          └── {compression}/<br />                              └── {encryption}/<br />                                  ├── {component-file}<br />                                  └── ...</pre><p>The <strong>backup manifest</strong> orchestrates the backup, listing the paths to all its SSTables:</p><pre>manifest.json<br />  ├── info<br />  │   ├── appName              &quot;cass-cluster&quot;<br />  │   ├── region               &quot;us-east-1&quot;<br />  │   ├── snapshotInstantMs    1748908800000<br />  │   ├── backupPathPrefix     &quot;prod_backup/1234_cass/token&quot;<br />  └── data[]<br />      └── [0]<br />          ├── keyspaceName     &quot;ks&quot;<br />          ├── columnfamily     &quot;tbl-a1b2...&quot;<br />          └── sstables[]<br />              └── [0]<br />                  └── components[]<br />                      ├── na-1-big-Data.db<br />                      ├── na-1-big-Index.db<br />                      ├── na-1-big-Filter.db<br />                      ├── na-1-big-Statistics.db<br />                      ├── na-1-big-Summary.db<br />                      ├── na-1-big-CompressionInfo.db</pre><p>Alongside the large Data.db file, backups include a compact set of index, metadata, and integrity files. This structure allows us to quickly locate, validate, and restore specific partitions or data ranges <em>without scanning the entire dataset</em>. Furthermore, since the manifest catalogs every S3 path, we can download most of these components directly to the Cassandra node (excluding the Data.db file) where they occupy negligible space. These local components enable efficient query planning <em>before any data is read from S3</em>.</p><h3>How Would Cassandra Read From Backups?</h3><p>Cassandra reads typically pull data from two sources: the <a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/doc/4.0/cassandra/architecture/storage_engine.html#memtables">Memtable</a>, which holds recent unflushed writes, and flushed SSTables on durable storage. As of Cassandra 4.1, we can leverage <a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/_/blog/Apache-Cassandra-4.1-Features-Pluggable-Memtable-Implementations.html">pluggable Memtables</a> to redirect reads from “cold” tables <em>directly to the backup storage in S3.</em></p><pre>-- create table for a given Time Slice<br />CREATE TABLE data_20260616_00_04 (<br />    ...<br />    ...,<br />    PRIMARY KEY ((time_series_id, time_bucket, event_bucket), event_time, event_id, key)<br />) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (event_time DESC, event_id DESC, key DESC)<br />    ...<br />    AND compaction = ...<br />    AND compression = ...<br />    AND memtable = 'backupmemtable:timestamp=1781953200000' ← Custom memtable<br />    ...</pre><p>The timestamp marks the point at which the backup is guaranteed to cover the full table, and the table is guaranteed to be immutable.</p><p>Opening a backup Memtable kicks off a state-machine initialization that pulls the index data from S3 onto the node once:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*w4X2VjET64msgpMcqVY42A.png" /><figcaption>State Machine for Cassandra Backup Memtable Initialization</figcaption></figure><p>This process ensures all SSTable components are downloaded reliably through several resilience mechanisms:</p><ul><li><strong>Retries and backoff:</strong> All S3 operations and file-system cache writes use retries with exponential backoff.</li><li><strong>Resource isolation:</strong> Initialization runs on a dedicated thread pool, isolated from normal Cassandra operations.</li><li><strong>Partial download protection:</strong> If a downloaded component’s size doesn’t match the manifest, it is deleted and re-downloaded.</li><li><strong>DDL change protection:</strong> A <em>.timestamp</em> file marks completed initialization and ties it to the backup timestamp. If the table’s backup timestamp changes, this file is removed and initialization restarts for the new timestamp.</li><li><strong>Read protection:</strong> Memtable initialization is asynchronous; reads to an initializing memtable block for at most half the read RPC timeout.</li></ul><p>Once initialization has pulled every SSTable component (except Data.db) from S3 onto the local disk, the Memtable is ready to serve reads. Here is what the read process looks like:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*Ly-PNbOUhgX8pVo-7J65fA.png" /><figcaption>Reading from Cassandra Backup Memtables</figcaption></figure><p>To minimize latency, the system parallelizes S3 reads across requests, SSTables, and individual chunks. A shared cache deduplicates fetches and prefetches data, so chunks are ready before they are needed.</p><p>We further optimize cluster and table settings to reduce the number of S3 calls:</p><ul><li><strong>Larger compression chunks:</strong> Reduce S3 calls per partition by reading fewer, larger chunks (accepting some over-fetch on small reads).</li><li><strong>Read repair disabled:</strong> Avoid expensive, unnecessary repair traffic against immutable, pre‑repaired backup data.</li><li><strong>Speculative retries disabled:</strong> Prevent S3’s higher latency from triggering redundant, overlapping reads.</li></ul><h3>How Would We Know When It Is Safe To Read From Backups?</h3><p>However, before the Memtable can point to a backup, we must confirm two things across the whole ring, and we use Cassandra <a href="https://cassandra.apache.org/doc/4.1/cassandra/new/virtualtables.html">Virtual Tables</a> to do that.</p><p>First, that ROCS re-compaction succeeded:</p><pre>cqlsh&gt; SELECT * FROM netflix_views.cluster_view                                                                                                                                                          <br />       WHERE keyspace_name = 'system_views' AND table_name = 'sstable_tasks';<br /><br />keyspace_name | table_name    | host     | value                                                                                                                                                        <br />--------------+---------------+----------+------------------------------------------------<br />system_views  | sstable_tasks | 10.0.0.1 | [{&quot;table_name&quot;:&quot;&lt;.&gt;&quot;,&quot;kind&quot;:&quot;compaction&quot;, ...}]<br />system_views  | sstable_tasks | 10.0.0.2 | [] &lt;---- no active compactions<br />system_views  | sstable_tasks | 10.0.0.3 | []</pre><p>And second, that backups were fully captured (denoted by <em>uploaded=True</em>):</p><pre>cqlsh&gt; SELECT * FROM netflix_views.complete_backups                                                                                                                                                      <br />       WHERE keyspace_name = 'my_ks' AND table_name = 'my_table';<br /><br />keyspace_name | table_name | timestamp     | num_tokens | num_uploaded | uploaded                                                                                                                       <br />--------------+------------+---------------+------------+--------------+----------<br />my_ks         | my_table   | 1765211400000 |          3 |            3 |     True<br />my_ks         | my_table   | 1765213200000 |          3 |            2 |    False</pre><h3>How Would We Automate This Entire Flow?</h3><p>The TimeSeries control plane automates the entire transition as slices cross the immutable boundary:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*0qTn_YDtisgdmtTNQji3lA.png" /><figcaption>Automating Time Slice Lifecycle Transitions</figcaption></figure><p>Each slice can be in any lifecycle stage at any time without impacting system availability, and the system remains<em> resilient to delayed backups or lagging compactions.</em></p><h3>How Would Cassandra Handle The Additional Load?</h3><p>Serving data from S3 is much more CPU-intensive than reading from local storage. A sudden spike in read requests can overwhelm the Cassandra cluster and affect the hot path, t<em>urning a cost-saving feature into a reliability risk.</em></p><p>To avoid this, cold reads can optionally run on a<em> separate Cassandra cluster</em> that points its backup Memtables at the <em>hot</em> cluster’s backups:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/984/1*GYe1SzObCPjwgqhPqCSu7w.png" /><figcaption>Isolated Cold Tier Cassandra Topology</figcaption></figure><p>The <em>cold</em> Cassandra cluster must match the original cluster in topology <em>but need not match it in hardware!</em> We can run the cold cluster on much smaller, cheaper, storage-light instances.</p><h3>Real World Performance</h3><p>Cassandra Cold Tier was rolled out to the most storage‑intensive TimeSeries Abstraction clusters. It currently stores over <em>15 PB of compressed data in Cassandra cold storage</em> and has allowed us to cut attached storage significantly on all affected clusters:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*EkE3iB1TDzz2g235OasVkA.png" /><figcaption>Storage Reduction During Rollout: from 80% disk usage to 7%</figcaption></figure><p>At the same time, it serves thousands of reads per second from cold tier storage in any given region:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*RkJHMO39yrqHSCLkeidE7A.png" /><figcaption>Cold Tier Read Requests Per Second</figcaption></figure><p>Cassandra Cold Tier improves p90 latency by ~30% over the previous cold storage implementation, thanks to more efficient S3 access patterns and smaller read sizes, while keeping p99 latency under ~500 ms. Cassandra already handles bursts of cold reads gracefully via its chunk cache, so this design preserves that same resilience for the S3-backed cold tier:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*n16MxzAWIijYOAofXhAepg.png" /><figcaption>Cold Tier Read Latency: blue — p90 latency, brown — p99 latency; latency measured in seconds)</figcaption></figure><p><strong>Acknowledgements</strong>: Special thanks to our stunning colleagues who contributed to the TimeSeries Abstraction’s success and collaborated with us on the cold storage solutions: <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kaidanfullerton/">Kaidan Fullerton</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/matt-lehman-39549719b/">Matt Lehman</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sumanth-pasupuleti/">Sumanth Pasupuleti</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/carlos-jmh/">Carlos Matias Herrero</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/npavlakis/">Nick Pavlakis</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/bryankeller2/">Bryan Keller</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/dichiarafrancesco/">Francesco Di Chiara</a></p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=e59d597c9d60" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/timeseries-tiered-storage-journey-kafka-flink-streams-to-native-cassandra-cold-reads-e59d597c9d60">TimeSeries Tiered Storage Journey: Kafka/Flink Streams to Native Cassandra Cold Reads</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>
