### [Behind the Scenes: Evolving Netflix’s Ads Event Pipeline for Live — Part II](https://netflixtechblog.com/behind-the-scenes-evolving-netflixs-ads-event-pipeline-for-live-part-ii-826ebf9ad9fb?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>By <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yogeshnagarur/">Yogesh Nagarur</a></p><h3>Introduction</h3><p>In <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/behind-the-scenes-building-a-robust-ads-event-processing-pipeline-e4e86caf9249">Part 1 of this series</a>, we shared how Netflix built the Ads Event pipeline and approached it as a system design problem: replacing fragmented pipelines with a centralized collection, enrichment, and a standard data contract. The result was Ads Event Publisher, the system that collects ad telemetry from client devices, <em>enriches</em> it with the context of the ad that was actually served, and publishes a single, unified stream of ad events to every downstream consumer: frequency capping, metrics, billing, measurement partners, and reporting. Any member viewing or account details that move through this pipeline end-to-end are encrypted and handled in accordance with Netflix’s strict privacy and security practices to safeguard member PII.</p><p>If ad serving is the “brain” and ad events are the “heartbeats” that feed it, then enrichment is what gives each heartbeat its meaning, turning a bare “[Somebody] watched an ad to completion” signal into “<em>this</em> campaign, <em>this</em> creative, at <em>this</em> price, for <em>this</em> break.” Part 1 described that enrichment step as a single box in the architecture. This post is about what was inside that box, and why we rebuilt it.</p><p>Part 1 ended with a look at what was on the horizon: ads on live streams, de-duplication, and richer data signals. A single project delivered all three by rebuilding the Ads Event Publisher around stateful stream processing. We replaced a database lookup with a real-time streaming join in Apache Flink, which let the system scale for Live, took a dependency off the critical path, and replaced the engine of a high-traffic pipeline without downtime.</p><p>Let’s dive in.</p><h3>A quick recap: enrichment and the Metadata Registry</h3><p>When an ad is served, the ad server knows everything about it: the campaign, the creative, the price of the impression, the targeting context, and the tracking URLs that measurement partners expect us to fire. The client device, by contrast, knows almost nothing: just an opaque token and <em>when</em> to send each event. Enrichment is the act of reuniting the two.</p><p>In the architecture from Part 1, that reunion happened through the <strong>Ads Metadata Registry</strong>, a Key-Value store:</p><ul><li>When the ad server made a decision, it <strong>wrote the metadata</strong> for that impression into the registry.</li><li>It encoded only reference identifiers into the opaque token returned to the device, keeping the token small.</li><li>Later, when the client fired an event carrying that token, the Ads Event Publisher decoded the token, <strong>looked up the metadata</strong> in the registry, and stitched the two together into an enriched ad event.</li></ul><p>This design served us well. The registry was globally replicated, which gave us a useful property almost for free: an event arriving in <em>any</em> region could find its metadata regardless of where the ad was decided. It future-proofed the token against ever-growing metadata, and it carried us through the first phase of Netflix’s advertising growth.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*LkXsONCex0Q1KvttewSZ1w.png" /><figcaption><em>The ad server writes per-impression metadata to the globally replicated registry on the serving path, and the Ads Event Publisher reads it back to enrich each client event.</em></figcaption></figure><p>But as we looked ahead, especially to ads on live streams, the limits of this design became clear.</p><h3>Why we needed to evolve</h3><p>The forcing function was Live. Netflix’s live slate has grown rapidly, from <a href="https://www.netflix.com/tudum/articles/nfl-games-on-netflix">NFL games</a> to <a href="https://www.netflix.com/tudum/tyson-fury-vs-arslanbek-makhmudov">blockbuster fight nights</a> to the <a href="https://www.netflix.com/tudum/features/fifa-womens-world-cup-2027">FIFA Women’s World Cup 2027</a>, and viewership keeps growing. That traffic looks nothing like Subscription Video on Demand (SVOD): a single event can surge toward tens of millions of concurrent viewers within minutes, then drain just as fast when it ends. Building the ad pipeline for Live meant building for that trajectory.</p><p>To see why the old design could not get there, let’s put some numbers on it. When Netflix streamed the <a href="https://www.netflix.com/tudum/articles/jake-paul-vs-mike-tyson-live-release-date-news">Jake Paul vs. Mike Tyson fight</a> to more than 100 million viewers, a single ad break at that scale would generate an enormous volume of ad events. Serving those ads in the old design would have meant writing metadata for each of them to the Metadata Registry and waiting for acknowledgment before the ads could serve: millions of must-succeed database writes per second on the ad-serving critical path. That pattern created three core problems at Live scale:</p><ol><li><strong>It was a scaling bottleneck.</strong> Every ad and every ad break is a write. Multiply that by tens of millions of concurrent viewers, keep it non-lossy (a dropped write is a dropped ad, and lost revenue), add Live’s spiky on/off pattern, and the write load becomes enormous. But you cannot safely scale the registry up once the event starts. Adding nodes reshuffles data across the cluster to rebalance it, and doing that in the middle of a live event could slow or even fail the very writes you are adding capacity for. That leaves one safe option: pre-scaling for the peak in advance and hoping the forecast is accurate.</li><li><strong>Fewer dependencies on the critical path.</strong> Serving an ad meant a synchronous, must-succeed write to the registry, followed by a wait for its acknowledgment. Every system on the critical path is one more thing that can stop an ad from serving, and the metadata registry had no fallback, so an unhealthy registry meant serving stopped. Taking hard dependencies off that path was the goal, and the registry was the biggest one we could remove.</li><li><strong>It put event freshness at risk.</strong> If writing slowed down under load, or lookups timed out, events spilled into retries and dead-letter queues, and enriched ad events were delivered late. Some systems might be fine, but others, such as frequency capping, cannot. It needs events under a strict SLA to decide whether the next selected ad can run without breaking policies.</li></ol><p>So we asked a simple question: <em>the ad server already knows and logs everything about the ad it served. Does it really need to write that to a database on the hot path? Can we match that against the downstream Kafka stream of client events, which is always there, after the fact?</em></p><p>The write leaves the critical path entirely; no shared datastore outage can stop serving, and enrichment becomes something we scale elastically with Live rather than a cluster we babysit.</p><p>Matching two streams after the fact is a streaming join, and that is where Flink comes in.</p><h3>Why Flink</h3><p>We chose Apache Flink because near-real-time, <strong>stateful stream processing</strong> is exactly what it is built for, and because it has deep, production-tested platform support at Netflix. Just as important, we already knew how to run it. As Part 1 noted, our Ads Sessionizer is a large stateful Flink job, so operating one at scale, tuning state, checkpoints, and recovery under production load, was familiar ground for the team. Enrichment on Flink was built on that experience rather than starting from scratch.</p><h3>The new architecture</h3><p>At its core, the new Ads Event Publisher is built as an Apache Flink streaming job. Each event moves through a handful of stages:</p><ol><li><strong>Normalize.</strong> Client events arrive from several sources, including SVOD, live, playback, and embedded ad breaks, each with a slightly different shape. We first normalize them into a common form.</li><li><strong>Re-route.</strong> Client events and response events may appear in different regions; we inspect and reroute them to the correct job-processing region for a successful join.</li><li><strong>Join.</strong> The heart of the job: we match each client event to the ad-serving response for the same impression, enriching it with the full serving context using a KeyedCoProcessFunction</li><li><strong>Deduplicate.</strong> Revenue-bearing events are de-duplicated, so nothing is counted twice.</li><li><strong>Publish.</strong> We extract measurement beacons for our partners and route the enriched ad event to its downstream consumers.</li></ol><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*jJ5eKCy_hgiUp0-fJLb6FQ.png" /><figcaption><em>The ad server logs responses to Kafka rather than writing to a registry; the Flink job joins responses with client events in real time and routes enriched ad events downstream.</em></figcaption></figure><p>The join is the interesting part. Client events and ad serving responses arrive on separate streams at different times, so the job holds recent responses in memory and matches events against them over a <strong>60-minute window</strong>. Three things can happen:</p><ul><li><strong>The response is already there</strong> when an event arrives → we enrich it on the spot.</li><li><strong>The event arrives first</strong> → we hold it for a configuration duration (currently set to 60 minutes) until the matching response shows up, then enrich it.</li><li><strong>No response arrives within the window</strong> → we mark the event as <em>unmatched</em> for later recovery.</li></ul><p>We built the join as a custom Flink operator (a KeyedCoProcessFunction) rather than a built-in windowed join. We do not need a windowed join ordering and time-window semantics, since downstream consumers handle ordering; we simply match each client event to its response by key. Owning the operator at its lowest API form lets us control the matching state directly, including how long we hold it and what we do with an event that never matches. This allows us to tune retention per stream or persist unmatched events to a durable store later without rewriting the join. Our goal is always to build a strong base to reap the benefits later as we scale.</p><p>To make that concrete, picture a member watching a live game. When the ad server decides which ad to show, it logs its response, and a few seconds later, the member’s device fires a “first quartile” event carrying the opaque token. Both arrive at the job keyed to the same impression, and because the response is already waiting, the event is enriched on the spot with its campaign, creative, price, and tracking URLs, then published downstream in near real-time.</p><p>Why 60 minutes? Almost all events, roughly 99.999% of them, reach the job well before that, so extending the window further would only grow the state store and lengthen checkpoints for a vanishingly small gain. The remaining 0.001% still matters for precise billing and reporting, and we recover those events via a separate path described later on.</p><h3>Cross-region: matching events where their data lives</h3><p>The Metadata Registry handled cross-region enrichment for us because it was built on a globally replicated key-value store: a client event could land in any region and still find its ad’s metadata. Global replication was a feature the platform provided (see <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/introducing-netflixs-key-value-data-abstraction-layer-1ea8a0a11b30">Netflix’s Key-Value Data Abstraction Layer</a>), so serving an ad in one region and enriching its events in another was simply not something we had to think about.</p><p>A streaming join does not come with that convenience, and here the two sides of the join live in different places. The ad serving response stays in the region where the ad was served. The client event, though, is fired by the device, and the device can be anywhere: a member might start an ad on home wifi and switch to mobile data partway through, and that network change can route the event to a different region than the one that served the ad. So a client event is routed to a region that does not have the matching serving response.</p><p>The tempting fix is to stream every region’s serving responses into every other region so that each region can match any client event locally. We rejected it: that would mean every region storing every serving response worldwide, the vast majority of which will never be used in the join, which is wasteful in both cost and compute.</p><p>Instead, we treat this as a routing problem and route the client event rather than replicating the response. We encode the serving region into the ad token, so when a client event arrives in the wrong region, we forward it to the region that owns its response. For example, an ad is served in <em>us-east-2</em>, the member’s device switches from wifi to mobile data mid-ad, and the resulting event arrives in <em>us-east-1</em>. That region reads the token, sees the ad belongs to <em>eu-west-1</em>, and forwards the event back there, where its serving response is waiting. Only the small trickle of genuinely cross-region events pays for the extra hop.</p><h3>Catching the long tail of late-arriving events</h3><p>Sixty minutes covers the vast majority of events, but not all of them. A device may have been offline, an ad paused, or an engagement event delayed by hours. Rather than drop these stragglers, we set them aside and recover them through a companion path: an hourly Spark batch job, orchestrated by <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/maestro-netflixs-workflow-orchestrator-ee13a06f9c78">Maestro</a>, Netflix’s workflow orchestrator, re-pairs each straggler with its serving response from our data warehouse (which has a far longer retention than our in-stream window) and publishes it through the same downstream sinks. This keeps the streaming job’s memory bounded while still catching the long tail.</p><p>This also lets us support event types that arrive well after the 60-minute window, including certain tracking-related events, while keeping the door open to extending the system to additional event types down the line and improving our match rate as needed. We also designed this offline job with reuse in mind: it can replay dead-letter queues and backfill historical data, giving us a single mechanism for both long-tail recovery and operational recovery.</p><h3>What was hard</h3><p>Beyond the architecture itself, three things took real work, and together they are what made the pipeline stable, correct, and safe to switch on.</p><h4>Keeping the core lean.</h4><p>Operating a stateful streaming join at live-event scale took real tuning, and most of our hard-won lessons come back to one idea: the job’s only job is matching events. Anything else we were tempted to bolt on, such as external lookups or incidental side-processing, was better moved off the hot path, or it eventually showed up as instability under load. Getting this right was the difference between a job that comfortably kept pace with live traffic and one that fell behind. It is unglamorous work, and it is most of the work.</p><h4>De-duplicating Events</h4><p>Duplicates are a fact of life here in an event processing pipeline. A client may retry an event, and our upstream delivery is at-least-once so that the same event can arrive more than once. For revenue-bearing events, the ones that feed billing, nothing should be counted twice.</p><p>The solution here is a simple and idempotent key. For each event, we compute a stable ID by hashing a few fields that uniquely identify it, such as the ad ID, the event type, and, for repeatable events, an offset or timestamp. The same underlying event always hashes to the same ID, so if we have seen an ID before, we drop the repeat.</p><p>What changed with Flink is where we remember those IDs. The previous design kept them in an external key-value store and did a check-and-write over the network for every event; Flink lets us keep that state local to the job instead. We key the stream by event ID, which routes every copy of a given event to the same place, and a local state store with a short time-to-live remembers the IDs we have recently seen. Keying by ID also removes the race the external store had to guard against, since a given ID is handled in only one place at a time, with no network hop on the hot path. Multiple improvements here alone.</p><p>We are deliberate about the guarantee: at-least-once delivery with best-effort de-duplication, not exactly-once. In-stream dedup removes the vast majority of duplicates within its window. However, a few can still slip past, for instance, through a cross-region reroute, a late-arriving recovery, or a restart that replays recent events. So we also carry the event ID through to the enriched ad event itself, which lets consumers who need exact counts, such as billing, run their own idempotent de-duplication over their own, longer windows.</p><h4>Production Rollout</h4><p>This was the most challenging part of the project. For a system that feeds billing and frequency capping, “trust me, the new one is correct” is not good enough, so we ran the Flink pipeline alongside the legacy one and made it earn its traffic.</p><p>Both pipelines continuously processed the full event stream, allowing us to compare them in real time: we published their outputs side by side. We ran automated audits that alerted us when the two drifted apart. Those audits earned their keep, surfacing subtle discrepancies we would never have caught from dashboards alone.</p><p>How do we know the enrichment logic between the two systems was equal for the audits to make sense? Early in development, we extracted that logic into a shared module that both the legacy service and the Flink job depend on, so any change to how an event is enriched is applied to both at the same time. A failure in the audits, therefore, pointed to a real difference in the pipeline mechanics.</p><p>Cutover was a separate question from comparison, and this is the part that warrants precision. We could not simply send a random percentage of events to each pipeline, because the same event landing in both would be double-counted downstream. Instead, the split was deterministic: a <em>stable key</em> on each event decided which pipeline was responsible for publishing it, so every event was published exactly once, even though both pipelines saw all of them. Raising the threshold moved events from the legacy pipeline to Flink a slice at a time, and we could dial it back instantly if anything looked off.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*Jkq04O2VHHi13xBaqb_Q3g.png" /><figcaption><em>Both pipelines process the same events behind a gradual traffic dial; an audit compares their outputs until we trust them; then the legacy service and registry are retired.</em></figcaption></figure><p>By the time we fully cut over, the two pipelines agreed on well over 99.99% of events, and we rolled it out region by region rather than all at once. Then came the satisfying part: we turned off the ad server’s writes to the Metadata Registry, and decommissioned the legacy service and the registry itself. The single point of failure on the ad-serving critical path is simply <em>gone</em>. One of the oldest ad serving components at Netflix was <em>deleted</em>.</p><h4>Operating the Flink platform</h4><p>Operating a large stateful streaming job in production is its own job, and we did not build that muscle alone. We leaned heavily on Netflix’s Flink platform team. Early on, the job’s checkpoints were slow and heavy. The first checkpoint in particular took a long time, and we were holding more state in memory than we needed. Working with the platform team, we trimmed the state, reduced checkpoint durations, and validated the gains in load tests. That is what keeps the job from stalling during Live bursts.</p><p>The same team also gave us operational scaffolding we did not have to invest in, including continuous delivery and canary deployments, so we could ship and roll out the Flink job with the same guardrails and confidence as the rest of our production systems. Netflix operates more than 25,000 Flink jobs (see <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=T_Z4KLye0J0">Building a Scalable Flink Platform at Netflix</a>), and that maturity is what we have to build on. We layered on custom canary metrics that catch issues before a release, CI/CD that lets us stagger rollouts, and a deploy flow that keeps the old job running until the new one has detected healthy checkpoints, so we never destroy a working job before the new job gives us the green light.</p><h3>The results</h3><ul><li><strong>Enrichment is off the critical path for ad serving.</strong> The ad server no longer performs a synchronous, must-succeed database write to serve an ad. It logs and moves on, and an entire class of “the lookup store is down, so ad serving is down” failures no longer exists.</li><li><strong>Fewer systems, fewer schemas, fewer hops.</strong> We retired both the legacy service and the Metadata Registry.</li><li><strong>Built for Live, and continuously tested for it.</strong> An ad break is what this service is designed to handle, so during our journey, we also ran large-scale load tests to simulate real-life traffic. In our latest load test, a single AWS region handled one ad break for a large concurrent audience, processing hundreds of thousands of events per second, joining the large majority of them against their serving responses in real time, and outputting enriched ad events at a comparable rate, all with negligible timeouts and errors. We test this throughout development and keep doing so as live audiences grow, and we are not at the ceiling yet.</li><li><strong>We delivered the Part 1 roadmap.</strong> Live-stream ad events, in-stream de-duplication, and richer real-time enrichment all shipped on this foundation.</li></ul><h3>Key takeaways</h3><ul><li><strong>Take the database off the hot path when you can.</strong> Our biggest win wasn’t raw performance — it was blast radius. Trading a synchronous write-and-wait for a fire-and-forget log plus a downstream join removed an entire failure mode from ad serving.</li><li><strong>Match events where their data lives.</strong> Real-time joins beat global replication by routing events to their data rather than copying the data everywhere.</li><li><strong>Earn the cutover.</strong> Running both systems in parallel and diffing their output on live traffic was slower than a flag-day switch — and it’s the only reason we trust the result.</li></ul><h3>What’s next</h3><p>We have an exciting list of projects on the horizon: scaling the live path for the next tier of concurrent audiences, bringing the remaining delayed <em>engagement</em> events fully into the streaming world, reducing the Flink state size we need to manage, working on bringing even more resilience to the eventing processing pipeline, and continuing to add new event types and richer signals for our downstream consumers.</p><p>Re-architecting the engine of a running, revenue-critical pipeline in place was a real multi-team effort. If building real-time systems at this kind of scale sounds like your thing, <a href="https://jobs.netflix.com/">Ads Engineering is hiring</a>.</p><h3>Acknowledgements</h3><p>This was a multi-team effort across Netflix. Thank you to everyone who designed, built, validated, and operated the new pipeline:</p><p><strong>Ads Engineering:</strong> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yogeshnagarur/">Yogesh Nagarur</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kineshsatiya/">Kinesh Satiya</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jeetm35/">Jeet Mehta</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/nsainiusc/">Nitin Saini</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/persisshah/">Persis Shah</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/guangyuc/">Guangyu Chen</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jimmy-liu-a904583a6/">Jimmy Liu</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/riley-berton/">Riley Berton</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/simonspencer1/">Simon Spencer</a>, <a href="http://linkedin.com/in/aadesh-patel-535ab998">Aadesh Patel</a></p><p><strong>Ads Data Engineering:</strong> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/eugene-yusim/">Eugene Yusim</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/brandon-nicklas-b111736a/">Brandon Nicklas</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/indunarayan/">Indu Narayan</a></p><p><strong>Ads SRE:</strong> <a Avalos</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/brianrchristie/">Brian Christie</a></p><p><strong>Data Movement:</strong> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/markcho/">Mark Cho</a>, <a ### [Netflix Conductor: The Next Chapter](https://netflixtechblog.com/netflix-conductor-the-next-chapter-41ad21067649?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <h3>Netflix Conductor : The Next Chapter</h3><p><em>by </em><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/aravindanr"><em>Aravindan Ramkumar</em></a><em> on behalf of the Conductor team</em></p><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/netflix-conductor-a-microservices-orchestrator-2e8d4771bf40">Conductor</a> is the workflow orchestration engine Netflix uses to stitch micro-services into reliable, observable business processes.</p><p>If you’ve followed Conductor from the outside, the last thing you heard was probably the <a href="https://github.com/Netflix/conductor">note on its GitHub repository</a>: Netflix discontinued maintenance of Conductor OSS to refocus its resources on the internal fork.</p><p>That announcement marked the end of the open-source chapter, and from the outside it may have looked like the end of the story. It wasn’t. Internally, Conductor never slowed down. If anything, it grew faster, and the demands of that growth pushed us to rebuild it from the ground up. This post is the answer to the natural question that announcement leaves open: <em>what happened to Conductor after that?</em></p><p>A lot, it turns out. The clearest way to put it is in terms of growth. <em>Not long ago, Conductor crossing a billion workflow executions in a single year felt like a milestone. Today it clears that bar roughly every quarter</em>, and the monthly volume is still climbing. Sustaining that kind of growth meant rethinking nearly every layer of the system. The data plane it runs on was modernized piece by piece. The evaluation engine was rewritten. The way it allocates work, controls concurrency, and runs compute all changes. The through-line is simple: scale forced a rethink of the evaluation engine and the layers beneath it, and everything else followed.</p><p>The rest of this post walks through that evolution: how the architecture changed, the performance and scale work at the heart of it, the leap forward in developer experience, and the newer capabilities for controlling how workflows and tasks execute.</p><h3>How Netflix uses Conductor</h3><p>When we <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/evolution-of-netflix-conductor-16600be36bca">last wrote about Conductor,</a> it ran a few hundred workflow definitions for a few dozen teams. That footprint has grown by orders of magnitude. Conductor now backs roughly <strong>200,000 workflow definitions</strong> owned by around <strong>150 applications</strong>, spanning Content and Studio Engineering, Ads, Games, and more. The runtime side has grown to match. In a typical month it executes on the order of <strong>420 million workflows</strong>, and on a given day it runs anywhere from <strong>5 to 20 million</strong>, with teams adding hundreds of new workflow types every month.</p><h4>Conductor and the Media Production Suite</h4><p>One of the clearest ways to see what Conductor does is Netflix’s <a href="https://medium.com/netflix-techblog/globalizing-productions-with-netflixs-media-production-suite-fc3c108c0a22">Media Production Suite (MPS)</a>. It’s a set of applications that move production data into the cloud. An average Netflix title generates around 200 terabytes of original camera files, and MPS is what gets that media ingested, validated, transcoded, and delivered to the right people around the world.</p><p>The multi-step pipelines behind MPS’s workflows (Footage Ingest, Dailies, VFX Pulls, and Conform Pulls) run as Conductor workflows. A dedicated orchestration layer called Studio Orchestrator stitches together the platform services these pipelines depend on, such as asset management, media encoding, and storage, using Conductor under the hood to ensure reliable execution and the durability to recover from the inevitable failures of long-running, distributed work.</p><h4>Beyond the studio: Ads and Games</h4><p>Content engineering is where Conductor started, but it’s no longer the whole story. Two of Netflix’s newer businesses lean on it just as heavily.</p><p>Netflix Ads has made Conductor a backbone of its programmatic pipelines. Creative ingestion, advertiser-certificate management, and privacy-preserving Data Clean Rooms all run as Conductor workflows.</p><p>Netflix Games uses Conductor to orchestrate the path a game takes from a partner’s submission to the app stores. When a studio uploads a build, a Conductor pipeline ingests the binary, runs the required validations (for example, iOS privacy-manifest checks), and drives App Store publishing, coordinating steps that span multiple platform services and external stores with the same reliability and visibility the studio pipelines rely on.</p><h3>How the architecture evolved</h3><p>Our <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/evolution-of-netflix-conductor-16600be36bca">earlier post</a> described a Conductor built on a particular set of building blocks: DynoQueues for queueing, Dynomite for metadata, Cassandra (partially) for execution data, and Elasticsearch for indexing. That stack carried Conductor a long way, but as adoption climbed, almost every layer of it was eventually rethought. The data plane Conductor runs on today looks very little like the earlier one.</p><h4>1.0: the original stack, and where it strained</h4><p>In its <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/netflix-conductor-a-microservices-orchestrator-2e8d4771bf40">first generation</a>, Conductor leaned heavily on <strong>Dynomite</strong> (with <strong>DynoQueues</strong> as the queueing recipe layered on top) backed by <strong>Elasticsearch</strong> for indexing. It worked, but two classes of problems showed up as scale grew. The first was the datastore. Dynomite is an in-memory store: a clustering layer that adds sharding and replication on top of Redis. You scale it by adding memory and nodes, with each replica rack holding a full copy of the data, which suits a cache but not the durable record for millions of workflows a day. Dyno Queues, built on the same store, inherited those limits, and its zone-sharded design made task scheduling uneven. The second was the engine’s design: evaluation ran synchronously and mutated workflow state in several places, so competing updates could leave a workflow inconsistent with no automated way to repair it.</p><p>The 2.x line was a sustained campaign to address these, and two replacements did most of the heavy lifting.</p><h4>2.0: Cassandra as the backbone</h4><p>The most consequential change was making <strong>Cassandra</strong> the primary datastore for execution data, displacing Dynomite. Cassandra gave Conductor the elastic, horizontal scaling it needed, and removed the scaling ceiling and zone-affinity constraints that the old datastore imposed. Around the same time, large task inputs and outputs were offloaded to S3, keeping bulky data off the primary datastore and further easing the pressure on it.</p><h4>Timestone: a purpose-built queueing layer</h4><p>The second replacement was the queue itself. <a href="https://medium.com/netflix-techblog/timestone-netflixs-high-throughput-low-latency-priority-queueing-system-with-built-in-support-1abf249ba95f"><strong>Timestone</strong></a>, Netflix’s high-throughput, low-latency priority queueing system, took over from DynoQueues. Beyond shedding the Dynomite dependency, it brought two capabilities that mattered for reliability: a <strong>delayed-queuing mechanism</strong>, and a <strong>background repair process</strong> that could detect and recover workflows. Timestone also turned out to be foundational for work that came later. The async evaluation queue and the queue-depth-driven dynamic thread allocation described later in this post both build on it.</p><p>Alongside these, the 2.x series also <strong>decoupled indexing from the critical path</strong>, moving to a Kafka-buffered, independently scalable indexer writing to Elasticsearch.</p><h4>3.0: consolidating the modern data plane</h4><p>By <strong>Conductor 3.0</strong>, these threads came together into a coherent platform. Dynomite was fully sunset. Timestone was the queue and Cassandra the datastore. A Kafka-fed indexer pushed to Elasticsearch, with <strong>Iceberg</strong> added for long-term storage. The release also shipped <strong>automated workflow repair</strong> and <strong>multi-cluster deployment</strong>, the last of which lets workflows run in fully isolated contexts (for example, a dedicated context for the Media Production Suite, kept separate from the shared default and scaled on its own).</p><p>The net effect is that by 3.0 the foundational reliability and scaling problems of the early era were largely solved, and the data plane reached roughly the shape it has today.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/940/1*cPgTnfCY3ngVsK8hTPyZrw.png" /><figcaption>1.0 to 3.0 architecture evolution</figcaption></figure><h4>Rebuilding the engine: Conductor 4.0</h4><p>By 3.0, everything around the evaluation engine had been modernized, but the engine and its data model were largely the original, and at this scale that became the bottleneck. The engine evaluated a workflow by loading its entire state into memory to decide what to do next, which meant heavy memory pressure and slow evaluations as workflows grew toward tens of thousands of tasks. It also stored a workflow’s metadata and its task payloads in the same Cassandra partition, producing oversized “wide rows” and leaving room for race conditions when several updates to the same task competed. Evaluation overhead, vertical-scaling pressure, and occasional inconsistent state needed to be addressed.</p><h4>A partitioned task model</h4><p>The old data model co-located everything about a workflow in one place. The rewrite <strong>separates workflow metadata from task and user data</strong> and gives each task its own record. The engine no longer loads the whole workflow graph to make a decision. It evaluates against a lightweight <em>blueprint</em> of the workflow and loads only the specific task data a decision needs. The practical result is a dramatically smaller memory footprint per evaluation and far less work to advance a workflow by one step.</p><p>The 3.0 engine topped out around <strong>2,500 tasks per workflow</strong>. The rewritten engine scales to <strong>30,000 tasks per workflow</strong>, more than a 10x increase. Evaluation got faster too: in production, the rewrite delivered roughly a <strong>40% reduction in p99 workflow-evaluation latency</strong>.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/788/1*nxKmaDyWSLHYzPkx6entBg.png" /><figcaption>Metric graph showing a reduction in workflow-evaluation latency</figcaption></figure><h4>Making competing updates safe using partitions</h4><p>Separating the data is only half the story. The engine also has to make competing updates to the same task safe, because a task can be written from more than one place at once. Picture a long-running task whose worker sends periodic updates to keep it alive, each one marking the task in-progress, before finally reporting it COMPLETED. If a stray in-progress update arrives just after that completion, reordered by the network, it could quietly revert a COMPLETED task back to IN_PROGRESS.</p><p>The first version leaned on Cassandra lightweight transactions (LWT) to serialize these writes, but mixing LWT with ordinary writes is a known Cassandra anti-pattern. The two use different timestamping mechanisms, and combining them breaks the strict consistency Conductor needs.</p><p>So the data model evolved away from locking entirely. Task state is split across two partitions: a pending partition for tasks still in flight, and a terminal partition that a task is written to exactly once, when it reaches a terminal state (COMPLETED, FAILED, etc). When the engine evaluates a workflow, it reads both and reconciles them in the application layer with one simple rule: a terminal state always wins over a non-terminal one, and between two writes of the same kind (two in-progress, or two terminal) ordinary last-write-wins applies. So in the race above, when the task is marked COMPLETED it lands in the terminal partition, and the late in-progress update loses on read.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/860/1*YVq-xtTEvHS-WWfRN-bPBg.gif" /><figcaption>Race condition illustration</figcaption></figure><p>The net of the rewrite is reliable task lifecycle management, a lower memory footprint, faster evaluation, and far higher concurrent throughput.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*zoP0vyFrohYiy13CMCu8Sw.png" /><figcaption>Conductor 4.0 — Architecture</figcaption></figure><h4>Removing the last synchronous bottleneck: async workflow evaluation</h4><p>A faster engine still left one structural problem. Workflow evaluation ran <strong>synchronously</strong>. When an update arrived, a new workflow or a task completing, the request itself evaluated the workflow, and to do that safely it had to take a lock on that workflow. Under load, concurrent updates to the same workflow raced for that lock. Only one won, and the rest were deferred to a background repair process that, in extreme cases, could take <strong>several minutes</strong> to catch up. The result was exactly the kind of unpredictable tail latency that hurts most under peak load.</p><p>The fix was to <strong>decouple the trigger from the evaluation</strong>. Updates no longer evaluate inline. Instead they enqueue the workflow onto a Timestone <a href="https://medium.com/netflix-techblog/timestone-netflixs-high-throughput-low-latency-priority-queueing-system-with-built-in-support-1abf249ba95f">exclusive queue</a>, and a central asynchronous evaluator processes each workflow’s pending work in order. Because the queue is exclusive, only one worker ever evaluates a given workflow at a time, so the lock contention simply goes away.</p><p>The results are most dramatic at the tail (p99 and p99.9), which is the point, since the tail is what users feel under load:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/704/1*iS0WwZ04zyQPkJatUOE7Pg.png" /></figure><p>Unsuccessful lock-acquire attempts, which had spiked to around 2,700 per interval under contention, drop to essentially zero with async evaluation.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/782/1*sPJMEwmMs2cMkCG_TO5FJw.png" /><figcaption>Unsuccessful lock acquistion attempts reducing to zero</figcaption></figure><h3>Controlling how workflows execute</h3><h4>Run Strategies: native control over workflow concurrency</h4><p>For most of its life, Conductor would happily run as many instances of a workflow as you started, all at once. That’s the right default, but it left a gap. When several workflow instances act on the <em>same</em> entity, the same movie id, the same creative, the same script, there is no built-in way to serialize them or guarantee only one runs at a time. Teams that needed this had to build it themselves: custom locks, custom queues, custom coordination logic bolted onto their workflows. That’s extra complexity in every workflow that needs it, and a real source of operational risk.</p><p>Run Strategies are a way to declare, on a workflow definition, how concurrent executions should be handled. There are two strategies today. Parallel, the default, applies no restrictions and behaves exactly as workflows always have. Sequential runs at most one workflow at a time per sequence key, with subsequent requests enqueued and started automatically, in order, as each running workflow finishes. A run strategy is declared on the workflow definition, and can be set directly in the Workflow SDK as a @RunStrategy annotation argument on the @WorkflowMethod.</p><p>The key idea is the <strong>sequence key</strong>: two executions that share a sequence key run one after another, and executions with different keys still run in parallel. So a content-ingestion workflow keyed on movie id processes each title strictly serially while happily running many <em>different</em> titles concurrently, with no custom locking required.</p><h4>Beyond polled workers: managed, serverless tasks</h4><p>A task meant a <strong>polled worker</strong>: your application implements the worker, polls the Conductor server for work, runs it, and reports the result back. That model is simple and language-agnostic, and it’s still the backbone for most workloads. But it has a cost. You operate the worker fleet. You stand it up, keep it running, and scale it, even for work that’s bursty or runs rarely.</p><p>So Conductor added <strong>managed tasks</strong>: built-in task types where Conductor drives the compute directly, with no long-lived worker for you to host or scale. Two integrations carry most of this today: <strong>Titus</strong> for containers and <strong>Stratum</strong> for serverless media-processing functions.</p><p>A TITUS task launches a <a href="https://medium.com/netflix-techblog/titus-the-netflix-container-management-platform-is-now-open-source-f868c9fb5436">Titus</a> job to run a script, whether shell, Python, or whatever your image packages, using Titus, Netflix’s container management platform, as the compute plane. You point the task at a Docker image and an entry point, and specify the shape of the run, such as cpu and memory. The lifecycle is fully managed. The task starts IN_PROGRESS, Conductor monitors the Titus job, and it transitions on the container’s exit behavior. An exit code of 0 marks the task COMPLETED, and a non-zero code, a timeout, or a missing job marks it FAILED. The upshot is that you can spin up containerized compute as a step in a workflow without running a worker that sits around polling.</p><p>A STRATUM task invokes a Stratum function and collects its output. Stratum is the serverless function layer of Netflix’s <a href="https://medium.com/netflix-techblog/the-netflix-cosmos-platform-35c14d9351ad">Cosmos platform</a>, purpose-built for media-processing workloads, and a Conductor STRATUM task is the natural way to call one from a workflow. You give it a function name and an ordered list of arguments. The task stays IN_PROGRESS until the function finishes, then resolves to COMPLETED, FAILED, or TIMED_OUT. Because Stratum exposes functions to Conductor directly, teams can fold resource-intensive media operations into a workflow without owning the compute that runs them.</p><p>Polled workers, Titus jobs, and Stratum functions are three points on a spectrum, and a single workflow can mix all three: a long-lived worker for steady high-volume work, a Titus container for an occasional heavy batch step, and a Stratum function for media processing. Managed tasks shift the operational burden from the team to the platform.</p><h3>Scaling task polling: dynamic thread allocation</h3><p>The engine work in the previous section made Conductor <em>evaluate</em> workflows fast. But there’s a second half to throughput: how quickly workers pick up the tasks that evaluation schedules. That’s a polling problem, and at scale it has its own failure mode, one that showed up most sharply for Studio Orchestrator, the heavy Conductor user behind the Media Production Suite pipelines from earlier in this post.</p><p>Conductor clients poll for work using worker threads, and historically the number of threads was <strong>fixed per task type</strong>. That’s predictable, and it prevents any one task from starving the others, but it’s static. During <strong>burst traffic</strong>, when thousands of instances of a single task suddenly pile into a queue, a fixed thread count can’t ramp up to drain them. The symptom was distinctive: CPU on the instances stayed flat while queues backed up, and idle threads sat stranded on quiet queues unable to help the overloaded one.</p><h4>Allocating threads by real-time queue depth</h4><p>The fix is a dynamic allocation strategy that rebalances threads across task queues continuously, based on how much work each queue actually has, while still guaranteeing every queue a floor so nothing starves.</p><p>Each instance periodically polls Timestone for the current depth of every task queue it manages. It then splits its thread pool into two parts. A <strong>minimum allocation</strong> gives every queue a baseline number of threads, so no queue is ever fully starved. A <strong>floating pool</strong> holds the remaining threads, distributed across queues in proportion to each queue’s share of the total load. A queue with a deep backlog draws a large slice of the floating pool. An idle queue falls back toward its minimum, freeing threads for whoever needs them.</p><p>One guardrail keeps this from becoming its own problem: a <strong>multiplier ceiling</strong>. A queue’s thread count can grow by at most a configurable factor per cycle, so even a dramatic load spike ramps up in controlled steps rather than all at once, giving downstream and dependent services time to absorb the increase instead of being bombarded. Allocations are recomputed every polling interval, so the system tracks shifting load in near real time while always honoring the per-queue minimums.</p><p>Two things come out of this. First, because threads are allocated where they’re actually needed, each instance can safely run a much larger pool. Second, CPU utilization becomes an <em>accurate</em> signal of how busy an instance is, which means autoscaling on CPU makes the cluster scale out in response to real computational demand.</p><h3>Developer experience: Workflow SDK</h3><p>For most of Conductor’s life, authoring a workflow meant writing JSON. You declared each task, then wired one task’s output into the next with ${taskRef.output.field} string templates, and on the worker side you pulled inputs back out of a Map&lt;String, Object&gt; and cast them to the types you <em>expected</em>. It worked, but it pushed an entire class of errors to runtime, and made large workflows genuinely hard to read.</p><p>The Workflow SDK is our answer: a <strong>type-safe Java DSL</strong> for authoring Conductor workflows. You write Java code. The compiler and IDE catch mistakes before anything deploys, and a Gradle plugin translates your Java code into the JSON workflow definition the Conductor server understands.</p><p>Crucially, the SDK gives you the best of both worlds. One of Conductor’s defining strengths has always been <em>visibility</em>. Because a workflow is a declared graph rather than imperative code, during execution the exact control flow can be inspected, every branch, loop, and parallel fork, and light up live as an execution moves through it. Writing workflows in imperative code gives you none of that. A timeline is not a workflow diagram. The SDK keeps both halves. You write in idiomatic Java and at execution time you still get the full visual graph: the same task-by-task flow, inputs and outputs, and at-a-glance “where is this stuck and why” that Conductor has always offered.</p><h4>The authoring pains it removes</h4><p><strong>No type safety.</strong> In the JSON model, a task input like “movieId”: “${awesomeTask2.output.movieId}” is just a string template. The worker reads it back with inputData.get(“movieId”) and casts. If the upstream value is actually an Integer and you cast to String, you get a <em>ClassCastException</em> at runtime. Fix that, and a subtle typo bites next: the JSON declares an input variable movieId but the worker reads inputData.get(movieid), which returns null and you get a <em>NullPointerException</em> downstream. None of this is visible until the workflow runs.</p><pre>public class GetMovieWorker implements Worker {<br />  @Override<br />  public TaskResult execute(Task task) {<br />    Map&lt;String, Object&gt; inputData = task.getInputData();<br />    long movieId = (Integer) inputData.get(&quot;movieId&quot;); // cast, fingers crossed<br />    // more logic here<br />  }<br />}</pre><p><strong>Verbosity.</strong> Defining a couple of tasks is already wordy. A real workflow’s JSON balloons into hundreds of lines that are difficult to review or refactor. A complex branching workflow becomes a single large JSON blob that no one wants to touch.</p><pre>{<br /> &quot;name&quot;: &quot;wrap_movie_workflow&quot;,<br /> &quot;version&quot;: 1,<br /> &quot;semver&quot;: &quot;0.0.1&quot;, <br /> &quot;schemaVersion&quot;: 2,<br /> &quot;restartable&quot;: true,<br /> &quot;workflowStatusListenerEnabled&quot;: false,<br /> &quot;ownerEmail&quot;: &quot;team@netflix.com&quot;,<br /> &quot;timeoutPolicy&quot;: &quot;ALERT_ONLY&quot;,<br /> &quot;timeoutSeconds&quot;: 0,<br /> &quot;tasks&quot;: [<br />   {<br />     &quot;name&quot;: &quot;get_movie&quot;,<br />     &quot;taskReferenceName&quot;: &quot;movie&quot;,<br />     &quot;type&quot;: &quot;SIMPLE&quot;,<br />     &quot;inputParameters&quot;: {<br />       &quot;movieId&quot;: &quot;${workflow.input.movieId}&quot;<br />     }     <br />   },<br />   {     <br />     &quot;name&quot;: &quot;wrap_movie&quot;,<br />     &quot;taskReferenceName&quot;: &quot;wrapMovie&quot;,<br />     &quot;type&quot;: &quot;SIMPLE&quot;,<br />     &quot;inputParameters&quot;: {<br />       &quot;movie&quot;: &quot;${movie.output}&quot;<br />     }<br />   }<br /> ]<br />}</pre><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*wvkIUmmgN9JQHpUVfvPcrA.png" /></figure><h4>How the Java DSL works</h4><pre>@TaskMethod<br />public Movie getMovie(long movieId) { /* real worker logic */ }<br /><br />@TaskMethod<br />public WrappedMovie wrapMovie(Movie movie) { /* real worker logic */ }</pre><pre>@WorkflowStub(taskNames = {&quot;movieapp.getMovie&quot;, &quot;movieapp.wrapMovie&quot;})<br />public interface MovieWorkflows {<br />  @WorkflowGenerated(name = &quot;movieapp.getMovie&quot;)<br />  Movie getMovie(int movieId);<br /><br />  @WorkflowGenerated(name = &quot;movieapp.wrapMovie&quot;)<br />  WrappedMovie wrapMovie(Movie movie);<br /><br />  @WorkflowMethod(name = &quot;wrap_movie_workflow&quot;, ownerEmail = &quot;team@netflix.com&quot;)<br />  default WrappedMovie wrapMovieWorkflow(MovieInput input) {<br />    Movie movie = getMovie(input.getMovieId());  // long in, Movie out (checked)<br />    return wrapMovie(movie);                     // Movie in, WrappedMovie out (checked)<br />  }<br />}</pre><p>The DSL has two core building blocks. The first, @TaskMethod, is a method that <em>is</em> executed at runtime and can contain arbitrary code. Crucially, it takes and returns real types: a getMovie task can take a long movieId and return a Movie object directly, with no maps and no casts. The second, @WorkflowMethod, is a method that is <em>not</em> executed and cannot contain arbitrary code. It expresses the workflow as ordinary method calls (call getMovie(…), then pass the result to wrapMovie(…)), and a Gradle build plugin parses it into a Conductor JSON definition at build time. The server does not see the Java code. It gets the same JSON it always has.</p><p>Code generation is central to how this works. In the @WorkflowStub annotation you declare the task and workflow names you want to use, and at build time the Gradle plugin generates their input and output types along with typed stub methods that you call inside a @WorkflowMethod. Those generated stubs are what let the method body compile against real signatures with full type safety. Because the stubs are generated from names rather than shared code, teams can reuse each other’s tasks and workflows without sharing libraries.</p><h4>Real control flow, expressed in Java</h4><p>The examples above are linear, but most production workflows branch, loop, and fan out. The DSL expresses these as the Java constructs you’d reach for anyway, and the SDK parses them into the corresponding Conductor operators. A useful mental model: you’re <em>describing</em> a workflow graph in Java, not running Java, so the parser only supports the constructs that map cleanly to Conductor (if/else, switch, do-while, parallel forks, sub-workflow calls) and asks you to express a few things its way. Iteration, for instance, is always a do-while, since there are no for or while tasks in Conductor.</p><p>A common shape is to kick off work, poll until it settles, then branch on the outcome, which is a do-while loop wrapped around an if/else:</p><pre>@WorkflowMethod(name = &quot;process_asset_workflow&quot;, ownerEmail = &quot;team@netflix.com&quot;)<br />default void processAssetWorkflow(AssetInput input) {<br />  JobStatus status = submitJob(input);<br /><br />  do {                                        // loops are do-while<br />    status = checkStatus(input.getJobId());<br />  } while (status.getState() == &quot;PENDING&quot;);<br /><br />  if (status.getState() == &quot;SUCCEEDED&quot;) {     // branch on the result<br />    publish(input);<br />  } else {<br />    flagForReview(input);<br />  }<br />}</pre><p>Independent work runs concurrently with ParallelExecution.fork(…) plus allOf(…) (a FORK and JOIN), and any other @WorkflowMethod can be called as a sub-workflow just by listing it in workflowNames:</p><pre>@WorkflowStub(<br />    taskNames = {&quot;movieapp.getMovie&quot;, &quot;movieapp.enrichMovie&quot;, &quot;movieapp.getArtwork&quot;,<br />                 &quot;movieapp.refreshCache&quot;, &quot;movieapp.publishPage&quot;},<br />    workflowNames = {&quot;movieapp.localize_page_workflow&quot;})<br />public interface MoviePageWorkflows {<br />  // stubs not shown for brevity<br />  @WorkflowMethod(name = &quot;prepare_movie_page&quot;, ownerEmail = &quot;team@netflix.com&quot;)<br />  default void prepareMoviePage(MoviePageInput input) {<br />    // three independent branches, run in parallel<br />    Fork&lt;WrappedMovie&gt; details = ParallelExecution.fork(() -&gt; {<br />      Movie movie = getMovie(input.getMovieId());<br />      return enrichMovie(movie);<br />    });<br />    Fork&lt;Artwork&gt; art = ParallelExecution.fork(() -&gt; getArtwork(input.getMovieId()));<br />    Fork&lt;Void&gt; cache = ParallelExecution.fork(this::refreshCache);<br /><br />    ParallelExecution.allOf(details, art, cache);   // FORK_JOIN and JOIN<br /><br />    WrappedMovie enriched = details.get();<br />    Artwork artwork = art.get();<br />    LocalizedPage localized = localizePageWorkflow(input);  // sub-workflow call<br /><br />    publishPage(enriched, artwork, localized);<br />  }<br />}</pre><h4>Sharing tasks and workflows across applications</h4><p>Teams shouldn’t re-implement common tasks. Listing another team’s task or workflow in your @WorkflowStub is enough to call it like a local method, or invoke it as a sub-workflow, and the shared POJO types (a Movie, say) come along directly, with no copy-pasting between codebases.</p><h4>Feature parity and rapid adoption</h4><p>The SDK is gaining rapid adoption and has become the primary choice for authoring new workflows at Netflix. We continue to invest in the developer experience, with intelligent IDE plugins, better debugging, and an integrated testing framework so that testing a workflow in code feels like testing any other code.</p><h3>In production: Netflix Ads</h3><p>Netflix Ads runs complex, multi-step processes on Conductor with the Workflow SDK, and three examples show the range. Creative ingestion is a heavily branching pipeline where the SDK turned a sprawling JSON blob into type-safe, testable code, and a sequential run strategy eliminated a duplicate-processing race by serializing work per creative. Advertiser certificate management automates a previously manual, hard-to-scale process across several system integrations, with downstream automation when certificates expire. And Data Clean Rooms are privacy-preserving environments where Netflix and a partner run joint analysis on shared data, built on the SDK from day one. Provisioning a clean room is a multi-day sequence (create at a vendor, wait for the partner to join, activate, and eventually archive) that previously relied on hand-rolled async polling. Re-authored as Conductor workflows, each phase became a durable, observable state machine with automatic retries, state that survives restarts, and long partner waits that <em>yield</em> rather than hold a thread.</p><p>The common thread is that the SDK made multi-process, multi-integration workflows easy to author and maintain, and let teams lean on Conductor’s reliability and visibility instead of hand-rolling retries and distributed locking.</p><h3>What’s next</h3><p>Like the last time we wrote about Conductor, the road ahead is less a fixed roadmap than a wish-list, a sense of where we’re headed, shaped by what teams keep asking for.</p><p>On <strong>scale</strong>, the demands keep growing. As Netflix expands into new content types, like live, games, and podcasts, we anticipate our workflow load could grow 5x in the coming year, and the engine work described above is what gives us confidence we can absorb it without re-architecting again.</p><p>On <strong>developer experience</strong>, the Workflow SDK now covers all task types, so the focus shifts to the experience <em>around</em> writing workflows: a richer integrated testing story so a workflow can be validated against real worker behavior as easily as any other code, deeper agent tooling such as skills, plugins, and MCPs, and safe schema evolution: a schema registry for shared tasks and workflows, with guardrails that block non-backward-compatible changes so they can evolve without breaking the teams that depend on it.</p><p>On <strong>execution control</strong>, sequential is only the first run strategy. A family of richer policies is on the roadmap, including running <em>N</em> at a time per key, idempotent de-duplication, keep-first and keep-latest, and combinations of these, to cover the coordination patterns teams currently hand-build. And managed tasks will keep expanding the ways Conductor can run compute directly, beyond today’s container and serverless function integrations.</p><p>None of this is exhaustive, and priorities shift with the workloads we take on. But the direction is consistent: keep the overhead low, keep authoring safe and simple, and keep widening the range of work a single workflow can reliably coordinate.</p><h3>Acknowledgments</h3><p>Netflix Conductor is built and operated by the Enterprise Workflow Platform team, with contributions from many partner teams across the company.</p><p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/andrew-seier/">Andrew Seier</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/anoop-panicker/">Anoop Panicker</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/christiandjohnson11/">Christian Johnson</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/christinehyu/">Christine Yu</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jasmine-lee10/">Jasmine Lee</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/jiaofen-x-39044552/">Jiaofen Xu</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/michael-torku/">Michael Torku</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/peterslau/">Peter Lau</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/prashant-rastogi-8b4977147/">Prashant Rastogi</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/ryan-kastilani/">Ryan Kastilani</a> <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/surafel/">Surafel Korse</a> and <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/victor-lee-b9a02a163/">Victor Lee</a> have contributed to Conductor. We thank <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/czhao/">Charles Zhao</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sumukhshivaprakash/">Sumukh Shivaprakash</a> and the teams in the Content and Business Products and Ads organizations for their guidance and constructive feedback while developing and operating Conductor.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=41ad21067649" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/netflix-conductor-the-next-chapter-41ad21067649">Netflix Conductor: The Next Chapter</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [Running Apache Spark experiments in my sleep (and on a plane)](https://netflixtechblog.com/running-apache-spark-experiments-in-my-sleep-and-on-a-plane-588816b6b5f8?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p><em>By </em><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/prashanthgedde/"><em>Prashanth Gedde Narayanaswamy</em></a></p><p>As one of Netflix’s largest and critical data pipelines, <strong>member-sessionizer</strong> consolidates tens of billions of hourly client events, encompassing user taps, playbacks, and engagement signals, into individual viewing sessions per profile.</p><p>A quick two-week tuning effort spiraled into two months when standard approaches hit a wall. Because the failures only happened at full production scale, each test took hours, limiting me to just three or four attempts daily. The turning point was changing the goal: instead of trying to speed up a single run, I built a way to run and monitor multiple tests at once and track every result.</p><p>That mindset led to an unconventional setup: kicking off five Spark jobs at once and monitoring them over spotty inflight Wi-Fi during a day-long trip home, while cloud servers handled the actual heavy lifting. This post covers how I set up that loop, along with the Spark bottlenecks and memory quirks I uncovered along the way.</p><h3>The Scale Dilemma: Small Samples Don’t Catch Scale Bugs</h3><p>Standard engineering wisdom says that you should isolate bugs on small datasets to iterate quickly. That works for standard software bugs, but it fails completely for big data performance bottlenecks.</p><p>While upgrading our sessionization pipeline from a legacy logging architecture to a modern platform, the new job hit critical failures, including executor Out-Of-Memory (OOM) errors, severe shuffle spillage, and thousands of stage retries. None of these issues could be reproduced using sampled data. The failures only triggered at full production scale because of data skew: an idle Netflix tab left streaming overnight generates tens of thousands of telemetry events, while a typical session only produces a handful. A standard 1% sample misses these extreme long-tail sessions entirely.</p><p>To catch the real bugs, every test run had to execute against a full hour of unsampled production data, along with all the deployment overhead that came with it.</p><h3>The Strategy: Branch-Driven Isolation</h3><p>The biggest win came from a feature in <a href="https://medium.com/@netflixtechblog/etl-development-life-cycle-with-dataflow-9c70c64aba7b">Dataflow</a>, Netflix’s command-line tool for building, testing, and deploying data pipelines. It supports <em>branch-driven development</em>: our workflows run on <a href="https://github.com/Netflix/maestro"><em>Maestro</em></a><em> (</em>Netflix’s workflow orchestrator), which schedules and executes the jobs, and they already use a sanitized branch name (the <em>${dataflow.sanitizedbranch}</em> placeholder). So if I push a branch like <em>pgn/cap-experiment</em>, then the deployed workflow, its tables, and its run namespace all get tagged with that branch name. This eliminates collisions with team members or between my own concurrent runs.</p><p>With that isolation in place, the model was simple: <strong>one branch is one independent experiment</strong>. I parameterized the scheduler (cap size, shuffle partitions, heavy-device threshold, feature flags) and gave each variant its own branch and output table. Instead of queuing five sequential tests over 10 hours, I fired off five variants simultaneously against the same time window. Total execution time dropped from the sum of all runs to just the length of the single slowest job.</p><p>Managing per-branch tables added zero overhead. Dataflow provisions and updates tables automatically from declared schemas without manual DDL. Whenever I modified the event bag structure between experiments, the table schema updated seamlessly, no CREATE TABLE or ALTER TABLE required.</p><p>The only real constraint was cluster contention. Because these heavy Spark jobs shared resources with the rest of the company, peak hours sometimes limited me to two concurrent runs instead of five. But even running two or three jobs in parallel was a massive improvement over single-threaded debugging.</p><p>The key takeaway: <strong>don’t focus on making a single multi-hour run faster, build a setup where you can run many experiments simultaneously without them stepping on each other.</strong></p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1020/1*XXIKbAHpGvPtFzHcDL8Kcg.png" /></figure><p>While the previous chart shows the time savings, the architecture that enables this isolation looks like below:</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1020/1*HIG25d1I6CwaimsRd_zYLw.png" /></figure><h3>How an AI Agent Helped</h3><p>Running five parallel experiments gets messy fast. Tracking instance IDs, configs, memory spills, and stage failures across variants makes it easy to lose track.</p><p>This is where an AI agent stepped in, not to write clever code, but to handle operational grunt work. I described variants in plain English, and the agent updated scheduler configs, deployed to the branch, triggered jobs, and polled metrics. Once complete, it compared spill and failure counts across variants in a summary table, allowing me to ask, <em>“Which run is closest to OOM-free at peak load?”</em> and get an immediate answer.</p><p>The value wasn’t deep Spark expertise, but operational memory. It turned tedious Spark UI cross-checks into a single prompt.</p><p>Eventually, I packaged this workflow into a reusable agent skill (.agents/skills/sandbox-deploy-and-run/) run via /sandbox-deploy-and-run &lt;module&gt; &lt;scheduler-file&gt; [PARAM=value]. It handles the full lifecycle—preflight, deploy, background polling, auto-diagnostics, and re-entrant resume &lt;instance_id&gt; support—turning multi-hour experiments into a single command.</p><h3>Why tmux Mattered</h3><p>All of this ran on Workbench, Netflix’s cloud dev environment, using my laptop as a thin terminal. Without session persistence, an SSH drop or sleeping laptop would kill multi-hour Spark runs mid-flight.</p><p>tmux running directly on Workbench solved this. Every experiment lived in a named cloud session: I could close my laptop, reattach hours later, and pick up where I left off. Combined with branch isolation, this made &quot;start five and walk away&quot; possible.</p><p>This is now my default setup: a local terminal (<a href="https://ghostty.org/">Ghostty</a>), dedicated <a href="https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree">git worktrees</a> per branch, and persistent tmux sessions on Workbench. The local laptop is disposable—closing it or losing network never interrupts the jobs.</p><p>That setup turned a long travel day into active iteration time. I kicked off batches at the gate, checked results via AI agent over spotty inflight Wi-Fi, and squeezed 4–5 full experiment cycles into a single 24-hour flight.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1020/1*CyOA_m84BlHHPoztLbZsfQ.png" /></figure><h3>What All Experiments Uncovered</h3><p>The root cause came down to a single operator: collect_list. Because Spark buffers each full event bag in memory, long-session OOMs were driven by individual bag size, not total dataset volume. The legacy pipeline handled identical row counts without crashing, proving that each event in the new bag had grown significantly heavier.</p><p>Two factors inflated event weight:</p><ul><li><strong>Field types and Option boxing:</strong> The session ID changed from an 8-byte Long to a UUID string. Worse, wrapping fields in Scala Option forced Spark to deserialize typed Datasets into JVM objects (Some wrappers), driving up object allocation and GC pressure across tens of thousands of events per session.</li><li><strong>Bloated string attributes:</strong> Low-value fields (e.g., referrer URLs, test allocations) added several hundred bytes per event. Multiplying these strings across collect_list wasted terabytes per hour, even though downstream tasks rarely used them.</li></ul><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1020/1*j3V9l8U5DubLYGJl_o8B4A.png" /></figure><p><strong>The Fixes:</strong></p><ol><li><strong>Unboxed primitives:</strong> Removing Option wrappers from non-nullable fields dropped peak executor memory from <strong>112% to 58% of the slot</strong> with zero disk spill.</li><li><strong>Slimmed event bags:</strong> Dropped unused per-event attributes and hoisted session-invariant metadata (app version, country) up to the session header.</li></ol><p><strong>Takeaway:</strong> Reducing individual event weight delivered far better stability than throwing more cluster memory at the problem.</p><h3>Two Experiments That Changed My Mind</h3><p>Out of dozens of runs, two findings completely overturned my assumptions about Spark memory management:</p><ul><li><strong>Finding 1: Narrowing rows can trigger faster OOMs.</strong> Slimming our table dropped its size below Spark’s auto-broadcast threshold, prompting the query planner to switch from shuffle joins to broadcast joins. Because broadcast joins pin build-side tables directly into executor memory, narrower rows actually <em>increased</em> total memory pressure.</li><li><strong>Finding 2: Broadcast hash joins were silently draining memory.</strong> Adaptive Query Execution (AQE) rewrote nine sort-merge joins into broadcast hash joins at runtime. While each join stayed under our 400 MB broadcast limit individually, AQE pinned <strong>~3.1 GB of cumulative data simultaneously in a single executor</strong>, pushing containers over the edge.</li></ul><p><strong>The Surgical Fix:</strong> Instead of disabling broadcast joins globally, I lowered only AQE’s runtime threshold (spark.sql.adaptive.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold) to 10 MB while leaving the compile-time threshold untouched. That single change freed up ~3.1 GB of executor memory without impacting initial query planning.</p><p><strong>Takeaway:</strong> Broadcast joins speed up queries, but pinned memory isn’t free. Watch accumulated broadcasts in your execution plan, not just dataset sizes.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1020/1*Azu3gOVpaAjix4VjT0NG8A.png" /></figure><h3>Keeping a Single Experiment Log</h3><p>When running dozens of parallel tests, the biggest risk isn’t job failure, it’s losing track of what you’ve already tried or confusing which JAR and config produced a result.</p><p>To solve this, I scaffolded the AI agent to append a row to a repository Markdown table after every run:</p><ul><li><strong>Run #</strong> | <strong>Jar ver</strong> | <strong>Change vs. Previous Run</strong> | <strong>Config</strong> | <strong>Key Metrics</strong> <em>(Spill, Failed Tasks, Peak Memory, Duration)</em></li></ul><p>If a run yielded a key finding, the agent added a short note explaining what the result proved or disproved.</p><p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p><ul><li><strong>Single source of truth:</strong> It created an auditable history for the entire investigation.</li><li><strong>Forced discipline:</strong> Requiring <em>“Change vs. Previous Run”</em> forced me to isolate variables. Whenever I bundled multiple changes, I couldn’t isolate the root cause and had to rerun the test — a mistake the log format immediately exposed.</li></ul><p>A secondary doc (events-join-spark-tuning.md) captured the distilled findings as a quick &quot;symptom-to-knob&quot; lookup, so teammates didn't have to wade through the full chronological history.</p><h3>A Mistake I Learned From: <strong>The Fix That Only Worked Off-Peak.</strong></h3><p>To cap session size, I added row_number().over(Window...) and filtered for the first N events. It passed off-peak tests cleanly, but crashed with OOMs at peak load.</p><p>The issue was structural: Spark’s Window operator buffers an entire session in memory before evaluating row numbers and filtering. The cap trimmed output downstream, but it didn't limit memory usage during buffering, so a single skewed session overwhelmed executor memory.</p><p><strong>The Fix &amp; Lesson:</strong> We moved truncation ahead of the Window stage to cap events before buffering occurs.</p><p><strong>Takeaway:</strong> Test fixes against worst-case input skew. Off-peak runs hide structural bottlenecks that peak scale will immediately trigger.</p><h3>The final fix</h3><p>Pulling it together, four changes shipped, each isolated to a single experiment before it landed:</p><ul><li><strong>Unboxed the never-null fields</strong> from Option to plain primitives. Peak executor memory dropped from about 112% of the slot to about 58%, with zero spill.</li><li><strong>Slimmed the event bag.</strong> Dropped per-event fields nobody used, and hoisted session-invariant fields (versions, country, connection type) to the session level so they are stored once per session, not once per event.</li><li><strong>Capped session size before the Window stage.</strong> Dropped oversized sessions ahead of the buffering, and truncated the bag to the cap rather than dropping whole sessions.</li><li><strong>Lowered AQE’s runtime broadcast threshold.</strong> Set spark.sql.adaptive.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold to 10 MB, leaving the compile-time threshold untouched.</li></ul><h3>The Unresolved Bottleneck</h3><p>While these fixes made the pipeline run cleanly, they didn’t solve the underlying scaling issue: aggregating an entire session’s event bag into a single row has a hard ceiling. As our upstream telemetry source evolves and adds new attributes, each event in the bag becomes heavier, meaning this memory pressure will eventually return without a single line of code changing on our end.</p><p>Slimming and unboxing bought us headroom, but they didn’t change the fundamental data shape. The biggest takeaway from this effort is that <strong>tuning buys time, not infinite scale.</strong> If your underlying data model allows unbounded row growth, tuning alone won’t save you , eventually, the architecture itself has to change.</p><h3>Summary</h3><ul><li>If the bug only appears at scale, stop trying to sample. Build a way to run many full-scale runs in parallel instead.</li><li>If your scheduler can use the branch name, then one branch becomes one isolated experiment, and running many at once becomes easy.</li><li>Use an AI agent to run the operation, not only to write code. Deploy, poll, pull metrics, compare variants: this is where most of the time goes.</li><li>Once a loop repeats, package it as a skill. The deploy-run-watch-diagnose cycle becomes one command.</li><li>Use tmux for long runs. The run must survive you closing the laptop.</li><li>Keep one single experiment log, and make every entry state the one change from the previous run. The format itself forces you to change one variable at a time.</li><li>For memory problems, look at the per-row width first, not only at the total data volume. Bigger field types, Option boxing, and fat unused strings all add up inside a collect_list bag.</li><li>Tuning buys headroom, not a new ceiling. If the data shape can grow without a bound, the design has to change eventually, tuning alone will not save you.</li></ul><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=588816b6b5f8" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/running-apache-spark-experiments-in-my-sleep-and-on-a-plane-588816b6b5f8">Running Apache Spark experiments in my sleep (and on a plane)</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>
